## Decisions and Dispositions

### Arizona v. California

Submitted October 10, 2000 | Decided October 10, 2000 `OT_2000CHUNK1.md` `JUDICIAL_TURN_OUTPUT_RENDER_CONTRACT.md` `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md` `STANDING_STATE.md` `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`  
On submission of the proposed supplemental decree in this original action. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/1/))  
Area of Law: Interstate water allocation; Original jurisdiction; Decree administration

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Court should enter the proposed supplemental decree implementing previously adjudicated Colorado River rights and settled tribal-boundary matters.

#### Judgment

Supplemental decree entered, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam | Per curiam | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports entry of the supplemental decree | Complete disposition and decree |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to enter the supplemental decree.  
Public role: joins the per curiam disposition and decree in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s disposition and decree.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Entry of the supplemental Colorado River decree

Holding and operative rule: The Court may enter a supplemental decree that faithfully implements prior adjudications and approved settlements while leaving unmodified rights and unresolved matters outside the submitted text intact.  
Reasoning: The June 19, 2000 decision approved the Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation settlements and directed their incorporation through a supplemental-decree procedure. The Special Master submitted the decree contemplated by that judgment, and no objection was filed. The proposed provisions carry the approved accords into the operative decree, preserve the previously adjudicated priority structure, and expressly leave the earlier decrees in force except where amended. Entry therefore administers the existing original-jurisdiction judgment rather than reopening the interstate apportionment. Continuing jurisdiction remains available for decree administration and for the separate Fort Yuma proceedings, but it supplies no authority to alter allocations or decide claims outside the decree now entered.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Arizona v. California*, 530 U.S. 392 (2000): applied; the approved Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation settlements are incorporated into the governing decree without reopening the unresolved Fort Yuma component.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The supplemental decree is entered, the prior decrees remain in force except as expressly amended, and jurisdiction is retained for further administration.  
Effect: The Colorado River and Fort Mojave Reservation entitlements and related boundary provisions stated in the supplemental decree bind the parties. The unresolved Fort Yuma boundary, title, and water-entitlement proceedings remain before the Special Master and are not adjudicated by this decree.

### Artuz v. Bennett

Argued October 10, 2000 | Decided November 7, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: state collateral application treated as properly filed for AEDPA tolling, 199 F.3d 116 (2d Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/4/))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas corpus; AEDPA tolling

Questions Presented:
1. Is a state collateral application “properly filed” under 28 U.S.C. §2244(d)(2) when particular claims may be rejected under state procedural bars?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Properly filed state collateral applications

Holding and operative rule: A state collateral application is properly filed when its delivery and acceptance comply with the State’s rules governing filing, even if particular claims are subject to procedural or merits defenses that may prevent relief.  
Reasoning: Section 2244(d)(2) distinguishes an application from the claims contained within it. Filing rules govern whether the application is delivered to and accepted by the proper court or officer in the required form, within any applicable filing period, and with any required fee. Rules that instead determine whether a claim is procedurally barred govern entitlement to relief after filing. Treating every claim-level defense as a defect in filing would collapse those separate inquiries and make tolling depend on a federal court’s anticipatory resolution of state defenses. Bennett’s application was accepted under New York’s filing rules. The possibility that particular claims would later be denied therefore did not prevent the application from tolling the federal limitations period.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

28 U.S.C. § 2244(d)(2): clarified; proper filing concerns compliance with conditions governing delivery and acceptance of the application, not claim-level conditions governing entitlement to relief.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Bennett’s accepted state collateral application qualifies for statutory tolling notwithstanding procedural bars asserted against particular claims. Untimeliness, filing in the wrong court, nonpayment of a required fee, defective form, the duration of pending status, and other questions not decided here remain governed by ordinary law.

### Cleveland v. United States

Argued October 10, 2000 | Decided November 7, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: mail-fraud convictions and related judgments affirmed, 182 F.3d 296 (5th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/12/))  
Area of Law: Federal criminal law; Mail fraud

Questions Presented:
1. Are Louisiana’s unissued video-poker licenses property in the State’s hands for purposes of 18 U.S.C. § 1341?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: The mail-fraud property element

Holding and operative rule: A State’s unissued regulatory license is not property in the State’s hands under 18 U.S.C. § 1341 merely because the State controls issuance and expects application fees, taxes, or other revenue from licensed activity.  
Reasoning: The mail-fraud statute protects money and property, not the government’s abstract interest in accurate regulatory decisionmaking. Before issuance, Louisiana held authority to permit, deny, condition, and supervise video-poker operations. That sovereign regulatory power was not a proprietary asset that Cleveland’s alleged scheme obtained from the State. Application fees and expected future revenue are money, but they do not transform the underlying licensing decision into property, and the prosecution did not identify money already owed to Louisiana as the object of the charged scheme. Because the Fifth Circuit sustained the mail-fraud convictions on the unissued-license theory, that judgment cannot stand. Any independently valid counts or theories remain for disposition on remand without enlarging § 1341’s property element.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*McNally v. United States*: followed; § 1341 protects money and property rather than an undifferentiated governmental interest in regulatory integrity.  
*Carpenter v. United States*: distinguished; confidential business information already possessed as an economic asset differs from an unissued governmental license whose function is regulatory.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to set aside the mail-fraud judgment insofar as it rests on treating unissued licenses as state property.  
Effect: Louisiana’s unissued video-poker licenses cannot supply the property object required by the charged mail-fraud theory. The lower courts may address independently valid counts or theories, but this decision does not determine the treatment of an issued or transferable license, money already owed, honest-services fraud, or another existing proprietary asset.

### City of Indianapolis v. Edmond

Argued October 3, 2000 | Decided November 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: denial of preliminary relief reversed and matter remanded, 183 F.3d 659 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/32/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Suspicionless checkpoints

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Fourth Amendment permits suspicionless vehicle checkpoints whose primary programmatic purpose is detecting illegal narcotics, and what disposition follows from the Seventh Circuit's reversal of the denial of relief.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse | Questions the prior checkpoint precedents while adhering to them absent briefing on overruling |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Narcotics-interdiction checkpoints

Holding and operative rule: A suspicionless checkpoint whose primary programmatic purpose is general narcotics detection violates the Fourth Amendment because ordinary criminal investigation is not a special need that dispenses with individualized suspicion.  
Reasoning: A vehicle stop is a seizure, and individualized suspicion remains the ordinary constitutional requirement. The limited checkpoint exceptions recognized for the border, roadway safety, and comparable immediate public dangers rest on purposes and operational needs distinct from a general effort to discover evidence of crime. Indianapolis designed this program principally to uncover illegal narcotics. Its brief duration, standardized operation, and use of an exterior dog sniff may bear on intrusiveness, but they do not supply the threshold justification for seizing every motorist. The dog sniff also cannot cure the antecedent seizure because the stop must be lawful before officers employ investigative techniques during it. The Seventh Circuit’s constitutional judgment is therefore affirmed, while final equitable relief remains for ordinary lower-court proceedings.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The checkpoint should be sustained under the neutral roadblock balancing used in *Sitz* and *Martinez-Fuerte* because the stops were brief, standardized, and minimally intrusive, and the dog sniff added no constitutionally cognizable search. He would reverse rather than make programmatic purpose controlling.

Thomas: Although he doubts that suspicionless roadblock precedents are consistent with the original Fourth Amendment, he would not reconsider them without briefing and argument. Applying those precedents, he would uphold the Indianapolis program.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chandler v. Miller*: extended; a suspicionless program directed principally to ordinary crime detection fails at the threshold because general law enforcement is not a concrete special need.  
*Michigan Department of State Police v. Sitz*: distinguished; a sobriety checkpoint addresses an immediate roadway-safety danger rather than general evidence gathering.  
*United States v. Martinez-Fuerte*: distinguished; the border context and its distinct sovereign and operational concerns do not authorize ordinary narcotics checkpoints throughout the interior.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for ordinary equitable proceedings consistent with this opinion.  
Effect: Indianapolis may not operate the challenged narcotics-interdiction checkpoint program without individualized suspicion. The Supreme Court’s judgment does not itself prescribe the final injunction and does not invalidate checkpoints directed to border control, roadway safety, an immediate public danger, or another recognized purpose beyond general crime detection.

### Eastern Associated Coal Corp. v. United Mine Workers of America

Argued October 2, 2000 | Decided November 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: labor-arbitration award enforced, 188 F.3d 501 (4th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/57/))  
Area of Law: Labor arbitration; Public-policy review

Questions Presented:
1. Is an award reinstating a truck driver who twice tested positive for marijuana unenforceable as contrary to an explicit, well-defined, dominant public policy?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | Scalia, Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Would confine the public-policy exception to awards that violate or require violation of positive law |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors a concurrence in the judgment, joined by Scalia and Thomas.  
Announced ground: the public-policy exception permits nonenforcement only when an award violates or requires violation of positive law; no statute or regulation requires permanent discharge or forbids the conditional reinstatement ordered here.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Public-policy review of conditional reinstatement

Holding and operative rule: A labor-arbitration award may be refused enforcement only when it conflicts with an explicit, well-defined, dominant public policy ascertained from laws and legal precedents. (joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer) The transportation-safety sources identified here do not require permanent discharge and therefore do not invalidate the conditional reinstatement award.  
Reasoning: The parties authorized the arbitrator to determine whether just cause supported discharge, and the employer does not contend that the arbitrator exceeded that contractual authority. Judicial public-policy review therefore addresses the legal obligation created by the award rather than whether the employee’s past drug use was unlawful or whether a court would prefer discharge. Federal transportation law requires testing, treatment, temporary removal from safety-sensitive service, and compliance safeguards. It does not require permanent discharge after a second positive test. The award imposed treatment, repeated testing, and disciplinary conditions directed to the identified safety risk. No explicit, well-defined, dominant policy grounded in the governing legal sources forbids that result, so the narrow public-policy exception does not permit a court to displace the bargained-for award.

#### Concurrences

Stone, joined by Scalia and Thomas: They agree that no positive law forbids the conditional reinstatement and therefore concur in the judgment. They would confine the public-policy exception to awards that violate or require violation of positive law rather than recognize a broader federal common-law power.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*W.R. Grace & Co. v. Rubber Workers*: followed; a public policy sufficient to defeat an award must be explicit, well defined, dominant, and grounded in law rather than general views of the public interest.  
*United Paperworkers International Union v. Misco*: reaffirmed; review addresses the legal obligation created by the award and an explicit policy grounded in law, not merely the employee’s underlying conduct or a court’s preferred discipline.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit enforcing the arbitration award is affirmed.  
Effect: Eastern Associated Coal must comply with the arbitrator’s conditional reinstatement award. An award requiring unlawful operation, defeating mandatory testing, or retaining an employee whom governing law expressly orders removed would present a different case.

### Bush v. Palm Beach County Canvassing Board

Argued December 1, 2000 | Decided December 4, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Florida; judgment below: presidential-election certification judgment entered under revised state-law deadlines and canvassing requirements, 772 So. 2d 1220 (Fla. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/70/))  
Area of Law: Presidential elections; Article II; Federal safe-harbor law

Questions Presented:
1. Did the Florida Supreme Court impermissibly alter the legislature’s Article II framework or misunderstand 3 U.S.C. §5 when construing Florida’s presidential-election statutes?

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam | Per curiam | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the per curiam opinion in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Federal review of an ambiguous state-law judgment

Holding and operative rule: When preserved federal objections to a state high court’s presidential-election judgment depend on materially unclear state-law, state-constitutional, or federal premises, the judgment should be vacated and remanded for authoritative clarification rather than reviewed on assumptions about state law.  
Reasoning: The Florida Supreme Court extended the certification timetable and required acceptance of amended returns, but its opinion did not make clear whether ordinary statutory construction, the Florida Constitution, or an asserted understanding of federal law supplied the decisive premise. That uncertainty matters because the Article II objection depends on what state law the authoritative state court understood itself to be applying, and the § 5 objection depends on the role the court assigned the federal safe harbor. Federal review cannot responsibly decide either issue by selecting among unstated state-law rationales. The narrow and adequate course is to vacate the judgment and obtain prompt clarification from the Florida Supreme Court. The Court therefore does not decide Article II, the operation of § 5 beyond the issue presented, Florida’s controlling deadline, or the identity of the lawful electoral slate.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Minnesota v. National Tea Co.*: followed; a state judgment is vacated for clarification when uncertainty concerning its state-law basis prevents intelligent resolution of a preserved federal question.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Florida is vacated, and the case is remanded for prompt clarification of the state-law, state-constitutional, and federal premises supporting its construction.  
Effect: The Florida Supreme Court must identify the grounds on which its certification judgment rests before federal Article II and § 5 objections can be adjudicated. This disposition selects no state deadline, electoral slate, or election winner and adopts no broader independent-state-legislature theory.

### Green Tree Financial Corp.-Alabama v. Randolph

Argued October 3, 2000 | Decided December 11, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: order compelling arbitration treated as final and arbitration agreement held unenforceable on the cost record, 178 F.3d 1149 (11th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/79/))  
Area of Law: Arbitration; Appellate finality; Effective vindication

Questions Presented:
1. Whether an order compelling arbitration and dismissing the action is final under FAA § 16.
2. Whether contractual silence about costs alone makes the agreement unenforceable.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens as to appellate finality, contractual silence, and remand; O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy in full; Souter as to appellate finality, contractual silence, and remand; Thomas in full; Ginsburg and Breyer as to appellate finality, contractual silence, and remand | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion for five Justices; appellate-finality, contractual-silence, and remedial propositions for all nine |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Agrees that silence cannot support categorical invalidity and that the cost issue requires record development, but rejects placing all informational uncertainty on the consumer |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm appellate finality, reverse the categorical cost ruling, and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: dismissal of every claim produced a final decision under FAA § 16; contractual silence alone establishes neither accessibility nor foreclosure, and a prohibitive-cost objection requires concrete claimant-specific evidence before arbitration is enforced or denied on that ground.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Appellate finality after dismissal

Holding and operative rule: An order compelling arbitration and dismissing every claim in the action is a final decision with respect to arbitration under FAA § 16(a)(3).  
Reasoning: Finality turns on what the district court’s order does. An order that compels arbitration but stays a pending action ordinarily leaves further judicial proceedings and is not final merely because arbitration must occur. Here, however, the District Court dismissed the entire action and left no claim pending for later adjudication. The order therefore ended the federal case and fit § 16(a)(3)’s final-decision category. The Eleventh Circuit properly exercised appellate jurisdiction, and its ruling on that threshold issue is affirmed.

##### Question 2: Silence about arbitral costs

Holding and operative rule: Contractual silence about arbitral costs does not itself make an arbitration agreement unenforceable, and the categorical invalidity ruling is reversed and remanded for record development. A claimant invoking prohibitive expense must present concrete, claimant-specific evidence that likely unavoidable charges would make the arbitral forum practically unavailable. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)  
Reasoning: Federal statutory claims may be sent to arbitration only if the arbitral forum remains capable of effective vindication, and costs can in principle make a forum practically unavailable. Silence, however, identifies neither the administrator, the governing fee schedule, the allocation of charges, available waivers, nor the amount the claimant is likely to bear. It therefore supports no categorical inference that arbitration is accessible or foreclosed. The claimant must identify likely mandatory charges and show, in light of personal circumstances, that those charges would probably prevent pursuit of the claim. Randolph’s present record does not permit that determination. The cost ruling is reversed, but the case is remanded so the relevant arrangements and claimant-specific facts may be developed before arbitration is compelled or denied on prohibitive-cost grounds.

#### Concurrences

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer: They agree that dismissal produced a final order, that silence alone cannot sustain categorical invalidity, and that remand is required. They would not assign all uncertainty to the consumer where the drafter and repeat user of the arbitral forum has superior access to the governing cost practices and should be required to disclose that information during the remand inquiry.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

FAA § 16(a)(3): clarified; an order compelling arbitration is final when it dismisses all claims and ends the federal action.  
*Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto*: applied; neutral contract and forum-access defenses remain available, but arbitration may not be rejected categorically from a feature, such as silence, that establishes no actual barrier.  
Effective-vindication principle: clarified; prohibitive-cost objections require concrete evidence of likely unavoidable charges and practical unavailability, with lawful cost allocation and severability remaining available where applicable.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The appellate-finality ruling is affirmed, the categorical cost ruling is reversed, and the case is remanded for a concrete evidentiary determination of whether likely mandatory arbitral charges would make the forum practically unavailable to Randolph.  
Effect: The agreement is not invalid merely because it is silent about costs, but arbitration may not be enforced or denied on prohibitive-cost grounds until the relevant cost arrangements and claimant-specific evidence are lawfully evaluated. Ordinary inconvenience, uncertain expense, severability, lawful cost allocation, and issues not decided below remain open under governing law.

### Bush v. Gore

Argued December 11, 2000 | Decided December 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Florida; judgment below: statewide manual recount ordered in the presidential-election contest, 772 So. 2d 1243 (Fla. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/98/))  
Area of Law: Elections; Equal Protection; Recount administration

Questions Presented:
1. Whether materially varying standards for comparable ballots in one statewide recount violate equal protection.
2. Whether federal law required termination of the recount and whether the broader Article II theory had to be decided.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor in full; Scalia as to Question 1; Kennedy and Souter in full; Thomas as to Question 1; Breyer in full | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion for five Justices; Question 1 for seven Justices |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees with the equal-treatment holding but would terminate the recount and reach the Article II ground |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Rejects a federal equal-protection basis for vacating the Florida judgment and would leave recount administration to the state courts |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate the defective recount directions and remand for corrective proceedings if Florida law still permits a recount.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: one statewide adjudicative recount may not assign materially different legal treatment to materially comparable ballots through county or counting-team happenstance; the defect is corrected by remand under prospective statewide standards, while § 5 remains a conditional safe harbor and the broader Article II theory is reserved.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Equal treatment in a statewide recount

Holding and operative rule: Once a State undertakes one statewide adjudicative recount for one office, equal protection requires sufficiently uniform and reviewable standards to provide materially equivalent legal treatment to materially comparable ballots. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer)  
Reasoning: The constitutional problem is not the use of human judgment to determine voter intent, nor does equal protection require identical rules for physically different ballots or voting technologies. The defect arises when a single statewide legal adjudication assigns different legal value to materially comparable ballots according to county, counting team, or an unannounced shift in criteria. The recount order used a general voter-intent instruction while the record showed materially varying standards, differing ballot universes, and no statewide mechanism for reconciling inconsistent applications. Those conditions made legal treatment depend on happenstance rather than a sufficiently definite statewide rule. Written, public, prospective, and reviewable criteria can preserve judgment where needed while ensuring materially equivalent treatment. The existing directions therefore cannot remain in force.

##### Question 2: Remedy, § 5, and Article II

Holding and operative rule: The December 12 safe-harbor date does not itself terminate an otherwise lawful state election contest. The defective recount directions are vacated, and Florida may proceed if state law permits only under prompt, written, public, sufficiently objective, prospective, consistently applicable, and reviewable statewide standards; the broader Article II claim is not reached.  
Reasoning: Section 5 specifies conditions under which a State’s timely final determination receives the conclusive federal treatment Congress prescribed. It does not command a State to abandon an election contest when the safe-harbor date passes. Missing that date may affect later treatment of the State’s electoral determination, but it does not itself supply a federal injunction against further state proceedings. The proper response to the equal-treatment violation is therefore correction, not federal selection of a winner. Primary responsibility for the governing recount framework belongs to the state institutions authorized to prescribe the manner of appointing electors. State courts may interpret and administer that law, but the federal judiciary does not choose the ballot universe, technology, substantive voter-intent rule, state deadline, or electoral slate. If Florida law still authorizes a recount, it may resume only after constitutionally adequate statewide standards are fixed and made reviewable. Because that disposition resolves the federal defect, the broader Article II theory remains reserved.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that the recount’s varying standards violate equal protection. They would hold that Florida’s lawful timetable and Article II authority leave no permissible basis for resuming the recount and therefore would terminate it rather than remand for possible corrective proceedings.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg: The variations inherent in decentralized ballot review do not establish the federal equal-protection violation the Court announces, particularly where state courts remain available to supervise implementation. They would affirm and leave the conduct and completion of the election contest to Florida’s lawful institutions.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Equal-protection principles governing the franchise: extended; a single statewide adjudicative recount must use sufficiently definite and reviewable standards to give materially equivalent treatment to materially comparable ballots.  
3 U.S.C. § 5: clarified; the safe harbor is conditional federal protection for a qualifying state determination, not an independent command terminating otherwise lawful state proceedings.  
*Bush v. Palm Beach County Canvassing Board*, decided December 4, 2000: followed; federal review respects authoritative state-law clarification and does not decide a broader Article II claim when a narrower corrective disposition is sufficient.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Florida is vacated, and the case is remanded immediately; the existing federal stay shall not operate as final termination, and any further recount authorized by Florida law must proceed under written, public, prospective, sufficiently objective, consistently applicable, and reviewable statewide standards.  
Effect: The existing recount directions may not be used. Florida remains responsible for determining whether state law authorizes further proceedings and, if so, the lawful ballot universe, treatment of different technologies, recount procedures, and state-law deadlines. This Court selects no winner or slate, imposes no federal December 12 termination date, and does not decide how Congress must treat electoral votes outside § 5’s safe harbor.

### Fiore v. White

Argued October 12, 1999 | Decided January 9, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: federal habeas relief denied, 149 F.3d 221 (3d Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/225/))  
Area of Law: Due Process; Criminal elements; Federal habeas corpus

Questions Presented:
1. Does Due Process permit a conviction when the State’s highest court confirms that the prosecution never proved an element that was always part of the offense?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam | Per curiam | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the per curiam opinion in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Failure to prove an element the offense always required

Holding and operative rule: Due Process forbids a conviction when authoritative state law confirms that the prosecution failed to prove an element that formed part of the offense at the time of the conduct and trial.  
Reasoning: The certified answer from the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania establishes that the permit requirement identified in *Commonwealth v. Scarpone* was not a new rule. It stated what the statute meant when Fiore acted and when he was tried. The case therefore presents no question about retroactive application of a later change in law. Pennsylvania convicted Fiore of operating a hazardous-waste facility without a permit, yet the Commonwealth conceded that he possessed a permit and introduced no evidence proving the statutory no-permit element. A criminal judgment cannot stand when the prosecution fails to prove beyond a reasonable doubt conduct falling within every element of the offense as authoritatively defined. The denial of federal habeas relief must be reversed, with the precise remedial and double-jeopardy consequences left for lawful determination on remand.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Gaudin*: applied; a criminal conviction requires lawful determination and proof beyond a reasonable doubt of every element of the charged offense.  
*Jackson v. Virginia*: followed; a conviction cannot stand when the record contains no evidence of a basic statutory element.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for lawful habeas relief based on the failure to prove an element the offense always required.  
Effect: Fiore’s conviction may not remain in force on the present no-permit theory. The lower courts retain responsibility for the precise form of relief and any double-jeopardy consequence, and this decision does not govern a genuinely new state-law construction.

### Glover v. United States

Argued November 27, 2000 | Decided January 9, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: collateral sentencing claim rejected, 182 F.3d 921 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/198/))  
Area of Law: Sixth Amendment; Ineffective assistance; Sentencing

Questions Presented:
1. May a court reject Strickland prejudice because counsel’s error increased imprisonment by less than a judicially defined “significant” amount?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Prejudice from increased imprisonment

Holding and operative rule: A defendant may establish *Strickland* prejudice by showing a reasonable probability that counsel’s deficient performance produced any greater term of imprisonment; no separate minimum-duration, percentage, or free-standing significance threshold applies.  
Reasoning: *Strickland* asks whether deficient performance created a reasonable probability of a different lawful result. It does not authorize courts to declare some additional term of imprisonment constitutionally insignificant as a categorical matter. A numerical threshold would also have no principled boundary because an increase could be measured in months, as a percentage of the sentence, or against some other judicially invented baseline. *Williams v. Taylor* confirms that *Lockhart v. Fretwell* does not replace the ordinary outcome-focused prejudice inquiry where counsel’s error affects a legal sentencing calculation. Glover must still prove deficient performance and a reasonable probability that the correct grouping analysis would have produced a lower lawful sentence. Those unresolved questions belong to the lower courts under the proper standard.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Strickland v. Washington*: clarified; prejudice from a sentencing error has no independent quantitative significance threshold when deficient performance probably caused additional imprisonment.  
*Williams v. Taylor*, 529 U.S. 362 (2000): followed; *Lockhart v. Fretwell* does not supplant ordinary *Strickland* prejudice where counsel’s failure affects a lawful sentencing calculation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for application of ordinary *Strickland* deficiency and counterfactual-sentencing requirements without a minimum-duration or percentage threshold.  
Effect: Glover’s ineffective-assistance claim returns for determination under the correct prejudice rule. The Court does not presume deficient performance, accept speculation about a lower sentence, or dictate the sentence to be imposed on remand.

# OCTOBER TERM 2000, CHUNK 2 OF 8

## Docket and Chronology

| Date | Case or matter | Event |
