| January 9, 2001 | *Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. U.S. Army Corps of Engineers* | Merits decision |
| January 17, 2001 | *Seling v. Young* | Merits decision |
| February 5, 2001 | *Wasson v. Sonoma County Junior College* | Merits decision |
| February 12, 2001 | *Daggett v. Commission on Governmental Ethics & Election Practices* | Merits decision |
| February 20, 2001 | *Brentwood Academy v. Tennessee Secondary School Athletic Association* | Merits decision |
| February 20, 2001 | *Illinois v. McArthur* | Merits decision |
| February 21, 2001 | *Board of Trustees of the University of Alabama v. Garrett* | Merits decision |
| February 21, 2001 | *Buckman Co. v. Plaintiffs’ Legal Committee* | Merits decision |
| February 27, 2001 | *Semtek International Inc. v. Lockheed Martin Corp.* | Merits decision |
| February 27, 2001 | *Whitman v. American Trucking Associations, Inc. / American Trucking Associations, Inc. v. Whitman* | Consolidated merits decision |

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. U.S. Army Corps of Engineers

Argued October 31, 2000 | Decided January 9, 2001 `OT_2000CHUNK2.md` `OT_2000CHUNK1.md` `JUDICIAL_TURN_OUTPUT_RENDER_CONTRACT.md` `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md` `STANDING_STATE.md` `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: Clean Water Act jurisdiction sustained, 191 F.3d 845 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/159/))  
Area of Law: Environmental law; Administrative law; Clean Water Act jurisdiction

Questions Presented:
1. Does the Clean Water Act authorize federal jurisdiction over isolated, nonnavigable, intrastate ponds solely because migratory birds use them?

#### Judgment

Reversed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: the Clean Water Act’s reference to navigable waters, defined as waters of the United States, retains an enacted water-based jurisdictional boundary; an informal migratory-bird rationale cannot extend § 404 to isolated intrastate ponds lacking adjacency, a tributary connection, hydrological linkage, or another statutory water-based connection.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Isolated ponds used by migratory birds

Holding and operative rule: The Clean Water Act does not authorize § 404 jurisdiction over isolated, nonnavigable, intrastate ponds solely because migratory birds use them. The Corps’ jurisdictional assertion on that basis exceeds the authority Congress enacted.  
Reasoning: The Act reaches more than waters navigable in fact, but its use of navigable waters and waters of the United States still supplies a water-based limit. *United States v. Riverside Bayview Homes, Inc.*, 474 U.S. 121 (1985), sustained jurisdiction over wetlands adjacent to covered waters because their physical relationship made the statutory boundary difficult to draw; it did not eliminate that boundary for isolated ponds. The record identifies no tributary, adjacency, hydrological connection, or comparable link and states that the Corps relied solely on migratory-bird use. Under *Christensen v. Harris County*, decided May 1, 2000, the informal rationale carries no delegated force of law and receives weight only to the extent it persuades. Birdwatching, recreation, tourism, and ecological importance cannot substitute for the enacted connection. Reading the statute to reach ordinary intrastate ponds on that basis would also raise serious federalism questions under the entering Commerce Clause law. Because the statutory question resolves the case, the Court does not decide Congress’s outer constitutional authority.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Congress used waters of the United States to reach the full class of aquatic resources whose destruction bears materially on interstate commerce, and migratory-bird use supplies a reasonable basis for treating these ponds as part of that class. He would defer to the Corps’ longstanding administration and affirm without deciding that every isolated water is covered.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Riverside Bayview Homes, Inc.*: distinguished; jurisdiction over adjacent wetlands does not erase the Act’s water-based boundary for isolated ponds.  
*Christensen v. Harris County*: applied; an informal agency interpretation lacking delegated force of law cannot enlarge statutory jurisdiction beyond the boundary supplied by ordinary interpretation.  
Clean Water Act §§ 301, 404, and 502(7): clarified; migratory-bird use alone does not bring isolated, nonnavigable, intrastate ponds within waters of the United States.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the Corps’ assertion of § 404 jurisdiction based solely on migratory-bird use is set aside.  
Effect: The challenged ponds are not subject to federal § 404 jurisdiction on the theory adjudicated. State environmental regulation remains unaffected, and a materially different federal assertion grounded in an enacted water-based connection remains open under governing law.

### Seling v. Young

Argued October 31, 2000 | Decided January 17, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: as-applied punishment challenge permitted and case remanded for further proceedings, 192 F.3d 870 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/250/))  
Area of Law: Civil commitment; Ex Post Facto Clause; Double Jeopardy

Questions Presented:
1. May a person confined under a facially civil statute prove that its actual administration is punitive for Ex Post Facto or Double Jeopardy purposes?

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm and remand.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Punitive administration of a facially civil commitment law

Holding and operative rule: A facially civil commitment statute remains subject to an as-applied Ex Post Facto or Double Jeopardy challenge upon the clearest proof that its combined conditions, treatment structure, duration, release mechanics, and actual administration operate as punishment. The civil label does not foreclose that inquiry.  
Reasoning: Simulated *Kansas v. Hendricks*, decided June 23, 1997, makes a legislature’s civil designation presumptively controlling but not conclusive. Its holding classified the confinement by examining the measure’s combined design and actual operation, including whether treatment, review, less restrictive alternatives, and release functioned as a genuine civil system. A rule barring all as-applied inquiry would remove the very evidence the controlling classification standard makes relevant. Young alleges prisonlike conditions, materially inadequate treatment, indefinite duration, and release administration that together make his confinement punitive. Those allegations are not findings, and ordinary deficiencies, isolated misconduct, or disagreement with clinical judgment will not satisfy the clearest-proof requirement. The Ninth Circuit therefore correctly allowed the legal theory, but the necessary factual determinations belong to the lower courts. Any relief must correct a proven punitive feature and need not entail facial invalidation or automatic release.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy: Once the statutory scheme has been classified as civil, conditions arising during administration do not convert the law itself into punishment for Ex Post Facto or Double Jeopardy purposes. Defective treatment or unlawful conditions may support due-process or remedial relief, but he would reverse the Ninth Circuit’s punishment theory.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Kansas v. Hendricks*: followed; the clearest-proof classification inquiry includes the combined actual operation of a nominally civil commitment scheme.  
*United States v. Ursery*: reaffirmed; a civil designation controls unless the measure’s design and operation provide the clearest proof of punishment.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for focused factfinding under the clearest-proof standard.  
Effect: Young may attempt to prove that the combined administration of his confinement is punitive. No punitive defect is presumed, and any relief must be tied to a defect proved on the record rather than to automatic release or facial invalidation of Washington’s statute.

### Wasson v. Sonoma County Junior College

Argued December 6, 2000 | Decided February 5, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: individual-capacity claims dismissed, 203 F.3d 659 (9th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/203/659/474781/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Public employment; Qualified immunity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a public employer can violate the First Amendment by taking adverse action against an employee because officials mistakenly attribute protected expression to her, whether the attributed expression itself must be protected, and what motive and causation showing is required.
2. Whether the individual defendants are personally liable in damages.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone in full; Stevens in full; Scalia as to qualified immunity; Kennedy in full; Souter in full; Thomas as to qualified immunity; Ginsburg in full; Breyer in full | Supports affirmance | Stone: complete opinion; Stevens: complete opinion; Scalia: qualified-immunity holding; Kennedy: complete opinion; Souter: complete opinion; Thomas: qualified-immunity holding; Ginsburg: complete opinion; Breyer: complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Rejects perceived-speech liability while agreeing that qualified immunity requires affirmance |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retaliation for mistakenly attributed expression

Holding and operative rule: A public employer may violate the First Amendment by taking adverse action because it attributes protected expression to an employee who did not actually speak, when the attributed expression is constitutionally protected and materially motivates the action. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)  
Reasoning: The constitutional injury lies in the government’s use of adverse employment power to suppress or punish what it understands to be protected expression. That purpose does not become permissible because officials identified the speaker incorrectly. The attributed communication must itself concern a matter protected under the public-employee framework, and the employer retains the ordinary authority to act on a genuinely held, objectively reasonable understanding of the communication’s content, context, and workplace effects under *Waters v. Churchill*, 511 U.S. 661 (1994). Attribution must materially motivate the challenged action, and the employer may avoid liability by proving that it would have taken the same action for lawful reasons. The rule therefore protects against retaliatory motive without creating liability for unprotected private grievances, materially disruptive speech, or employment action resting on an independent lawful ground.

##### Question 2: Qualified immunity

Holding and operative rule: The individual defendants are entitled to qualified immunity because the perceived-speech rule was not clearly established when they acted.  
Reasoning: Clearly established law is assessed from the complete body of materially relevant authority under *Elder v. Holloway*, 510 U.S. 510 (1994). Existing public-employee cases prohibited retaliation for an employee’s protected expression and addressed employers’ reasonable factual understandings of speech that had occurred. They did not establish with sufficient clarity that the First Amendment also imposed personal damages liability when officials acted against a person who had not spoken but whom they mistakenly believed to be the speaker. The Ninth Circuit’s contrary categorical merits rule confirms the unsettled character of the question rather than supplying earlier notice. Because reasonable officials could have believed that actual expression by the plaintiff was required, damages may not be imposed on these defendants. The constitutional rule stated today governs the merits, but it does not retroactively defeat immunity.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in the judgment: The First Amendment protects an actual exercise of speech or association, not a government official’s mistaken belief that an employee exercised the right. Because no perceived-speech rule was clearly established, he agrees that the damages judgment must be affirmed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Waters v. Churchill*: extended; public-employment retaliation may rest on protected expression attributed to the employee even when the attribution of authorship is mistaken.  
*Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*: applied; attributed protected expression must materially motivate the action, and the ordinary same-decision defense remains available.  
*Elder v. Holloway*: applied; qualified immunity is determined from the complete body of law existing when the officials acted.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit dismissing the individual-capacity damages claims is affirmed on qualified-immunity grounds.  
Effect: Wasson may not recover damages from the individual defendants, but the lower court’s categorical conclusion that actual authorship is indispensable is rejected. Entity liability and unrequested prospective relief are not before the Court.

### Daggett v. Commission on Governmental Ethics & Election Practices

Argued December 6, 2000 | Decided February 12, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: Maine’s public-financing and capped matching-fund provisions sustained, 205 F.3d 445 (1st Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/205/445/559226/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Campaign finance; Public financing

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Maine’s capped matching-fund trigger for participating publicly financed candidates violates the First Amendment rights of privately financed candidates or independent speakers.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Capped responsive public financing

Holding and operative rule: A capped public-financing trigger that releases additional public funds when opposing private spending rises does not violate the First Amendment merely because it enables responsive speech, so long as the private candidate and independent speaker remain legally free to spend.  
Reasoning: Simulated *Day v. Holahan*, decided May 15, 1995, resolves the operative question. Maine’s trigger does not prohibit, cap, tax, delay, or otherwise regulate the private speaker’s expenditure. It supplies public resources to a participating candidate within a voluntary financing program after specified private spending occurs, and those responsive funds cease at an enacted cap. The First Amendment secures the speaker’s ability to advocate; it does not secure freedom from counterspeech or a right to prevent another candidate from receiving lawful resources. Equalization of influence cannot justify suppressing private expression, but this mechanism suppresses none. The privately financed candidate and independent spender remain able to raise and spend without a legal ceiling created by the trigger. A materially different arrangement imposing a charge, disclosure burden, expenditure limit, viewpoint-based condition, or uncapped automatic response would require separate review.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: Awarding money to the opposed candidate dollar for dollar when private advocacy crosses the statutory threshold makes the speaker’s own expenditure the legal cause of a targeted governmental benefit to the other side. He would treat that consequence as a substantial burden on marginal campaign speech and reverse.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Day v. Holahan*: followed; responsive public funding does not burden a private speaker who remains free to spend and incurs no restriction, charge, delay, reporting obligation, or other legal disability.  
*Buckley v. Valeo*: applied; a voluntary public subsidy may increase electoral speech without becoming an expenditure restriction on nonparticipants.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit sustaining the challenged matching-fund provision is affirmed.  
Effect: Maine may continue the capped responsive-funding mechanism within its voluntary public-financing program. The judgment does not approve an uncapped trigger, a viewpoint-defined trigger, an indirect expenditure ceiling, or a separate legal disability imposed on private speakers.

### Brentwood Academy v. Tennessee Secondary School Athletic Association

Argued October 11, 2000 | Decided February 20, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: the Association held not to be a state actor, 180 F.3d 758 (6th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/288/case.html))  
Area of Law: State action; Fourteenth Amendment; Interscholastic athletics

Questions Presented:
1. Is a statewide athletic association a state actor when public schools and officials are pervasively entwined in its membership, governance, and enforcement?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Pervasive public entwinement

Holding and operative rule: A nominally private statewide association is a state actor when public schools and public officials are pervasively entwined in its composition, governance, and enforcement of the challenged rule. Public membership or funding alone is insufficient, but the combined institutional control here makes the challenged action fairly attributable to the State.  
Reasoning: Constitutional attribution turns on the relationship between government and the particular conduct challenged. The Association regulates interscholastic athletics throughout Tennessee, consists overwhelmingly of public schools, and exercises its rulemaking and disciplinary authority through public-school officials acting within an institution built to serve those schools. Those facts show more than government approval, funding, or regulation of independent private choice. The public institutions collectively constitute the Association, select and supply its governing personnel, and use it as the statewide mechanism for rules they then enforce against member schools. *Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp.*, 513 U.S. 374 (1995), confirms that formal private status cannot defeat constitutional responsibility where government creates or controls the relevant institutional authority. The attribution is function specific and does not determine whether Brentwood’s underlying First Amendment claim succeeds.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Scalia and Kennedy: The Association is a voluntary private membership organization whose rules are adopted and enforced by the entity rather than by Tennessee, and public-school participation does not convert collective private action into state action. He would require governmental creation, coercion, direction of the challenged decision, or delegation of an exclusive public function and would affirm.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp.*: extended; pervasive public composition and control may establish constitutional attribution even without a formal governmental charter.  
*American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan*: distinguished; independently exercised private choice differs from action by an association whose governing and enforcement authority is pervasively constituted by public institutions and officials.  
*NCAA v. Tarkanian*: distinguished; the NCAA’s relationship to a single public university did not involve the statewide public composition and control present here.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the preserved First Amendment claim and any source-authorized remedy.  
Effect: The Association must be treated as a state actor when enforcing the rule challenged in this case. The decision does not constitutionalize every private association receiving public funds, serving public institutions, or including public members.

### Illinois v. McArthur

Argued November 1, 2000 | Decided February 20, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Appellate Court of Illinois; judgment below: suppression of the evidence sustained, 304 Ill. App. 3d 395, 713 N.E.2d 93 (1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/326/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Home; Temporary seizure

Questions Presented:
1. May police with probable cause temporarily prevent unaccompanied entry into a home while diligently obtaining a warrant?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes the narrow dependence on probable cause, diligent warrant pursuit, and short duration |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Temporary securing of a home from outside

Holding and operative rule: Police with probable cause may briefly prevent unaccompanied entry into a home while diligently obtaining a warrant when a concrete evidence-preservation risk exists and the restraint uses the least intrusive practical means. The reasonableness of the measure depends on its purpose, scope, and duration.  
Reasoning: The home receives the Fourth Amendment’s highest protection, but the officers did not enter or search it before a magistrate issued a warrant. McArthur’s wife had just reported marijuana inside, the officers had probable cause, and they reasonably believed that McArthur could destroy the evidence if allowed to enter alone. They preserved the status quo for approximately two hours while another officer diligently sought the warrant, permitted supervised entry for personal needs, and ended the restraint when the warrant issued. That combination materially limited the interference with possessory and residential interests while preserving the warrant requirement itself. The decision rests on probable cause, a concrete risk, diligence, supervised access, and brief duration together. It supplies no general authority to exclude a resident from a home whenever officers hope to obtain a warrant.

#### Concurrences

Souter: The restraint is reasonable only because the officers possessed probable cause, promptly sought judicial authorization, permitted supervised access, and maintained the restriction for no longer than the warrant process required. A longer or less carefully limited exclusion would present a different case.

#### Dissents

Stevens: Preventing a resident from entering his home is a grave seizure that should require a warrant or a genuine emergency comparable to the exceptions permitting entry. He would hold that the officers could guard the premises and seek a warrant without imposing this warrantless exclusion and would affirm suppression.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Segura v. United States*: applied; officers with probable cause may preserve the status quo while diligently obtaining a warrant when they do not conduct the search the warrant requirement reserves to a magistrate.  
*Payton v. New York*: distinguished; the officers neither entered nor searched the home before the warrant issued.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Appellate Court of Illinois is reversed, and the case is remanded for further proceedings consistent with the validity of the temporary outside restraint.  
Effect: The evidence may not be suppressed on the ground that the officers briefly prevented McArthur’s unaccompanied entry while diligently obtaining the warrant. Any distinct challenge to the warrant, search, or evidence remains governed by the issues preserved below.

### Board of Trustees of the University of Alabama v. Garrett

Argued October 11, 2000 | Decided February 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: Title I abrogation of state sovereign immunity sustained, 193 F.3d 1214 (11th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/356/))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; Fourteenth Amendment § 5; State sovereign immunity

Questions Presented:
1. What scrutiny governs governmental disability classifications?
2. Whether Congress validly abrogated state immunity for bounded Title I duties.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens in full; O’Connor in full; Scalia as to Question 1; Kennedy in full; Souter in full; Thomas as to Question 1; Ginsburg in full; Breyer in full | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Stevens: complete opinion; O’Connor: complete opinion; Scalia: Question 1; Kennedy: complete opinion; Souter: complete opinion; Thomas: Question 1; Ginsburg: complete opinion; Breyer: complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the judgment | Would sustain additional Title I applications while joining the bounded holding and remand |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees with rational-basis review but would reverse the abrogation judgment |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: ordinary rational-basis review remains the constitutional baseline, but Congress validly abrogated immunity for two bounded Title I damages classes closely fitted to irrational or stereotyped state exclusion: intentional disability discrimination against an otherwise-qualified employee and denial of a specific necessary reasonable accommodation within Title I’s qualification, essential-function, undue-hardship, and direct-threat limits.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Constitutional standard for disability classifications

Holding and operative rule: Governmental disability classifications remain subject to ordinary rational-basis review for purposes of the Fourteenth Amendment § 5 inquiry in this case. Disability distinctions may fail when they rest on irrational prejudice, stereotype, or a sham rationale, but the Constitution does not independently prohibit every disability distinction or every failure to accommodate. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)  
Reasoning: *City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc.*, 473 U.S. 432 (1985), supplies the received constitutional baseline. *City of Boerne v. Flores*, decided June 25, 1997, and simulated *Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents*, decided January 11, 2000, require the Court to identify that baseline before evaluating Congress’s enforcement legislation. The Court therefore does not substitute intermediate scrutiny or the ADA’s statutory standard for the Equal Protection Clause. Rational-basis review remains capable of invalidating classifications that are genuinely arbitrary, rest on unsupported negative stereotypes, or invoke a governmental interest that does not explain the actual distinction. It does not make every workplace accommodation constitutionally mandatory or every adverse effect a constitutional violation. The narrower constitutional target does not end the § 5 inquiry, because Congress may enact fitted preventive and remedial rules addressing conduct that threatens or conceals that target.

##### Question 2: Claim-specific Title I abrogation

Holding and operative rule: Congress validly abrogated state sovereign immunity for Title I damages claims alleging either intentional disability discrimination against an otherwise-qualified state employee or denial of a specific reasonable accommodation necessary for equal participation by an otherwise-qualified employee who can perform the job’s essential functions, when the accommodation does not fundamentally alter the job, impose undue hardship, or create a direct threat. No broader Title I-wide abrogation is decided.  
Reasoning: Section 5 validity is assessed claim by claim and remedy by remedy. Congress addressed a substantial history of state exclusion resting on disability stereotypes and confronted conduct whose irrational basis may be difficult to expose through direct proof. The first bounded class targets intentional unequal treatment of an otherwise-qualified employee, conduct closely aligned with the constitutional prohibition on irrational or stereotyped classification. The second uses a specific necessary accommodation as a limited preventive device: when an employee can perform essential functions and the requested measure creates no fundamental alteration, undue hardship, or direct threat, refusal may reveal that a facially neutral rule is operating as an avoidable proxy for the same forbidden exclusion. Title I’s elements and defenses preserve legitimate safety, qualification, and operational judgments and prevent the accommodation duty from becoming an unlimited substantive command. The record before this Court establishes only that state employees seek Title I damages and that the Eleventh Circuit sustained abrogation generally. The lower courts must classify each pleaded claim and adjudicate every statutory element, defense, causation requirement, and remedial limit without presuming ultimate liability.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The two claim classes identified by the Court are valid § 5 enforcement and suffice to reject categorical immunity. They would sustain additional Title I applications supported by Congress’s record and the statute’s limiting defenses, but agree that those questions need not be resolved before the claims are classified on remand.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: Rational-basis review is the correct constitutional baseline, but Title I’s intentional-discrimination and accommodation claims do not require proof that the State acted irrationally. They would reverse because § 5 may enforce actual constitutional violations but may not expose the state treasury to damages for broader statutory duties on the fit asserted here.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc.*: followed; ordinary rational-basis review remains the constitutional standard for governmental disability classifications in this § 5 inquiry.  
*City of Boerne v. Flores*: clarified; congruence and proportionality are assessed claim by claim, and bounded preventive duties may support retrospective remedies when closely fitted to the constitutional target.  
*Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents*: distinguished; Title I’s qualification, essential-function, undue-hardship, and direct-threat limits make the two approved claim classes materially narrower and closer to unconstitutional exclusion than the ADEA’s broad age prohibition.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed insofar as it rejects sovereign immunity for the two fitted claim classes, vacated insofar as it sustains Title I-wide abrogation, and the cases are remanded for claim-specific classification and adjudication.  
Effect: A qualifying damages claim within either approved class may proceed subject to every statutory element, defense, causation requirement, and remedial limit. Other Title I applications require their own § 5 analysis, and the Court makes no finding of discrimination, qualification, accommodation necessity, causation, or damages; properly framed prospective officer relief remains separately governed.

### Buckman Co. v. Plaintiffs’ Legal Committee

Argued December 4, 2000 | Decided February 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: fraud-on-the-FDA state-law theories held not preempted, 159 F.3d 817 (3d Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/341/))  
Area of Law: Federal preemption; Medical-device regulation; State tort law

Questions Presented:
1. Does federal law preempt state claims whose asserted duty exists solely because a consultant allegedly defrauded the FDA?

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Would reverse on the failure of the fraud-on-the-FDA causation theory without adopting the Court’s full preemption rationale |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: State duties existing solely through the federal regulatory relationship

Holding and operative rule: State claims whose duty and breach exist solely because a party allegedly defrauded the FDA are conflict preempted, while independently grounded traditional state-law duties remain available. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)  
Reasoning: The relationship between a regulated applicant and the FDA exists exclusively by federal law, and Congress placed enforcement of the applicant’s disclosure duties within the federal regulatory scheme. A state claim that requires a jury to determine whether the agency was defrauded, how the agency would have acted, and what regulatory consequence should follow creates a private enforcement mechanism for that federal relationship. It risks inconsistent fraud standards and can disrupt the FDA’s calibrated choices among investigation, corrective submissions, administrative action, and other remedies. That is the operative federal conflict required by simulated *Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*, 514 U.S. 280 (1995), rather than an inference from regulatory silence alone. The result is source specific. A design, manufacturing, warning, or misrepresentation duty owed independently to patients or consumers under traditional state law does not become preempted merely because FDA materials provide evidence relevant to breach or causation.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Thomas, concurring in the judgment: The plaintiffs cannot establish that the FDA would have denied or withdrawn authorization absent the alleged representations without impermissible speculation about the agency’s counterfactual decision. They would reverse on that causation ground and would not adopt a categorical preemption rule for every claim involving alleged fraud on a federal agency.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*: clarified; an operative conflict exists when state law creates private enforcement of duties arising solely from an exclusively federal agency relationship.  
*Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr*: distinguished; traditional state duties existing independently of federal disclosure obligations remain available even when federal regulatory evidence bears on their application.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit preserving the fraud-on-the-FDA theories is reversed.  
Effect: Claims whose asserted duty exists solely because of alleged fraud on the FDA may not proceed. Independently grounded traditional state-law duties are unaffected and remain subject to their own elements, causation requirements, defenses, and any separately applicable preemption rule.

### Semtek International Inc. v. Lockheed Martin Corp.

Argued December 5, 2000 | Decided February 27, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Special Appeals of Maryland; judgment below: the later action held claim-precluded, 128 Md. App. 39, 736 A.2d 1104 (1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/497/))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; Diversity jurisdiction; Claim preclusion

Questions Presented:
1. What claim-preclusive effect does a federal diversity judgment dismissing an action as time barred have in another State’s court?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: External preclusive effect of a federal diversity judgment

Holding and operative rule: The external claim-preclusive effect of a federal diversity judgment is governed by federal common law, which ordinarily adopts the preclusion law of the State in which the federal court sat. Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 41(b) does not itself prescribe nationwide claim preclusion.  
Reasoning: Rule 41(b)’s statement that specified dismissals operate as an adjudication on the merits governs the effect of the dismissal within the rendering federal action and distinguishes a dismissal that permits refiling in that court from one that does not. Reading the Rule as a nationwide preclusion code would regulate substantive consequences far beyond case procedure and create serious Rules Enabling Act concerns. A federal rule is nevertheless necessary to determine the effect of a federal judgment. In a diversity case, the ordinary federal common-law rule incorporates the rendering State’s preclusion law because the substantive claim originates in state law and a materially different federal rule would encourage forum shopping and unequal administration. A significant federal interest may require a different federal rule in a particular setting, but none has been identified here. The Maryland court therefore erred by treating Rule 41(b) itself as conclusively barring Semtek’s action.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 41(b): clarified; adjudication on the merits does not itself create a nationwide claim-preclusion rule.  
*Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc.*: applied; federal diversity law ordinarily preserves state-created substantive consequences while using an appropriate federal procedural mechanism.  
*Erie Railroad Co. v. Tompkins*: followed; federal common law ordinarily incorporates the rendering State’s preclusion rule for a diversity judgment.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Special Appeals of Maryland is reversed, and the case is remanded for application of federal common law ordinarily incorporating California’s law of claim preclusion.  
Effect: The California federal dismissal does not receive nationwide claim-preclusive effect merely because it was labeled with prejudice or treated as an adjudication on the merits under Rule 41(b). The Maryland courts must determine its external effect under the governing incorporated rule, subject to any demonstrated significant federal interest.

### Whitman v. American Trucking Associations, Inc. / American Trucking Associations, Inc. v. Whitman

Argued November 7, 2000 | Decided February 27, 2001  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit in consolidated Nos. 99-1257 and 99-1426; judgments below: implementation costs excluded, the delegation held constitutionally deficient, and EPA directed to adopt a limiting construction, 175 F.3d 1027, on rehearing 195 F.3d 4 (D.C. Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/457/))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; Clean Air Act; Nondelegation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Clean Air Act § 109 permits consideration of implementation costs.
2. Whether § 109(b)(1) supplies an intelligible principle.
3. Whether an agency may cure an unconstitutional delegation by self-limitation.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No. 99-1426, implementation costs | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The exclusion of implementation costs from § 109 standard setting remains in force. |
| No. 99-1257, nondelegation ruling | Reversed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The ruling that § 109(b)(1) lacks an intelligible principle is set aside. |
| No. 99-1257, agency-cure instruction | Vacated, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The instruction that EPA invent a limiting principle for itself is vacated. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone in full; Stevens as to Question 1; O’Connor in full; Kennedy in full; Souter as to Question 1; Thomas in full; Ginsburg in full; Breyer as to Questions 2 and 3 | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Stone: complete opinion; Stevens: Question 1; O’Connor: complete opinion; Kennedy: complete opinion; Souter: Question 1; Thomas: complete opinion; Ginsburg: complete opinion; Breyer: Questions 2 and 3 |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Accepts the intelligible-principle doctrine while reserving whether it adequately enforces Article I |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stevens | Souter | Supports the judgment | Agrees that the delegation is adequately limited but characterizes the delegated authority as legislative power |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Agrees that costs are excluded based on statutory structure and history rather than the Court’s clear-text presumption |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the exclusion of implementation costs, reverse the nondelegation ruling, vacate the agency-cure instruction, and remand.  
Public role: joins Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Implementation costs under § 109

Holding and operative rule: Section 109 directs health-based national ambient air-quality standards and does not permit EPA to consider implementation costs when setting those standards.  
Reasoning: Section 109(b)(1) directs EPA to set standards requisite to protect public health with an adequate margin of safety, using the air-quality criteria developed under § 108. That language identifies the public-health objective and the scientific record relevant to the standard. Congress addressed economic and technological considerations elsewhere in the Clean Air Act’s implementation provisions but did not make them part of the standard-setting judgment. Ordinary interpretive tools therefore supply a fixed boundary before any question of administrative deference arises under *Christensen v. Harris County*, decided May 1, 2000. Excluding implementation costs at this stage does not make costs irrelevant throughout the statutory scheme; it preserves Congress’s separation between identifying the health-protective level and administering attainment through the mechanisms Congress enacted.

##### Question 2: Intelligible principle

Holding and operative rule: Section 109(b)(1) supplies an intelligible principle by directing EPA to set standards requisite to protect public health with an adequate margin of safety.  
Reasoning: Article I requires Congress to state the governing policy and legal boundary, while permitting an agency to apply that policy to technical evidence and changing conditions. Section 109 identifies the regulated pollutants through the statutory criteria process, fixes protection of public health as the objective, requires an adequate margin of safety, and subjects EPA’s judgment to judicial review for conformity with those commands and the administrative record. That direction is at least as definite as delegations sustained under the entering intelligible-principle doctrine. The statute does not give EPA authority to select any social objective it prefers or to balance health protection against implementation cost. The D.C. Circuit therefore erred in treating the breadth of scientific judgment within the enacted boundary as an unconstitutional absence of law.

##### Question 3: Agency self-cure

Holding and operative rule: An agency may administer and interpret a statutory boundary, but it cannot cure an unconstitutional delegation by inventing a limiting principle that Congress did not enact.  
Reasoning: The constitutional source of delegated authority is Congress. If a statute supplied no adequate governing principle, an agency’s voluntary promise to limit itself would leave the underlying transfer of power unchanged and would permit the recipient of the power to determine the legal bounds of its own authority. Judicial review could not convert that self-selected policy into the missing congressional command. Section 109 is valid because Congress itself supplied a health-protection standard with an adequate margin of safety, not because EPA can devise a narrower policy. The court of appeals’ instruction that EPA cure the supposed defect through self-limitation must therefore be vacated even though its nondelegation judgment is reversed on the antecedent statutory ground.

#### Concurrences

Thomas: Existing precedent treats an intelligible principle as sufficient, and § 109 satisfies that standard; an agency also cannot cure a missing congressional boundary by self-limitation. He reserves whether the intelligible-principle doctrine fully enforces Article I in a future case presenting and briefing that question.

Stevens, joined by Souter, concurring in part and in the judgment: Section 109 constitutionally delegates rulemaking power because Congress adequately limits it through the public-health and margin-of-safety commands. They would candidly characterize the authority exercised by EPA as delegated legislative power rather than deny that characterization.

Breyer, concurring in part and in the judgment: Section 109 excludes implementation costs, but the conclusion rests on the Act’s structure, history, and technology-forcing design rather than a general presumption that cost consideration requires an unusually clear textual authorization. He otherwise agrees with the Court’s disposition.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Christensen v. Harris County*: applied; ordinary statutory interpretation fixes § 109’s cost boundary before agency views receive persuasion-based weight.  
*J.W. Hampton, Jr. & Co. v. United States*: clarified; an intelligible principle must be supplied by Congress, and § 109’s public-health and margin-of-safety commands satisfy that requirement.  
*Mistretta v. United States*: reaffirmed; Congress may delegate technical application within an enacted policy and judicially enforceable boundary.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgments of the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit are affirmed as to exclusion of implementation costs, reversed as to nondelegation, vacated as to the agency-cure instruction, and the consolidated cases are remanded for any remaining statutory or administrative proceedings.  
Effect: EPA may not consider implementation costs when setting national ambient air-quality standards under § 109, but § 109(b)(1) is constitutionally valid. EPA may administer the congressional standard and may not replace a missing statutory boundary with a self-imposed one; no other statutory or administrative issue is resolved beyond the questions stated.
