## Matter-Specific Validation Conflicts

### Mariani v. United States

Adjudication is paused. The controlling simulated grant includes both the federal prohibition on corporate contributions and corporate spending coordinated with a candidate. Stone’s approved Section II supplies a position on direct corporate treasury contributions and reimbursement of employee contributions, then expressly reserves coordination standards as outside the holding. The official public record likewise treats coordinated-spending limits as a separate issue presented in another pending matter. Because the omitted position concerns a granted component and could change scrutiny, Stone’s formal judgment, the controlling holding, and the remedy, the Engine may not infer the missing position or silently narrow the grant. The matter remains at the post-argument merits stage. `OT_2000CHUNK3.md` `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md` `STANDING_STATE.md` ([Department of Justice](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/mariani-v-united-states-opposition))

### Shafer v. South Carolina

Adjudication is paused. Stone’s approved Section II treats future dangerousness as established and directs reversal of the death sentence for a new penalty proceeding. The authoritative record establishes that the South Carolina Supreme Court did not determine whether the prosecution’s evidence or argument actually placed future dangerousness at issue, leaving that predicate open for state-court resolution. Correcting the record would materially alter application of the *Simmons* trigger and could change Stone’s controlled remedy from an immediate new penalty proceeding to a remand for predicate determination. The Engine cannot preserve both the authoritative posture and the approved controlled remedy without a revised Section II. The matter remains at the post-argument merits stage. `OT_2000CHUNK3.md` `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md` ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/36/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Cook v. Gralike

Argued November 6, 2000 | Decided February 28, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: permanent injunction against Missouri’s congressional ballot labels affirmed, 191 F.3d 911 (8th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/510/))  
Area of Law: Elections Clause; Congressional elections; Ballot regulation

Questions Presented:
1. May Missouri place adverse labels on congressional ballots identifying candidates who refuse to support specified term-limit measures?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone in full; Scalia in full; Kennedy in full; Souter as to Parts I, II, and IV; Thomas as to Parts I and IV; Ginsburg in full; Breyer in full | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion and partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in the judgment | O’Connor | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | First Amendment ground |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | National representation and direct electoral accountability |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Joins Parts I and IV while rejecting the Court’s broader reserved-powers premise |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Outcome-directed congressional ballot labels

Holding and operative rule: A State may not use the official congressional ballot to punish candidates for refusing a policy pledge or to favor candidates who support the State’s preferred position. Such labels are outcome-directed advocacy rather than regulation of the time, place, or manner of an election. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The Elections Clause authorizes broad regulation of the procedural mechanisms by which congressional elections are conducted, including notices, registration, ballot administration, voting safeguards, and comparable neutral requirements. Missouri’s labels perform none of those functions. They single out one policy question, characterize a candidate’s position in adverse terms, and place the State’s condemnation beside the candidate’s name at the point of voting. Their design and operation favor candidates who accept Missouri’s prescribed term-limit proposal and impose political disadvantage on those who reject it or prefer another proposal. *U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill* forbids a State from using ballot regulation to impose an indirect disability or dictate electoral outcomes concerning an otherwise eligible congressional candidate. The permanent injunction is therefore sustained without deciding the independent Qualifications Clause, Article V, Speech or Debate Clause, or broader First Amendment theories.

#### Concurrences

O’Connor: Missouri’s viewpoint-based pejorative labels violate the First Amendment rights of candidates and voters because the State may not use the ballot to condemn candidates who reject its favored policy. She would affirm on that ground without joining the Court’s broader account of state power over congressional elections.

Kennedy: Senators and Representatives are accountable directly to the people who elect them, not to the States as intermediate sovereigns. Missouri may petition Congress or advocate for an amendment, but it may not condition the electoral relationship through official sanctions.

Thomas: He adheres to his disagreement with the reserved-powers premise of *U.S. Term Limits*, but the ballot labels still fall outside permissible election administration. He therefore joins the dispositive Elections Clause analysis and the judgment.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill*: applied and clarified; the prohibition on additional state-imposed qualifications also bars official-ballot devices designed to favor or disfavor congressional candidates according to policy compliance.  
*Anderson v. Martin*: applied; the State may not use the ballot to direct voters’ attention to a state-selected characteristic or position in a manner calculated to influence the electoral choice. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed, and the permanent injunction against enforcement of Missouri’s adverse congressional ballot labels remains in force.  
Effect: Missouri may not place the challenged pledge and noncompliance labels beside congressional candidates’ names. Neutral designations concerning party, office, incumbency, filing status, and ordinary ballot administration remain available.

### Legal Services Corp. v. Velazquez / United States v. Velazquez

Argued October 4, 2000 | Decided February 28, 2001  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: injunction entered against enforcement of the welfare-law advocacy restriction, 164 F.3d 757 (2d Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/533/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Viewpoint discrimination; Government-funded legal representation

Questions Presented:
1. May Congress fund adversarial legal representation while prohibiting funded lawyers from challenging existing welfare law?

#### Judgment

Affirmed in both consolidated matters, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm in both consolidated matters.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Viewpoint restriction within funded adversarial representation

Holding and operative rule: When the Government funds independent lawyers to represent private clients in adversarial proceedings, it may define eligible clients and subject matters through neutral rules, but it may not prohibit a funded lawyer from presenting a nonfrivolous challenge to existing law because the Government disfavors that legal position.

Reasoning: An LSC lawyer appearing in court does not deliver a governmental message. The lawyer represents the client, exercises independent professional judgment, and participates in an adjudicative process whose function depends on the presentation of competing legal arguments. Congress may choose the population served, the categories of cases funded, and the accounting conditions attached to federal money. It may not, after creating that independent representation, require the lawyer to omit a statutory or constitutional argument solely because the argument challenges the validity of existing welfare law. The restriction distorts the medium Congress chose, disadvantages one viewpoint within the funded representation, and impairs the courts’ ability to receive complete legal analysis. The injunction is therefore affirmed. Neutral restrictions addressing eligibility, subject matter, competence, conflicts, accounting, and misuse of funds remain available, and the judgment creates no governmental obligation to fund every client or claim.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: Congress may define the scope of a publicly financed legal-assistance program and decline to subsidize efforts to change or invalidate the law the program administers. They would treat the restriction as a permissible definition of the funded activity rather than viewpoint discrimination within a forum for private speech.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: clarified and extended; governmental discretion over direct funding does not authorize viewpoint discrimination within independent adversarial representation the Government has chosen to fund.  
*Rust v. Sullivan*: distinguished; LSC attorneys speak for private clients in court rather than transmitting a governmental program message.  
*National Endowment for the Arts v. Finley*: distinguished; discretionary funding criteria do not authorize suppression of one legal position within representation already made eligible for support.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed in both consolidated matters, and the injunction against the viewpoint-based advocacy restriction remains in force.  
Effect: LSC-funded lawyers representing eligible clients may present nonfrivolous statutory and constitutional challenges to existing welfare law. Congress and LSC retain authority to administer neutral client, case, competence, conflict, accounting, and program-integrity requirements.

### Summary Reversal: Ohio v. Reiner, 532 U.S. 17 (2001), March 19, 2001

Acting Justice or Court: The Court.  
Public action: The petition for a writ of certiorari is granted, the judgment of the Supreme Court of Ohio is reversed, and the case is remanded.  
Publicly stated basis: A witness does not lose the Fifth Amendment privilege by professing innocence. The privilege applies when truthful answers present a real and appreciable danger of furnishing evidence or a link in the chain of evidence for a possible prosecution. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/17/))  
Effect on judgment below or existing order: The Ohio court’s conclusion that the babysitter’s assertion of innocence categorically defeated her privilege is set aside. The Court does not decide whether the particular grant of immunity was otherwise valid or what ultimate consequence follows for Reiner’s conviction.  
Next procedural stage: The Supreme Court of Ohio shall reconsider the immunity, testimony, prejudice, and conviction issues under the correct Fifth Amendment rule.

Controlling proposition: A witness who maintains innocence may invoke the privilege against self-incrimination when truthful answers create a real and appreciable risk of furnishing evidence for a possible prosecution.

Authority limit: A remote, speculative, imaginary, or legally impossible risk remains insufficient. The summary disposition does not decide the scope or validity of immunity under Ohio law.

Doctrinal Effect: *Hoffman v. United States*, 341 U.S. 479 (1951), followed; entitlement to the privilege turns on reasonable cause to apprehend incrimination, not on a profession of innocence.

### TrafFix Devices, Inc. v. Marketing Displays, Inc.

Argued November 29, 2000 | Decided March 20, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment denying trade-dress protection reversed, 200 F.3d 929 (6th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/23/))  
Area of Law: Trademark; Trade dress; Product functionality

Questions Presented:
1. What evidentiary effect does an expired utility patent have when the patented product feature is asserted as protectable trade dress?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Functionality shown by an expired utility patent

Holding and operative rule: A utility patent that claims and explains a product feature is strong evidence that the feature is functional. When the patent materials establish that the feature is essential to the product’s use or purpose, the feature cannot acquire trade-dress protection merely because alternative designs exist or consumers associate it with one producer.

Reasoning: Trademark law protects source identification, not perpetual control over useful product features after patent rights expire. The expired patents claimed the dual-spring mechanism and explained that it allowed the sign stand to resist wind while remaining upright. That evidence identifies the mechanism as the product’s central utilitarian advance and establishes functionality under the traditional inquiry. Once functionality is shown on that basis, evidence of secondary meaning cannot create protection, because consumer recognition does not convert a useful mechanism into a trademark. Nor must a competitor prove that no alternative design could perform the same task. Alternative-design evidence may matter when functionality turns on competitive necessity, but it cannot defeat proof that the asserted feature is itself essential to use or purpose. Any distinctly pleaded, separable, nonfunctional source-identifying feature remains available for ordinary Lanham Act adjudication.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co.*: clarified; alternative-design evidence does not defeat functionality when the asserted feature is essential to the article’s use or purpose.  
*Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.*: followed; product-design protection remains dependent on nonfunctionality as well as any required showing of source significance.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to treat the patented dual-spring mechanism as functional and to address only any distinctly pleaded, separable nonfunctional features.  
Effect: Marketing Displays may not obtain trade-dress control over the useful dual-spring mechanism after expiration of its utility patents. Names, logos, packaging, surface decoration, and other separable nonfunctional indicia remain governed by ordinary Lanham Act rules.

### Circuit City Stores, Inc. v. Adams

Argued November 6, 2000 | Decided March 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: order compelling arbitration reversed under a general employment-contract exemption, 194 F.3d 1070 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/105/))  
Area of Law: Arbitration; Statutory interpretation; Employment contracts

Questions Presented:
1. Does FAA § 1 exclude employment contracts generally or only employment contracts involving transportation workers?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter as to Parts II and III; Ginsburg and Breyer in full | Would affirm | Complete opinion and partial join as stated |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Scope of the FAA employment exemption

Holding and operative rule: FAA § 1 exempts employment contracts of transportation workers, not employment contracts generally. A retail employee’s arbitration agreement therefore falls within the FAA, subject to generally applicable contract defenses and any properly preserved effective-vindication objection.

Reasoning: Section 1 excludes contracts of employment of seamen, railroad employees, and any other class of workers engaged in foreign or interstate commerce. The residual phrase is read in context with the two specifically named transportation categories. It covers workers whose occupations involve moving goods or passengers through interstate or foreign commerce, rather than every employee whose work affects commerce in the broader constitutional sense. That interpretation also respects the difference between § 1’s narrower phrase and § 2’s broad reference to transactions involving commerce. Adams worked for an electronics retailer and is not alleged to have belonged to a transportation-worker class. The FAA therefore governs the agreement. Enforcement remains subject to neutral doctrines concerning formation, assent, fraud, duress, unconscionability, severability, and practical forum availability. The Court does not decide any independent-contractor classification or the validity of any particular disputed term not resolved below.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer, and by Souter as to Parts II and III: The statutory text and enactment-era understanding of commerce indicate that Congress excluded employment contracts generally. He would not use the neighboring occupations to narrow language that Congress employed against the broader historical background of employment disputes.

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The statutory history and the original limits of the FAA support a general employment-contract exemption. He would leave any expansion of federal arbitration coverage to Congress.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson*: clarified; § 2’s broad commerce coverage does not determine the narrower occupational exemption stated in § 1.  
*Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto*: applied; agreements within the FAA remain subject to generally applicable contract defenses but not arbitration-specific hostility.  
*Green Tree Financial Corp.-Alabama v. Randolph*, decided December 11, 2000: followed; a preserved prohibitive-cost objection requires a concrete showing of practical unavailability rather than categorical nonenforcement.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for enforcement proceedings under the FAA, subject to generally applicable contract defenses and any preserved effective-vindication objection.  
Effect: Adams’s retail employment does not place his agreement within § 1’s transportation-worker exemption. The decision does not determine the validity of every agreement term, classify every independent contractor, or require arbitration beyond the FAA’s enacted coverage.

### Egelhoff v. Egelhoff

Argued November 8, 2000 | Decided March 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Washington; judgment below: state divorce-revocation statute applied to ERISA plan benefits, 139 Wash. 2d 557, 989 P.2d 80 (1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/141/))  
Area of Law: ERISA; Federal preemption; Plan administration

Questions Presented:
1. Does ERISA preempt a state statute automatically revoking an ex-spouse’s beneficiary status upon divorce?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Textual basis for ERISA preemption |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: State alteration of the plan-designated beneficiary

Holding and operative rule: ERISA preempts a state law that automatically changes the beneficiary identified in governing plan documents and requires plan administrators to apply state-specific succession rules when paying benefits.

Reasoning: The Washington statute does more than influence a plan through background economic regulation. It directly determines who receives benefits and displaces the beneficiary named in the documents governing the plan. ERISA requires plans to specify the basis for payment and directs fiduciaries to administer them in accordance with their governing instruments. A state rule that automatically revises beneficiary status upon divorce requires administrators to investigate state family and succession law, resolve potential choice-of-law questions, and risk liability for paying either the named beneficiary or the person state law prefers. Those duties interfere with nationally uniform plan administration and concern a core ERISA function. The statute is therefore preempted as applied to plan payment. The judgment does not decide a qualified domestic-relations order, a valid plan amendment, or an independently authorized claim asserted after the administrator has properly distributed benefits according to the plan documents.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Ginsburg: The Washington rule is preempted under ERISA’s enacted language because it has a direct connection with plan administration and beneficiary payment. They would rely more directly on that text without adding a broader presumption against preemption.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens: State rules governing the effect of divorce on succession interests lie in an area of traditional state authority and do not alter the plan’s internal terms or funding. Because Washington permits plans to opt out and protects administrators lacking notice, they would find no sufficient interference with national administration.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers Insurance Co.*: clarified; indirect economic influence on a plan differs from a state rule that directly dictates beneficiary payment and plan administration.  
*UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward*: distinguished; a generally applicable insurance rule preserved through ERISA’s saving clause does not establish state authority to replace the beneficiary identified by an ERISA plan.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Washington is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings requiring payment and adjudication consistent with the governing ERISA plan documents.  
Effect: The Washington automatic divorce-revocation rule may not determine the plan administrator’s beneficiary payment. Qualified domestic-relations orders, valid plan amendments, and independently authorized post-distribution claims remain separately governed.

### Ferguson v. City of Charleston

Argued October 4, 2000 | Decided March 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: public-hospital drug-testing program upheld under the special-needs doctrine, 186 F.3d 469 (4th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/67/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Special needs; Medical testing

Questions Presented:
1. May a public hospital conduct nonconsensual diagnostic drug tests of pregnant patients and disclose positive results to police under the special-needs doctrine?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Direct police integration and coercive operation |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas as to Part II | Would affirm | Complete dissent and partial join as stated |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Police-integrated medical testing

Holding and operative rule: A public hospital may not rely on the special-needs doctrine to conduct nonconsensual diagnostic drug tests and disclose the results to police when the program’s primary purpose is materially integrated with criminal investigation and prosecution. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Collection and analysis of a patient’s urine for evidence constitutes a search. A medical setting does not by itself remove the ordinary Fourth Amendment requirement, and reduced intrusiveness cannot establish a special need. The challenged policy was jointly developed with police and prosecutors, used law-enforcement chains of custody, established criteria for arrest and prosecution, transmitted positive results for criminal use, and employed threatened prosecution to obtain compliance. Those operational features show that the immediate programmatic objective was not independent diagnosis, treatment, or nonprosecutorial public health, but acquisition of evidence for ordinary law enforcement. Under *City of Indianapolis v. Edmond*, decided November 28, 2000, that purpose cannot justify suspicionless searches. The Fourth Circuit did not resolve valid consent, and the lower courts may address that question, along with any independently authorized medical, public-health, or child-protection obligation, on remand.

#### Concurrences

Kennedy: The direct participation of police and prosecutors, the prescribed arrest criteria, and the threatened criminal consequences make this program indistinguishable from evidence gathering for law enforcement. He would avoid treating every medical test that later produces reportable evidence, or every limited cooperation with police, as constitutionally identical.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas as to Part II: The hospital obtained information through diagnostic procedures undertaken in a medical setting and could lawfully share evidence of crime with police. They would not make the Government’s additional law-enforcement objective dispositive where the testing also served treatment and protection interests, and they would affirm absent proof that the patients withheld consent.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Indianapolis v. Edmond*, decided November 28, 2000: applied and extended; a program designed principally to obtain evidence for ordinary criminal enforcement cannot invoke the special-needs exception merely because medical personnel conduct the search.  
*Chandler v. Miller*: applied; a concrete need beyond ordinary law enforcement is required before individualized suspicion may be dispensed with.  
*Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton*: distinguished; school athletic testing served a distinct custodial and safety function and was not integrated with prosecution.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for consideration of genuine consent and any independently authorized nonprosecutorial medical, public-health, or child-protection duty.  
Effect: The challenged police-integrated testing policy cannot be sustained as a special-needs program. Medically indicated testing, valid consent, routine treatment, genuine public-health surveillance, and independently authorized reporting duties remain governed by their own legal sources.

### Brewer v. West Irondequoit Central School District

Argued February 21, 2001 | Decided March 26, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: mandatory preliminary injunction vacated and case remanded for trial, 212 F.3d 738 (2d Cir. 2000). ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-2nd-circuit/1014190.html))  
Area of Law: Equal Protection; Public education; Race-conscious student assignment

Questions Presented:
1. May a public school system use an express racial classification to promote voluntary interdistrict integration without identifying a constitutional violation that it is remedying?
2. If voluntary integration is a compelling interest, what individualized consideration, duration, numerical limits, and race-neutral alternatives does narrow tailoring require?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Race as the eligibility gate for a particular transfer seat

Holding and operative rule: When an identified student’s race determines whether the student may receive a particular school-transfer opportunity, the classification is an individual racial allocation subject to strict scrutiny. A voluntary integration objective does not convert that allocation into a merely structural measure.

Reasoning: The program allowed minority Rochester students to seek suburban placements and nonminority suburban students to transfer into Rochester. Brewer’s child was accepted for a suburban placement and then declared ineligible because she was a white Rochester resident. Race therefore did not operate only in the design of an inclusionary districtwide arrangement; it determined an identified student’s eligibility for a particular educational opportunity. *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*, decided June 20, 2000, draws the controlling boundary. A genuinely structural measure directed to meaningful interracial participation may receive the demanding review stated there, but a rule that allocates a discrete seat by an individual’s race remains subject to strict scrutiny. The Court does not foreclose voluntary integration, race-neutral transfer incentives, outreach, location decisions, attendance-zone changes, or other access-expanding measures.

##### Question 2: Narrow tailoring of the eligibility rule

Holding and operative rule: Even assuming that preventing serious racial isolation supplies a compelling interest, a categorical racial eligibility gate fails strict scrutiny when the government has not shown that making race dispositive is necessary, has not adequately addressed workable race-neutral or less restrictive alternatives, and uses predetermined racial direction or proportionality as the operative measure of access.

Reasoning: The asserted educational objective is serious, but the means must be justified independently. The record shows that the program made a student’s racial category dispositive without an individualized assessment of how the particular transfer would advance integration, without a demonstrated necessity for the categorical exclusion, and without an adequate showing that workable race-neutral or less restrictive measures could not preserve the program’s educational benefits. *Eisenberg* also establishes that educational integration is constitutionally distinct from racial balance for its own sake. A jurisdiction may examine enrollment conditions and design bounded measures that expand interracial participation, but it may not define success through predetermined racial numbers and then use those numbers to deny an identified student a seat. Because the Second Circuit evaluated the preliminary injunction without applying the controlling boundary now supplied by *Eisenberg*, its judgment is reversed and the matter returned for reconsideration of preliminary and final relief on the existing record and any lawfully developed evidence.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The interdistrict program is a long-standing inclusionary measure designed to reduce racial isolation by expanding access across district lines, not a racial entitlement to a particular school. They would apply the structural-integration rule recognized in *Eisenberg*, affirm the Second Circuit’s vacatur of the mandatory preliminary injunction, and permit full merits development.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*, decided June 20, 2000: followed; race-determinative allocation of an identified school seat receives strict scrutiny, while genuinely structural and access-expanding integration measures remain distinct.  
*Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*: applied; an individual governmental benefit allocated according to race must satisfy strict scrutiny.  
*Missouri v. Jenkins*: followed; racial balance or proportional racial numbers cannot serve as an independent governmental end.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for reconsideration of preliminary and final relief under strict scrutiny and the individual-allocation boundary established in *Eisenberg*.  
Effect: The race-determinative eligibility restriction may not be enforced against Brewer’s child absent the constitutionally required showing. The lower courts may reconsider preliminary relief on the existing record and any lawfully updated evidence, while race-neutral and genuinely structural integration measures remain available.

### Mariani v. United States

The problem is primarily question-scope omission.

Standing State says the simulated grant contains two components:

1. scrutiny for the prohibition on corporate contributions and corporate spending coordinated with a candidate; and
2. whether soft-money, independent-expenditure, or other lawful channels make the prohibition underinclusive or insufficiently tailored.

But the current brief narrows the case to direct corporate treasury contributions and employee-reimbursement/conduit contributions. It then expressly reserves coordination. That is not allowed after grant. The template requires every granted component to receive a Stone position, and materially distinct issues must be separated if they can produce different legal answers or scrutiny.

There is a second problem: the current Section I itself uses the wrong granted question. It says the grant is essentially the narrower direct-contribution/reimbursement question, while Standing State expressly includes coordinated corporate spending.

The good news is that Stone does not need to change his overall disposition. He can still affirm the rejection of the facial challenge.

The existing simulated law already provides most of the bridge. The Stone Court's earlier *Colorado Republican* decision protects genuinely independent spending and says shared political interests alone are not coordination. It requires some operative connection such as coordination, agreement, candidate control, or a relationship showing candidate-controlled value or contribution circumvention. Critically, that earlier case expressly left actual coordinated spending unresolved. Contribution regulation, meanwhile, receives closely drawn review tied to objectively grounded quid-pro-quo corruption or circumvention, not equalization of political influence.

### Fix for Mariani

Do not change Stone's direct-contribution analysis. Add a separate coordinated-spending issue.

The clean Stone position is:

> Corporate spending undertaken at a candidate's request, direction, control, consultation, or other material participation may be treated as contribution-like support because the corporation is furnishing value into the candidate's campaign rather than engaging in genuinely independent advocacy. A prohibition on corporate treasury funding of that genuinely coordinated support is reviewed under contribution-style, closely drawn scrutiny and may be sustained to prevent actual or apparent quid-pro-quo corruption and circumvention of the direct corporate-contribution prohibition. Mere ideological agreement, parallel advocacy, use of public information, or support for the same candidate does not establish coordination.

That resolves the missing granted component without swallowing independent corporate speech.

Then the underinclusiveness issue should cover both categories:

> The existence of lawful independent expenditures, soft-money channels, segregated political funds, voluntary natural-person contributions, press activity, or other lawful advocacy does not make the direct-contribution and genuine-coordination prohibitions fatally underinclusive because those channels do not place corporate treasury value under candidate control in the same manner.

Stone should expressly reserve only genuinely independent expenditures and marginal applications where actual coordination has not been proved. He cannot reserve "coordination standards" wholesale because coordination is part of the grant.

The formal result can therefore stay:

**Affirm.** No separate remedy required.

This also has a chronology consequence. Because *Mariani* is scheduled for March 12, 2001, while *FEC v. Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee* is scheduled for June 25, 2001, *Mariani* would become the earlier same-term authority on the contribution-like treatment of proven coordination. The later Colorado Republican case would have to be revalidated against *Mariani*, not the other way around.

### Shafer v. South Carolina

This is a record-premise and remedy problem.

The current brief states as a fact that "future dangerousness was placed at issue" and then uses that asserted fact to order a new penalty proceeding. The reconstruction does the same thing.

But the authoritative posture leaves that predicate unresolved. The South Carolina Supreme Court had not determined whether the prosecution's actual evidence or argument triggered *Simmons*. Therefore Stone cannot make that factual determination merely because his ultimate constitutional rule is clear.

This is exactly what the template forbids: if Stone's result requires a factual finding the record does not supply, he must frame the conclusion conditionally or remand for lawful factfinding. It also matches Stone's jurisprudence: when the record cannot support a necessary finding, he remands instead of supplying it.

The legal holding itself is fine. Stone can decide that after the aggravating circumstance is found, South Carolina's sentencing structure leaves death and life without parole as the relevant choices, so *Simmons* applies if future dangerousness was placed at issue.

What he cannot decide is the unresolved "if."

### Fix for Shafer

Keep the legal rule. Change the application and remedy.

The corrected controlled disposition should be:

**Reverse and remand**, but do not immediately vacate the death sentence and order a new penalty proceeding.

The remand should say:

> The South Carolina Supreme Court shall determine in the first instance whether the prosecution's evidence or argument placed Shafer's future dangerousness at issue. If it did, the court shall apply *Simmons* on the understanding that, after the aggravating circumstance was found, life without parole was the sole noncapital sentencing alternative and Shafer was entitled to an accurate opportunity to communicate parole ineligibility. If future dangerousness was not placed at issue, *Simmons* supplies no basis for disturbing the sentence on this ground.

Stone's proposed holding becomes:

> Once the capital jury finds the aggravating circumstance that narrows South Carolina's lawful sentencing choices to death and life without parole, *Simmons* governs if the State places future dangerousness at issue. Whether the State did so in Shafer's sentencing proceeding remains for the South Carolina Supreme Court to determine on remand.

And the reasoning should explicitly say:

> The Court resolves the legal sequencing question but does not make the unresolved factual or record-sensitive determination whether the prosecution's presentation actually invoked future dangerousness.

That is the surgical fix. Stone's underlying *Simmons* position does not change. The only thing removed is the unsupported factual finding and the remedy that depended on it.

It also improves the later *Kelly v. South Carolina* architecture. The later Kelly materials already treat *Shafer* as resolving the sentencing-choice question while leaving the future-dangerousness predicate open, which Kelly then resolves on its much clearer evidence-and-argument record.

So the lineage becomes very clean:

**Simmons:** future dangerousness + life without parole triggers accurate parole information.

**Shafer:** the relevant sentencing alternatives are measured at the jury's actual selection stage; remand because whether dangerousness was invoked remains unresolved.

**Kelly:** explains how courts determine that dangerousness was actually invoked, including through the functional operation of the State's evidence and argument rather than magic words.

That is better than making *Shafer* prematurely do *Kelly*'s job.
