## Decisions and Dispositions

### Mariani v. United States

Argued February 20, 2001 | Decided March 12, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: facial First Amendment challenge rejected, 212 F.3d 761 (3d Cir. 2000). ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/212/761/632624/?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [justice.gov](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/mariani-v-united-states-opposition?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [fec.gov](https://www.fec.gov/legal-resources/court-cases/renato-p-mariani-v-usa/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Campaign finance; Corporate contributions and coordination

Questions Presented:
1. What level of First Amendment scrutiny governs the federal prohibition on corporate contributions and corporate spending coordinated with a candidate?
2. Does the availability of soft-money, independent-expenditure, or other lawful channels render the prohibition fatally underinclusive or insufficiently tailored to anticorruption interests?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Direct corporate treasury contributions | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The facial challenge to the direct corporate-contribution prohibition is rejected. |
| Reimbursement and conduit contributions | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The prohibition on contributions made in another person’s name remains enforceable. |
| Genuinely coordinated corporate treasury spending | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The prohibition remains enforceable when the required candidate connection is proved. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone and Stevens in full; Scalia as to the conduit-contribution holding; Kennedy as to the direct-contribution and conduit-contribution holdings and the corresponding underinclusiveness analysis; Souter in full; Thomas as to the conduit-contribution holding; Ginsburg and Breyer in full | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion and partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Supports the direct-contribution and conduit holdings but would require a narrower definition of coordinated spending |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Supports the conduit holding but would invalidate the direct corporate-contribution and coordinated-spending prohibitions |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm every judgment component.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Direct corporate treasury contributions

Holding and operative rule: A prohibition on direct corporate treasury contributions to candidates is reviewed as a contribution restriction and satisfies closely drawn review when fitted to objectively grounded risks of quid-pro-quo corruption, its appearance, and circumvention of contribution limits. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: A direct treasury payment transfers corporate value into the candidate’s campaign and places that value under candidate control. Congress may distinguish that transaction from independent advocacy and may account for the use of the corporate form to aggregate funds, obscure the natural persons supplying political support, or evade limits otherwise applicable to those persons. The prohibition does not rest on equalizing influence or suppressing corporate viewpoints. It addresses the contribution transaction itself while leaving corporations able to speak through genuinely independent advocacy and to establish a lawfully regulated segregated political fund. Closely drawn review therefore governs, and the facial challenge fails because the prohibition targets a recognized corruption and circumvention risk without disabling corporate political expression generally.

##### Question 2: Reimbursement and conduit contributions

Holding and operative rule: Congress may prohibit a corporation or its agents from reimbursing employees or other nominal contributors whose names are used to conceal the true source of candidate contributions. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: A reimbursed contribution is not the voluntary political contribution of the named individual. It is a corporate payment routed through an intermediary to evade the direct-contribution prohibition, source limits, and disclosure requirements. The false attribution prevents regulators, candidates, and the public from identifying the actual source and permits a corporation to multiply contributions through nominal donors. Section 441f therefore directly serves the integrity of valid contribution and disclosure rules. Its application does not depend on whether the corporation could have used another lawful channel to engage in political advocacy.

##### Question 3: Corporate spending coordinated with a candidate

Holding and operative rule: Corporate treasury spending undertaken at a candidate’s request, direction, control, consultation, or other material participation may be treated as contribution-like support and subjected to closely drawn regulation. Mere ideological agreement, parallel advocacy, use of public information, or support for the same candidate does not establish coordination.

Reasoning: The constitutional distinction turns on the operative connection between the spending and the candidate, not on the speaker’s corporate identity or political alignment. When a candidate requests, directs, controls, or materially participates in the spending decision, the corporation furnishes campaign value in a manner functionally comparable to a direct transfer. Regulation may then address quid-pro-quo corruption, its appearance, and circumvention of the direct corporate-contribution prohibition. The same reasoning does not reach genuinely independent advocacy. Shared objectives, common consultants without evidence of campaign involvement, public statements, or parallel timing do not by themselves show that the candidate controlled or participated in the expenditure. The Government retains the burden of proving the legally sufficient connection required by the prohibition.

##### Question 4: Lawful alternative channels and underinclusiveness

Holding and operative rule: The continued availability of independent expenditures, soft-money activity, segregated political funds, voluntary natural-person contributions, press activity, and other lawful advocacy does not make the direct-contribution, conduit, and genuine-coordination prohibitions facially underinclusive.

Reasoning: The Constitution does not require Congress to regulate every form of political finance identically. The prohibited transactions place corporate treasury value under candidate control, conceal its source, or furnish campaign support through actual coordination. The lawful channels identified by Mariani do not necessarily create those same operative risks. Independent advocacy remains controlled by the speaker, segregated funds use voluntarily contributed political money under separate rules, and natural persons remain accountable for their own contributions. Congress may address the transaction presenting the identified corruption or circumvention concern without suppressing materially different activity merely to make the statutory scheme more comprehensive.

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, concurring in part and dissenting in part: He agrees that direct corporate contributions and conduit reimbursements may be prohibited. He would treat spending as contribution-like only upon proof of candidate control, express agreement, or another comparably definite transfer relationship, and would not permit consultation or material participation alone to carry the Government’s burden.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that contributions falsely made in another person’s name may be prohibited. They would apply strict scrutiny to the categorical corporate treasury restrictions and hold that the Government has not justified either the direct-contribution ban or the broader coordination prohibition.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Buckley v. Valeo*: clarified; contribution-style review governs direct corporate transfers and proven candidate-coordinated treasury support, while the governmental interest remains limited to corruption, its appearance, and circumvention rather than equalization.  
*Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission*, decided June 26, 1996: clarified; actual coordination is now contribution-like when the candidate requests, directs, controls, consults on, or materially participates in the spending, but organizational identity, shared goals, and parallel advocacy remain insufficient.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit rejecting the facial challenge is affirmed.  
Effect: The federal prohibitions on direct corporate treasury contributions, reimbursed conduit contributions, and genuinely coordinated corporate treasury support remain enforceable within the limits stated by the Court. Genuinely independent corporate advocacy, press activity, and applications lacking proof of the required candidate connection remain outside the coordinated-spending holding.

### Shafer v. South Carolina

Argued January 9, 2001 | Decided March 20, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of South Carolina; judgment below: death sentence affirmed under South Carolina’s revised capital-sentencing scheme, 340 S.C. 291, 531 S.E.2d 524 (2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/36/))  
Area of Law: Due process; Capital sentencing; Parole ineligibility

Questions Presented:
1. Does *Simmons* require accurate parole-ineligibility information when, after an aggravating-circumstance finding, the jury’s only punishment choices are death and life without parole and future dangerousness is at issue?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would affirm | Rejects the controlling *Simmons* rule |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would affirm | Treats the instructions given as constitutionally sufficient |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand for determination of the unresolved future-dangerousness predicate.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Parole ineligibility under South Carolina’s sentencing sequence

Holding and operative rule: Once the capital jury finds the aggravating circumstance that narrows South Carolina’s lawful sentencing choices to death and life without parole, *Simmons* governs if the State places future dangerousness at issue. Whether the State’s evidence or argument did so in Shafer’s proceeding remains for the Supreme Court of South Carolina to determine on remand.

Reasoning: The relevant sentencing alternatives are those available when the jury makes the actual moral and legal choice between death and the noncapital sentence. Before an aggravating circumstance is found, South Carolina law may contemplate an additional sentencing route for the judge. After the jury finds the aggravator, however, that third route disappears and the jury chooses only between death and life without parole. At that stage, a State that relies on the defendant’s future dangerousness may not leave the jury with a materially false impression that a life sentence permits release. The Supreme Court of South Carolina rejected *Simmons* categorically without determining whether the prosecution’s actual evidence or argument invoked future dangerousness. This Court resolves the legal sequencing question but may not make that unresolved record-sensitive finding itself.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia: He continues to reject the due-process rule announced in *Simmons* and would affirm rather than extend it to South Carolina’s revised sentencing sequence.

Thomas: Even assuming *Simmons* applies after the aggravating-circumstance finding, he concludes that the trial judge’s explanation that life imprisonment lasts until the defendant’s death adequately communicated the relevant consequence.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Simmons v. South Carolina*: clarified; the available sentencing alternatives are measured at the jury’s actual penalty-selection stage, while the requirement of an accurate parole-ineligibility instruction remains conditioned on the State’s placing future dangerousness at issue.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of South Carolina is reversed and the case is remanded for that court to determine whether the prosecution’s evidence or argument placed Shafer’s future dangerousness at issue and, if so, to grant relief consistent with *Simmons*.  
Effect: Shafer’s conviction remains undisturbed, and this Court does not itself order a new penalty proceeding. If future dangerousness was placed at issue, the state court must set aside the sentence and provide a constitutionally adequate remedy; if it was not, *Simmons* supplies no basis for disturbing the sentence on this ground.

### Texas v. Cobb

Argued January 16, 2001 | Decided April 2, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Texas Court of Criminal Appeals; judgment below: capital-murder conviction reversed and matter remanded under a factually interwoven-offense rule, 2000 WL 275644. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/162/))  
Area of Law: Sixth Amendment; Right to counsel; Police interrogation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Sixth Amendment right to counsel extends to a distinct uncharged offense arising from the same criminal episode when police question a represented defendant, and whether Cobb’s police-initiated waiver was valid.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Questions the continuing basis of the separate police-initiation rule while joining the Court |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Would extend the counsel protection to factually inseparable offenses and evidence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: the Sixth Amendment right is offense specific under the same-elements rule; factual overlap does not attach the right to a distinct uncharged offense, although deliberate elicitation and use of statements concerning the represented charge remain prohibited.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Scope of the offense-specific counsel right

Holding and operative rule: The Sixth Amendment right to counsel attaches to the charged offense and any other offense that is the same under the same-elements rule. It does not attach to a distinct uncharged offense merely because the offenses arose from one criminal episode or their facts overlap.

Reasoning: The Sixth Amendment protects the accused in an existing criminal prosecution. The same-elements rule supplies an administrable boundary that includes an uncharged offense only when it is legally the same offense as the charged crime. Cobb had been charged with burglary, but the murder offenses required materially different elements and had not yet entered an adversarial prosecution. The right therefore did not categorically bar questioning about the murders. *Moulton* remains fully operative as to the charged burglary: police may not use an investigation of other crimes as a device to deliberately elicit and use statements concerning the represented prosecution. The state court must accordingly distinguish evidence concerning the charged burglary from the separate murder confession rather than suppress the entire interview solely because the crimes were factually interwoven.

##### Question 2: Police-initiated waiver

Not reached; the Sixth Amendment right had not attached to the distinct murder offenses, and any charged-burglary statements and their legally attributable consequences remain for ordinary *Moulton*, harmlessness, and remedial analysis on remand.

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: The offense-specific holding is sufficient to reverse. He emphasizes that the Court does not reaffirm any broader rule automatically invalidating a represented defendant’s otherwise voluntary decision to speak after proper warnings.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The charged burglary and murders formed one factually inseparable criminal episode, and the police questioning foreseeably obtained evidence concerning the represented prosecution. They would affirm the state court’s application of the Sixth Amendment’s anti-circumvention principle.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*McNeil v. Wisconsin*, 501 U.S. 171 (1991): followed; the Sixth Amendment right to counsel remains offense specific.  
*Maine v. Moulton*, 474 U.S. 159 (1985): clarified; the anti-circumvention rule bars deliberate elicitation and use of statements concerning the represented charge but does not attach the right to every factually related offense.  
*Blockburger v. United States*, 284 U.S. 299 (1932): applied; the same-elements inquiry defines whether charged and uncharged crimes are the same offense for Sixth Amendment purposes.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Texas Court of Criminal Appeals is reversed, and the case is remanded for separation of statements concerning the represented burglary from statements concerning the distinct murders, followed by ordinary harmlessness and remedial analysis.  
Effect: Cobb’s murder confession may not be suppressed categorically merely because counsel represented him on the burglary charge. Statements deliberately elicited concerning the represented burglary, and any legally attributable consequences of their use, remain subject to the Sixth Amendment.

### Denius v. Dunlap

Argued February 27, 2001 | Decided April 2, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: individual damages claims resolved on a claim-specific qualified-immunity basis and equitable claims remanded, 209 F.3d 944 (7th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/209/944/474129/))  
Area of Law: Due process; Informational privacy; Public employment

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a public employer may condition employment on an authorization permitting acquisition of medical and comparably intimate information.
2. Whether other confidential-record theories receive the same constitutional treatment and whether individual damages are available.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Medical-information theory and related equitable proceedings | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The medical-privacy theory may proceed if the authorization is found to reach medical records or communications. |
| Individual damages on financial and attorney-client theories | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Dunlap retains qualified immunity on those damages theories. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone and Stevens in full; Scalia as to Question 2; Kennedy and Souter in full; Thomas as to Question 2; Ginsburg and Breyer in full | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion and partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees with qualified immunity on the nonmedical theories but rejects the substantive medical-privacy right |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the Seventh Circuit’s claim-specific judgment.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Compelled access to medical and intimate information

Holding and operative rule: A public employer may not condition employment on unlimited governmental access to medical or comparably intimate records without a genuine and proportionate connection between the information demanded and the governmental function. A signature required as a condition of employment does not itself establish constitutionally sufficient consent.

Reasoning: The limited informational-privacy protection recognized in *Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority* applies when government compels authorization for identified medical information as well as when officials later examine, use, or disclose it. Relevant considerations include the information’s sensitivity, the actual governmental need, the authorization’s scope and duration, the persons receiving access, permissible uses, confidentiality safeguards, and whether narrower information would serve the same function. Here the stated objective was a criminal-background check, and Denius had already authorized access to criminal-history information. The later authorization demanded substantially broader access without an identified limiting purpose or safeguard. The present record does not establish whether its language in fact reached medical records or communications. That factual issue remains for the district court, and the medical theory proceeds only if the authorization had that reach.

##### Question 2: Other records and qualified immunity

Holding and operative rule: Qualified immunity is claim specific. The complete body of law existing when Dunlap acted did not clearly establish personal damages liability under the broader financial-information and attorney-client theories, and not every record denominated confidential receives categorical constitutional protection. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Medical information occupies a distinct position because existing law recognized its exceptional sensitivity and limited governmental access to it. The law was materially less settled concerning ordinary financial information and the constitutional consequences of requiring a broad attorney-record authorization outside an existing criminal prosecution. Common-law privilege, First Amendment access to counsel, Sixth Amendment attachment, and Fourteenth Amendment confidentiality also involve distinct sources and triggers. A reasonable official therefore lacked sufficiently clear notice that the authorization created personal damages liability on those theories. Prospective claims are not defeated by qualified immunity, but their merits must be addressed under the source-specific rules governing the particular information and asserted right.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that qualified immunity bars the financial and attorney-client damages theories. They reject a substantive-due-process right governing governmental acquisition of medical information and would reverse the medical component as well.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority*, decided May 5, 1997: extended; limited informational-privacy protection governs compelled authorization for governmental acquisition of identified medical information as a condition of public employment.  
*Elder v. Holloway*, 510 U.S. 510 (1994): applied; qualified immunity is determined claim by claim from the complete body of materially relevant law existing at the time of the conduct.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed, and the district court shall determine whether the authorization reached medical records or communications before adjudicating the medical-privacy theory.  
Effect: Dunlap is not immune from the medical damages claim if the authorization is found to have covered medical information and the remaining elements are proved. Individual damages are unavailable on the financial and attorney-client theories, while properly preserved prospective claims remain subject to their independent legal sources.

### Kansas v. United States

Argued February 28, 2001 | Decided April 16, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the Spending Clause challenge for failure to state a claim affirmed, 214 F.3d 1196 (10th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/214/1196/533464/))  
Area of Law: Spending Clause; Federalism; Coercion

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the challenged conditions were stated prospectively and related to the federally funded programs.
2. Whether the structure and magnitude of the threatened funding loss left Kansas a genuinely voluntary choice.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Would hold the pleaded funding consequences insufficient to establish coercion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the Rule 12(b)(6) dismissal and remand for factual development.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Advance notice and programmatic relation

Holding and operative rule: The pleaded TANF and Title IV-D conditions were stated prospectively and bear a direct relationship to the funded welfare and child-support programs; satisfaction of those requirements does not resolve coercion. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Congress identified participation in a compliant Title IV-D child-support system as a condition associated with the federal welfare program before Kansas accepted the relevant funds. The requirements govern administration of the same child-support and family-assistance structure for which the federal money is supplied. Kansas therefore had advance notice of the program terms, and the relationship between the funded programs and the administrative duties is not merely incidental. Clear notice and relatedness remain independent requirements, however. They establish what Congress offered and what obligations accompanied acceptance; they do not establish that the State retained a genuinely practicable ability to refuse the offer.

##### Question 2: Spending Clause coercion

Holding and operative rule: A funding condition is coercive when the structure and magnitude of the threatened loss materially eliminate a State’s reasonably practicable ability to decline, so that the formal offer operates in practical effect as a command to administer federal policy. No fixed percentage controls, and the State bears the ultimate burden of proving coercion.

Reasoning: Formal authority to reject federal funds is relevant but not conclusive. A court must examine the amount and centrality of the threatened funds, dependence on the existing stream, whether Congress offers genuinely new money or leverages an established program, the relation between the old funds and the new demand, the scope of added obligations, transition time, realistic replacement or restructuring options, effects on separately funded functions, and any genuine waiver or safety valve. Kansas alleges that approximately $29.3 million in Title IV-D funds and $101.9 million in TANF funds were placed at risk and that federal money supplied a substantial share of operating and systems costs. Those allegations do not prove coercion, but they preclude the assumption at the pleading stage that withdrawal or replacement was reasonably practicable. A factual record is required.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter and Ginsburg: The challenged provisions define the terms of related federal programs, and Kansas retains legal authority to decline participation. They would hold that the size and administrative importance of a grant, without more, do not state a constitutional claim and would affirm the dismissal.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education*, decided June 29, 2000: clarified; genuine voluntariness includes a State’s reasonably practicable ability to decline, not merely its formal legal authority to do so.  
*Printz v. United States*: clarified; voluntary funding conditions remain distinct from direct commandeering, but financial terms that eliminate practical choice may operate as a command.  
*South Dakota v. Dole*: clarified; Spending Clause coercion is contextual and has no fixed numerical threshold.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for development of the fiscal and programmatic record and application of the practical-choice standard.  
Effect: Kansas has not established that the conditions are unconstitutional, but its coercion claim may proceed beyond the pleading stage. The notice and relatedness objections are rejected, and lawful cooperative federal programs remain enforceable when a State retains a genuine practical choice.

### Lujan v. G & G Fire Sprinklers, Inc.

Argued February 26, 2001 | Decided April 17, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: separate prewithholding hearing required and statutory withholding provisions enjoined, 204 F.3d 941 (9th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/189/))  
Area of Law: Procedural due process; Public contracts; Withheld payments

Questions Presented:
1. Does California deny procedural due process by withholding disputed public-contract payments when the contractor may bring an ordinary judicial action determining entitlement and awarding payment?

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: a timely and genuinely accessible judicial contract action capable of determining entitlement and awarding the withheld payment supplies constitutionally adequate process on this record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Process for a disputed contract payment

Holding and operative rule: When a public contractor has a timely ordinary judicial action that can determine contractual entitlement and award the disputed payment, Due Process does not require a separate prewithholding administrative hearing. The judicial route must be genuinely accessible and timely rather than merely nominal.

Reasoning: The property interest asserted is the contractor’s claim of entitlement under the public-works contract. California disputes that entitlement because of alleged prevailing-wage violations, and the ordinary contract action permits a court to resolve the factual and legal dispute and order payment if the contractor prevails. A separate administrative hearing would largely duplicate that determination and add little protection against erroneous deprivation. Due Process evaluates the complete procedure the State makes available, not whether every governmental withholding is preceded by an additional tribunal. The record identifies no bad-faith delay, prohibitive barrier, or timing defect making the judicial remedy practically meaningless. The Ninth Circuit therefore erred in treating the absence of a separate prewithholding hearing as independently unconstitutional.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan*, decided March 3, 1999: applied; a disputed claim to payment may be resolved through a timely judicial entitlement proceeding without a duplicative prepayment or prewithholding hearing.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the injunction requiring a separate prewithholding hearing is set aside.  
Effect: California may withhold the disputed contract sums while entitlement is adjudicated through the available judicial action. A judicial route rendered practically useless by prohibitive delay, cost, obstruction, or bad faith would present a different case.

### Easley v. Cromartie / Smallwood v. Cromartie

Argued November 27, 2000 | Decided April 18, 2001  
On appeal from the United States District Court for the Eastern District of North Carolina; judgment below: North Carolina’s Twelfth Congressional District invalidated on a finding of racial predominance, 133 F. Supp. 2d 407 (E.D.N.C. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/234/))  
Area of Law: Equal Protection; Redistricting; Racial predominance

Questions Presented:
1. Did the district court clearly err in finding that race, rather than politics and traditional districting considerations, predominated in the design of North Carolina’s Twelfth Congressional District?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Race, politics, and clear-error review

Holding and operative rule: Where race and party closely correlate, racial predominance turns on whether race actually controlled district assignments or materially subordinated supported political and traditional criteria. Political objectives supported by political data are not converted into race by correlation alone, but deliberate use of race as a partisan proxy remains racial sorting.

Reasoning: Racial awareness, district shape, racial composition, and close race-party correlation establish the need to identify the legislature’s operative criterion, but they do not answer that question. The challengers retained the burden of proving that race, rather than political voting behavior and traditional districting considerations, controlled the material inclusions, exclusions, and boundary movements. The record contained substantial precinct-level political evidence and traditional explanations that the district court discounted without an adequate basis. Political data do not become racial classifications merely because racial identity predicts party preference. Conversely, a legislature cannot escape strict scrutiny by relabeling racial targeting as politics if it actually uses race as the proxy. On this record, the finding that race predominated is clearly erroneous.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Scalia and Kennedy: The district court heard the witnesses and possessed direct evidence supporting a racial explanation for the line drawing. Under clear-error review, they would not displace the permissible factual choice of the trial court.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Hunt v. Cromartie*, decided May 17, 1999: followed; supported political and racial explanations must be distinguished by identifying the criterion that actually controlled the mapmakers’ assignments.  
*Miller v. Johnson*: applied; racial predominance requires material subordination of traditional race-neutral districting principles.  
*Bush v. Vera*: clarified; race-party correlation alone does not establish racial predominance, while deliberate use of race as a partisan proxy remains subject to the racial-classification inquiry.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment invalidating District 12 is reversed, and the companion matters are remanded with instructions to set aside the finding of racial predominance and conduct any further proceedings consistently with the challengers’ burden and the clear-error standard.  
Effect: District 12 may not be invalidated on the racial-gerrymandering theory established below. Any independently preserved partisan-gerrymandering claim remains legally distinct and is not decided by this judgment.

### Alexander v. Sandoval

Argued January 16, 2001 | Decided April 24, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: private enforcement of a Title VI disparate-impact regulation sustained, 197 F.3d 484 (11th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/275/))  
Area of Law: Civil rights; Statutory remedies; Title VI

Questions Presented:
1. Does Title VI permit intended beneficiaries to obtain prospective private enforcement of a valid disparate-impact regulation promulgated under § 602?

#### Judgment

Reversed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Private enforcement of § 602 regulations

Holding and operative rule: Title VI does not imply a private cause of action to enforce disparate-impact regulations promulgated under § 602 when the enacted text and enforcement scheme create neither the private right nor the private remedy.

Reasoning: Section 601’s established private action addresses the intentional discrimination prohibited by that section. Section 602 separately authorizes federal agencies to promulgate regulations and administer compliance through the enforcement mechanisms Congress supplied. A regulation may impose a valid federal duty without creating a private cause of action in persons benefiting from it. The source of the private right and remedy must be found in enacted law, not in the regulation’s substantive breadth or in the desirability of private enforcement. *Lau* did not separately analyze and decide that source question, and statutory purpose cannot substitute for rights-creating language and a private remedial command. The Eleventh Circuit therefore erred by treating the disparate-impact regulation as privately enforceable merely because it was valid and intended to benefit the affected class.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The longstanding treatment of Title VI, its implementing regulations, and Congress’s subsequent actions support private prospective enforcement by intended beneficiaries. They would affirm the injunction against Alabama’s English-only examination policy.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District*, decided June 22, 1998: applied; statutory duty, private cause of action, recipient notice, and available remedy remain separate legal questions.  
*Lau v. Nichols*: declined to extend to establish an implied private cause of action for § 602 disparate-impact regulations without an independent analysis of enacted remedial intent.  
*Cannon v. University of Chicago*: clarified; the established private action tied to intentional discrimination does not automatically encompass every duty created by an implementing regulation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the privately asserted § 602 disparate-impact theory shall be dismissed.  
Effect: Sandoval may not maintain this action solely to enforce the disparate-impact regulation. Intentional-discrimination claims under § 601, federal agency enforcement, and any independently established statutory, § 1983, or prospective officer channel remain governed separately.

### Atwater v. City of Lago Vista

Argued December 4, 2000 | Decided April 24, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the officer and municipality affirmed, 195 F.3d 242 (5th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/318/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Custodial arrest; Fine-only offenses

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Fourth Amendment permits full custodial arrest for a fine-only offense when citation is legally available and no objective enforcement need requires custody.
2. What follows for individual, municipal, and remedial responsibility after the constitutional holding.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Souter | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse on the Fourth Amendment merits and remand the separate liability and remedial questions.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Custodial arrest for a fine-only offense

Holding and operative rule: For an offense carrying no possibility of incarceration when citation is legally available, citation and release are the Fourth Amendment baseline. Full custodial arrest requires specific objective facts showing an enforcement need that citation cannot adequately address.

Reasoning: Probable cause authorizes enforcement of the offense, but it does not make every degree of seizure reasonable. The legislature’s decision to prescribe only a fine identifies the offense’s legal severity, while the availability of citation ordinarily satisfies the interests in identification, notice, prosecution, and appearance. Custody remains reasonable when specific facts show uncertain identity, an outstanding warrant, danger, intoxication or incapacity, flight or nonappearance risk, evidence-preservation need, continuing misconduct, refusal to complete citation procedures, or a comparable enforcement necessity. Atwater identified herself, cooperated, and faced only fine-only seatbelt charges. The record identifies no safety, flight, evidence, identity, warrant, or continuing-conduct concern. Handcuffing, transport, booking, and detention therefore imposed an objectively unnecessary custodial seizure.

##### Question 2: Liability, immunity, and remedy

Holding and operative rule: The newly stated constitutional boundary does not itself establish individual damages, municipal liability, causation, or a particular prospective remedy. Those questions must be adjudicated separately on remand.

Reasoning: The merits inquiry asks whether the seizure was reasonable. Qualified immunity asks whether the governing law gave the officer sufficiently clear notice when he acted, and municipal liability requires proof of an attributable policy or custom and the remaining statutory elements. Because the Court states the citation baseline expressly for the first time and the present record does not establish an obvious knowing violation under prior law, the officer may be entitled to qualified immunity. The municipality’s responsibility, any policy authorizing unnecessary custodial arrests, causation, damages, and the need for prospective relief require their own findings and cannot be inferred from the constitutional violation alone.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Historical arrest authority and the need for an administrable rule permit a warrantless custodial arrest whenever an officer has probable cause to believe that a criminal offense occurred in the officer’s presence. They would not require officers to litigate the comparative adequacy of citation at the roadside.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Knowles v. Iowa*, decided December 8, 1998: extended; the distinction between citation and custody now limits full custodial arrest, not only a search incident to citation, when the offense is fine only and citation is legally available.  
Fourth Amendment reasonableness: established; probable cause does not alone justify full custody for a fine-only offense when no objective enforcement need distinguishes custody from citation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for separate adjudication of qualified immunity, municipal liability, causation, damages, and prospective relief.  
Effect: Atwater establishes a Fourth Amendment violation on the supplied facts, but no defendant is adjudged personally or municipally liable by this decision. Officers may still use custody when specific objective facts establish an enforcement need that citation cannot adequately address.

### Daniels v. United States

Argued January 8, 2001 | Decided April 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: § 2255 collateral attack on expired state predicates rejected, 195 F.3d 501 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/374/))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas corpus; Sentencing predicates; Finality

Questions Presented:
1. May a federal prisoner use § 2255 to challenge an enhanced sentence by relitigating fully expired state convictions?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone in full; Scalia except the reservation concerning rare circumstances; Kennedy and Thomas in full | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion and partial join as stated |
| Concurrence in part | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Rejects any suggestion of an additional rare-circumstances route under § 2255 |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Would permit collateral review of the predicate through the current enhanced sentence |
| Dissent | Breyer | No other Justice | Would reverse | Would reconsider the forum limitation adopted in *Custis* |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Section 2255 and expired state predicates

Holding and operative rule: A federal prisoner ordinarily may not use § 2255 to relitigate a fully expired state conviction used to enhance the federal sentence. A conviction obtained after complete denial of the *Gideon* right to counsel remains the categorical exception, and later lawful vacatur may support whatever separately authorized sentence correction governing law permits.

Reasoning: The forum and finality principles stated in *Custis* do not disappear after federal sentencing concludes. Constitutional challenges to a state conviction ordinarily must be pursued while the defendant is in custody on that conviction through the direct and collateral channels authorized to review it. Allowing § 2255 to reopen an expired predicate would require federal sentencing courts to reconstruct stale state proceedings, permit an end run around state and federal limitations rules, and undermine the finality of outstanding judgments. A complete denial of counsel remains distinct because it is a categorical defect identifiable without a new trial of fact-intensive claims. Daniels alleges invalid pleas and ineffective assistance, not complete absence of counsel, and therefore cannot obtain review through this § 2255 motion. Reliable actual innocence and a no-fault absence of any realistic earlier review channel remain unresolved.

#### Concurrences

Scalia: He joins the rule denying § 2255 review but rejects any suggestion that the statute may contain an additional exception for rare circumstances beyond the established complete-denial-of-counsel category.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg: When the present federal sentence depends on the validity of earlier convictions, § 2255 should permit adjudication of a substantial constitutional challenge that could not practically have been pursued when the lesser state sentence was being served.

Breyer: He would reconsider *Custis* and permit the sentencing or postconviction court to review a predicate whose renewed legal consequence materially increases current imprisonment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Custis v. United States*, decided May 23, 1994: extended; its forum-and-finality rule now governs a post-sentencing § 2255 attack on fully expired state predicates.  
*Gideon v. Wainwright*: reaffirmed; complete denial of counsel remains categorically distinct from ineffective-assistance, involuntary-plea, and other fact-intensive challenges.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Daniels may not use the present § 2255 motion to relitigate his expired state convictions. If a predicate is later vacated through a lawful reviewing channel, he may seek any sentence correction independently authorized at that time.

### Lackawanna County District Attorney v. Coss

Argued February 20, 2001 | Decided April 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: § 2254 review of expired predicate convictions permitted through a later sentence, 204 F.3d 453 (3d Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/394/))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas corpus; Current custody; Expired predicates

Questions Presented:
1. Does custody under a later state sentence create a § 2254 route for relitigating an earlier conviction whose sentence has fully expired?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone in full; Scalia as to the current-custody and general forum rule; Kennedy in full; Thomas as to the current-custody rule, general forum rule, and complete-denial-of-counsel exception | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion and partial joins as stated |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Would permit review through the current sentence |
| Dissent | Breyer | No other Justice | Would affirm or remand on the preserved causal record | Rejects the categorical forum bar and disputes the Court’s treatment of the current sentence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Current custody and an expired predicate

Holding and operative rule: A prisoner in custody under a later state sentence may not ordinarily use § 2254 to relitigate a fully expired predicate conviction merely because the earlier conviction affected the current sentence. A complete *Gideon* denial remains the categorical exception, and later lawful invalidation of the predicate may support separately authorized relief from current custody.

Reasoning: Coss satisfies § 2254’s custody requirement because he attacks the later sentence he is currently serving, but jurisdictional custody does not itself create a remedy for relitigating every earlier judgment considered at sentencing. The forum-and-finality principles applicable to outstanding expired convictions require constitutional challenges to be pursued through the direct or collateral process attached to the predicate while that route remains available. Otherwise, a later sentencing reference would reopen stale judgments without regard to limitations, exhaustion, lost records, or the authority of the original court. Complete denial of counsel remains categorically distinct. The Court leaves open a case involving a reliable showing of actual innocence or a no-fault absence of any realistic review channel. Coss’s present ordinary ineffective-assistance claim may not proceed through § 2254 on the theory accepted below.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg: A prisoner challenging the current sentence should be able to establish that the sentence rests on a constitutionally invalid predicate, particularly when the earlier state collateral proceeding was never adjudicated.

Breyer: The current sentence supplies the relevant custody, and the record does not justify resolving against Coss the causal effect of the 1986 convictions. He would preserve review or remand without imposing the Court’s categorical forum limitation.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Custis v. United States*, decided May 23, 1994: extended; its forum-and-finality rule independently governs § 2254 challenges to current state sentences based on fully expired predicates.  
*Maleng v. Cook*: clarified; custody under a later sentence establishes the habeas posture but does not itself create a remedy for relitigating an expired conviction.  
*Gideon v. Wainwright*: reaffirmed; complete denial of counsel remains the categorical predicate-conviction exception.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the expired-predicate challenge without prejudice to any later source-authorized request following lawful invalidation of the predicate.  
Effect: Coss may not use the present § 2254 proceeding for ordinary relitigation of the 1986 convictions. No-fault absence of review, reliable actual innocence, exhaustion, causal effect, and the form of relief after a lawful vacatur remain governed by their independent requirements.

### C & L Enterprises, Inc. v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe of Oklahoma

Argued March 19, 2001 | Decided April 30, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Civil Appeals of Oklahoma; judgment below: enforcement proceeding ordered dismissed on tribal-immunity grounds after remand under *Kiowa*. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/411/))  
Area of Law: Federal Indian law; Tribal sovereign immunity; Arbitration

Questions Presented:
1. Did the Tribe clearly waive sovereign immunity by executing a contract requiring arbitration, selecting Oklahoma law, making the award final, and authorizing judicial enforcement?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Contractual waiver of tribal immunity

Holding and operative rule: A tribe clearly waives immunity for a covered contract dispute when integrated provisions require binding arbitration, make the award final, select governing law, and authorize judicial enforcement of the award. No ritual use of the words sovereign immunity is required.

Reasoning: *Kiowa* establishes that tribal immunity applies to off-reservation commercial contracts unless Congress abrogates it or the tribe clearly waives it. The contract here does more than select arbitration as an informal private process. It requires binding resolution of all covered disputes, makes the award final, incorporates rules authorizing entry of judgment in a federal or state court possessing jurisdiction, and selects Oklahoma law, whose arbitration statute identifies the competent state court. Read together, those provisions would be deprived of their agreed practical operation if the Tribe could invoke immunity against the enforcement proceeding the contract expressly contemplates. The waiver remains clear even though it appears in a standard form and does not use a prescribed verbal formula. Its scope is defined by the contract’s dispute, forum, enforcement, and remedial terms.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies, Inc.*, decided May 26, 1998: clarified; a clear tribal waiver may arise from integrated arbitration and judicial-enforcement provisions without an express recital of the words sovereign immunity.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Civil Appeals of Oklahoma is reversed, and the case is remanded for enforcement proceedings within the contract’s claim-, forum-, and remedy-specific waiver.  
Effect: The Tribe may not invoke sovereign immunity to defeat the Oklahoma proceeding to enforce the covered arbitral award. The waiver creates no general jurisdiction over the Tribe and does not extend to unrelated claims, forums, or remedies outside the contract.
