## Decisions and Dispositions

### Urofsky v. Gilmore

Argued March 19, 2001 | Decided May 7, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: statute sustained against the professors’ First Amendment challenge, 216 F.3d 401 (4th Cir. 2000). ([Biotech Law](https://biotech.law.lsu.edu/cases/privacy/urofsky_v_gilmore.htm))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Public employment; Public-university scholarship

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a broad prospective content restriction on public-university employees’ online research and teaching is categorically outside First Amendment protection or must be reviewed under public-employee speech doctrine.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | No other Justice | Would affirm | Applies the prospective-restriction framework but finds the State’s resource limits and bona fide research process sufficient |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Would permit the State to control employee use of government-owned computers for official work |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: public-university research and teaching are not categorically unprotected because they occur within public employment; a broad prospective content restriction on scholarly inquiry must be reviewed under *National Treasury Employees Union* and *Pickering*, without creating a separate academic-freedom tier.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Prospective restriction on public-university scholarship

Holding and operative rule: Public-university research and teaching are not categorically outside First Amendment protection merely because they are undertaken as part of public employment. A broad prospective content restriction on scholarly inquiry is reviewed under the heightened prospective-restriction framework of *United States v. National Treasury Employees Union* together with *Pickering*, while giving full weight to the university’s authority over curriculum, resources, security, and workplace operations.

Reasoning: Virginia regulates the use of computers it owns or leases and retains substantial authority to prevent unlawful conduct, network misuse, workplace harassment, security risks, and expenditures unrelated to institutional work. That authority does not make all research performed by public-university employees constitutionally invisible. The Act prospectively restricts an identified category of content across a large class of employees before any particular misuse, disruption, or curricular conflict occurs and requires prior approval even for bona fide scholarly work. The breadth of that deterrent requires a demonstrated connection between the restriction and the State’s actual institutional interests, together with reasonable fit. The Fourth Circuit instead treated the employees’ work-related capacity as categorically dispositive. Because the record does not establish the standards governing approval, the practical treatment of bona fide scholarship, or the extent of any chilling effect, those questions must be evaluated on remand under the correct framework. ([Biotech Law](https://biotech.law.lsu.edu/cases/privacy/urofsky_v_gilmore.htm))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor: The statute concerns only state-owned or state-leased computers, leaves employees free to use private resources, and expressly permits approval of bona fide research. Because no professor had been denied approval and the university governance process supplied a narrow accommodation, she would affirm under *National Treasury Employees Union* and *Pickering*.

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The State may prescribe how employees use government equipment in performing official duties. They would hold that the First Amendment does not require judicial supervision of content rules governing work performed with state resources.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. National Treasury Employees Union*: extended; its demanding justification requirement applies to a broad prospective content restriction on work-related public-university scholarship, not only to unrelated off-duty expression.  
*Pickering v. Board of Education*: applied; institutional academic, operational, security, and resource interests receive full weight but must be balanced against the affected scholarly expression.  
*Waters v. Churchill*: distinguished; deference concerning an individualized disciplinary decision does not establish categorical authority for a prospective restriction covering an entire class of research.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for evaluation of the prior-approval process, its governing standards, its treatment of bona fide scholarship, and its practical chilling effect under the stated framework.  
Effect: Virginia may regulate unlawful use, network security, spending, workplace disruption, and genuine curricular decisions, but it may not treat public-university research as categorically unprotected. The decision confers no individual veto over curriculum or resource allocation and does not decide discipline imposed for particular disruptive speech.

### Cooper Industries, Inc. v. Leatherman Tool Group, Inc.

Argued February 26, 2001 | Decided May 14, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: constitutional excessiveness of the punitive-damages award reviewed for abuse of discretion, 205 F.3d 1351 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/424/))  
Area of Law: Due process; Punitive damages; Appellate review

Questions Presented:
1. What appellate standard governs the constitutional determination that a punitive-damages award is grossly excessive?

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Joins the Court while preserving his objection to constitutional review of punitive-damages amounts |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Accepts de novo review under controlling precedent while preserving his objections to its premises |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Would affirm | Abuse-of-discretion review |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Appellate review of constitutional excessiveness

Holding and operative rule: Appellate courts review de novo the ultimate constitutional question whether a punitive-damages award is grossly excessive, while giving ordinary deference to supported historical and credibility findings.

Reasoning: Whether an award crosses the Due Process Clause’s gross-excessiveness boundary is an ultimate legal determination applying a federal constitutional standard to established facts. Independent appellate review is needed to give the *BMW v. Gore* guideposts consistent content and to promote comparable treatment across cases. Trial courts retain their superior position concerning credibility, demeanor, and other historical facts, which remain subject to ordinary deferential review. The Ninth Circuit instead asked only whether the District Court abused its discretion. That standard did not independently determine whether the award satisfied the federal constitutional boundary. The Court does not decide whether the $4.5 million award is excessive, select a replacement amount, establish a numerical ratio, or direct remittitur. The Court of Appeals must perform the constitutional analysis under the proper standard on remand. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-2035.ZO.html))

#### Concurrences

Thomas: He continues to reject substantive constitutional limits on punitive-damages amounts and would reconsider *BMW v. Gore* in a case properly presenting that question. Because this case presents only the standard of appellate review, he joins the Court.

Scalia: Although he disagrees with the underlying constitutional excessiveness doctrine and previously favored deferential review of comparable mixed questions, *Gore*, *Ornelas*, and *Bajakajian* make de novo review the proper result under existing law.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg: *Gasperini* and the traditional allocation of responsibility between judge and jury support abuse-of-discretion review, particularly because reprehensibility and harm depend heavily on trial-level factual assessments. She would affirm the Ninth Circuit’s chosen standard.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore*: clarified; appellate application of the constitutional punitive-damages guideposts is de novo.  
*Ornelas v. United States*: applied; independent review is appropriate when a constitutional standard acquires content through case-by-case application.  
*United States v. Bajakajian*: followed; ultimate constitutional excessiveness is reviewed independently while historical findings remain deferential.  
*Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc.*: distinguished; review of compensatory-damages excessiveness under state law does not control the ultimate federal constitutional question presented here.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for de novo appellate determination of constitutional excessiveness with ordinary deference to supported historical and credibility findings.  
Effect: The existing punitive-damages award is neither sustained nor invalidated by this decision. No fixed ratio, replacement award, presumption, or automatic remittitur is established.

### Rogers v. Tennessee

Argued November 1, 2000 | Decided May 14, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Tennessee; judgment below: murder conviction affirmed after retroactive abolition of the year-and-a-day limitation, 992 S.W.2d 393 (Tenn. 1999). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-6218.ZS.html))  
Area of Law: Due process; Criminal fair warning; Retroactive judicial change

Questions Presented:
1. Did retroactive abolition of an acknowledged substantive criminal-law limitation deny Rogers fair warning?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Stevens, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Broader legality principle governing retroactive judicial enlargement of criminal law |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Treats the abolition as foreseeable common-law development |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Due Process bars retroactive judicial abolition of an established substantive criminal-law limitation when the change was unexpected and indefensible by reference to the law expressed before the defendant’s conduct.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retroactive abolition of the year-and-a-day rule

Holding and operative rule: Due Process bars retroactive judicial abolition of an established substantive criminal-law limitation when the change was unexpected and indefensible by reference to the law expressed before the defendant’s conduct.

Reasoning: Tennessee acknowledged that the year-and-a-day rule formed part of its substantive homicide law when Rogers acted. The rule limited the circumstances in which a death could support a murder conviction, and its removal therefore enlarged the criminal liability attached to completed conduct. The State remained free to abolish the rule prospectively, and courts retain authority to clarify uncertain law, apply an existing rule to new facts, and adopt developments clearly foreshadowed by prior decisions. This change was different. Tennessee did not merely resolve an ambiguity or apply an existing principle. It removed an acknowledged limitation in Rogers’s own case and used the new rule to convert the conduct into murder. That retroactive enlargement did not provide the fair warning the Due Process Clause requires. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-6218.ZO.html))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Stevens and Thomas: Retroactive judicial enlargement of criminal liability should be constrained at least as strictly as retroactive legislative enlargement. They agree that Rogers cannot be convicted of murder under a rule created only after his conduct.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg: The year-and-a-day rule was an obsolete common-law doctrine whose abolition was foreseeable in light of modern medicine, causation law, and judicial authority over the common law. They would hold that applying the change to Rogers was neither unexpected nor indefensible and would affirm.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bouie v. City of Columbia*: followed and clarified; its fair-warning rule applies when a state court retroactively removes an acknowledged substantive limitation on criminal liability.  
*Fiore v. White*, decided January 9, 2001: distinguished; *Fiore* concerned the unchanged meaning of an existing offense, while this case concerns an after-the-fact enlargement of the offense.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Tennessee is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings that do not depend on retroactive abolition of the year-and-a-day rule.  
Effect: Rogers’s murder conviction may not remain in force on the retroactively enlarged theory. Tennessee may apply the changed rule prospectively and may pursue any lawful lesser offense or alternative theory that does not depend on the after-the-fact enlargement.

### United States v. Oakland Cannabis Buyers’ Cooperative

Argued March 28, 2001 | Decided May 14, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: federal injunction modified to permit a medical-necessity exception to the Controlled Substances Act, 190 F.3d 1109 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/00-151.ZS.html))  
Area of Law: Federal drug law; Statutory defenses; Equity  
Participation: Breyer took no part in the consideration or decision of the case; the basis was not publicly stated. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/00-151.ZS.html))

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Controlled Substances Act permits a medical-necessity defense to marijuana manufacturing and distribution.
2. Whether equity may create such an exception when a court selects injunctive relief.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: No Justice. Took no part: Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Concurs in the judgment | Agrees as to manufacturing and distribution while reserving broader necessity and possession questions |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Medical necessity under the Controlled Substances Act

Holding and operative rule: The Controlled Substances Act provides no medical-necessity defense to the manufacture or distribution of marijuana. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: Section 841 prohibits manufacture and distribution except as authorized by the statutory scheme. Marijuana’s placement in Schedule I, the Act’s express research authorization, and the absence of any medical-distribution exception reflect Congress’s substantive determination concerning the permitted uses of the drug. A court cannot add a necessity exception that conflicts with that enacted classification merely because particular patients may experience serious need or lack an effective alternative treatment. The conclusion is confined to the manufacturing and distribution conduct before the Court. It does not decide individual possession, constitutional challenges to the Act, administrative rescheduling, approved research, or whether a necessity defense may remain available under a materially different federal statute. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/00-151.ZS.html))

##### Question 2: Equitable discretion and statutory boundaries

Holding and operative rule: Equitable discretion permits a court to choose among lawful enforcement mechanisms, but it does not authorize creation of a substantive exception that defeats the policy judgment Congress enacted. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: A district court ordinarily retains discretion when deciding whether and how to issue an injunction. That discretion concerns the comparative operation of lawful enforcement devices, including whether an injunction is necessary in light of criminal enforcement or another available mechanism. It does not permit the court to treat conduct covered by the statute as lawful or to withhold enforcement because it disagrees with Congress’s substantive balance. The Ninth Circuit directed consideration of medical necessity as a basis for exempting covered distribution from the injunction. Because the Act supplies no such exception, that instruction exceeded the lawful bounds of equitable discretion. The lower court may reconsider only the choice among enforcement mechanisms that remain consistent with the Act.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Souter and Ginsburg: They agree that a distributor or manufacturer cannot invoke medical necessity to avoid the Controlled Substances Act and therefore concur in reversal. They would expressly leave open individual possession by a seriously ill patient, broader common-law necessity questions, and the equitable treatment of materially different violations.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Bailey*: distinguished; discussion of common-law necessity does not authorize an exception contrary to the Controlled Substances Act’s specific scheduling and authorization provisions.  
*Hecht Co. v. Bowles*: clarified; equitable discretion concerns the selection of lawful relief and does not permit a court to negate the governing statute.  
*Weinberger v. Romero-Barcelo*: clarified; a court may choose among enforcement mechanisms that preserve statutory compliance but may not use equity to create substantive noncompliance.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for selection among enforcement mechanisms that remain lawful under the Controlled Substances Act.  
Effect: The Cooperative may not manufacture or distribute marijuana under an extra-statutory medical-necessity exception. Individual possession, constitutional claims, rescheduling, research authorization, palliative-care policy, and the general availability of necessity under materially different statutes remain unresolved.

### Dismissed as Improvidently Granted: United States v. Tank, 200 F.3d 627, May 14, 2001

Acting Justice or Court: The Court.  
Public action: The writ of certiorari is dismissed as improvidently granted.  
Publicly stated basis: The judgment below did not decide the stored-communications privacy, third-party-consent, or computer-warrant-particularity questions accepted for review. The Ninth Circuit addressed authentication of chat-room records, seizure of a removable disk during a vehicle search incident to arrest, evidentiary sufficiency, and sentencing, while the record lacks the access-control, ownership, consent, storage, and search-scope facts needed to decide the granted questions. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/200/627/521509/))  
Effect on judgment below or existing order: The Ninth Circuit’s judgment affirming the convictions, reversing the sentence in part, and remanding for resentencing remains undisturbed.  
Next procedural stage: Any remaining proceedings continue under the Ninth Circuit’s judgment; no Supreme Court merits instruction issues.

### Bartnicki v. Vopper / United States v. Vopper

Argued December 5, 2000 | Decided May 21, 2001  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: civil liability against the publishers rejected, 200 F.3d 109 (3d Cir. 1999). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-1687.ZS.html))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Speech and press; Truthful public-concern information

Questions Presented:
1. May the Government impose civil liability on a publisher who did not participate in unlawful interception but lawfully received and published truthful information concerning a matter of public concern?

#### Judgment

Affirmed in both consolidated matters, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | O’Connor | Supports affirmance | Narrow fit between the public concern, threatened harm, and the publishers’ lawful conduct |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Privacy and protection of confidential communications |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm both judgments.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Publication of lawfully received intercepted information

Holding and operative rule: A publisher who did not intercept or procure interception may not be held civilly liable for publishing lawfully received, truthful information on a matter of public concern merely because the source obtained the information unlawfully.

Reasoning: The publishers played no part in the interception, violated no law in obtaining the recording, and disclosed truthful information concerning contentious public labor negotiations and a threat of physical harm. The Government possesses a substantial interest in protecting private communications and may punish the interceptor, a procurer, or a publisher that participates in unlawful acquisition. The liability provisions as applied here, however, impose their burden at the point of protected publication rather than on the unlawful acquisition. Once truthful information concerning a matter of public concern came lawfully into the publishers’ possession, that privacy interest did not justify civil punishment of these nonparticipants on this record. The holding does not create a general privilege for unlawful access, trade secrets, purely private information, or publication producing an exceptional concrete harm under a narrowly fitted rule. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-1687.ZO.html))

#### Concurrences

Breyer, joined by O’Connor: The judgment rests on the publishers’ lawful conduct and the unusual public importance of information involving a potential threat of physical harm. A materially broader publication immunity would undervalue privacy and the role confidentiality plays in protecting private speech.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The interception and disclosure prohibitions protect the privacy necessary for candid electronic and telephone communication. They would permit civil liability against a publisher that knowingly disseminates an illegally intercepted private conversation, even when the conversation concerns a public dispute.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Smith v. Daily Mail Publishing Co.*: extended; truthful public-concern information lawfully possessed by a nonparticipating publisher receives protection notwithstanding unlawful conduct by the source.  
*Florida Star v. B.J.F.*: extended; the Government ordinarily must direct sanctions toward unlawful acquisition rather than punish lawful receipt and publication.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgments of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit are affirmed in both consolidated matters.  
Effect: The media defendants may not be held civilly liable on the interception-based publication theories presented. The interceptor, any procurer, and any publisher participating in unlawful access remain subject to otherwise valid law.

### United States v. Hatter

Argued February 20, 2001 | Decided May 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below: Medicare and Social Security tax challenges sustained and a later salary increase held not to cure the constitutional diminution, 203 F.3d 795 (Fed. Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/557/))  
Area of Law: Separation of powers; Judicial compensation; Federal taxation  
Participation: Stevens and O’Connor took no part in the consideration or decision of the case; the basis was not publicly stated. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/557/))

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Medicare tax diminishes sitting judges’ compensation.
2. Whether the Social Security tax distinctively disadvantaged sitting judges.
3. Whether an unrelated later salary increase cured the violation.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 5-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas. Took no part: Stevens, O’Connor.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Medicare tax | Reversed, 5-2 | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The constitutional challenge to the neutral Medicare tax is rejected. |
| Social Security tax | Affirmed, 7-0 | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The tax may not be imposed on the affected sitting judges under the challenged design. |
| Later salary increase | Affirmed, 7-0 | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The unrelated increase is not an automatic cure; the remedy remains for further proceedings. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Complete opinion: Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg; Parts I, II, and V: Scalia | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion and partial join as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Supports the Social Security and no-cure components but would affirm the Medicare component |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Supports the Social Security and no-cure components but would affirm the Medicare component |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the Medicare component, affirm the Social Security and no-cure components, and remand.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: neutral, generally applicable taxation does not itself diminish judicial compensation, but a tax design that distinctively disadvantages sitting judges relative to materially comparable incumbents violates the Compensation Clause, and an unrelated later salary increase is not an automatic cure.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Neutral Medicare taxation

Holding and operative rule: Neutral, generally applicable taxation of sitting judges does not itself diminish judicial compensation within the meaning of Article III.

Reasoning: The Compensation Clause protects judicial independence against legislative reduction of the compensation attached to judicial office. It does not give judges immunity from taxes shared on neutral terms with materially comparable citizens and employees. Such a tax operates through the Government’s general sovereign authority rather than through a reduction directed at judicial office, and it presents none of the distinctive risk of legislative retaliation that supports an absolute rule against direct salary cuts. The Medicare tax applied broadly to federal employees and the public rather than singling out sitting judges for unfavorable treatment. The Federal Circuit therefore erred in treating its application as a constitutional diminution. Direct reductions of judicial salary and tax measures specially targeting judges remain prohibited. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/557/))

##### Question 2: Distinctive Social Security disadvantage

Holding and operative rule: A tax that distinctively disadvantages sitting judges relative to materially comparable incumbent federal employees violates the Compensation Clause. (joined by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Congress permitted nearly all incumbent federal employees to avoid an additional Social Security burden through the treatment of their existing retirement systems, while the design excluded the noncontributory judicial retirement system and made the additional deduction effectively unavoidable for sitting judges. The proper comparison is with materially comparable incumbent federal employees affected by the same transition. The distinctive disadvantage imposed a substantial cost while offering little expected benefit to most affected judges, and the Government’s asserted equalization rationale did not explain the statutory difference with adequate precision. The Compensation Clause does not demand favorable tax treatment, but it forbids use of a transitional tax design that withdraws from sitting judges an option preserved for comparable incumbents.

##### Question 3: Effect of the later salary increase

Holding and operative rule: An unrelated later salary increase does not automatically cure a Compensation Clause violation. (joined by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: A later increase may matter when law gives it a remedial relationship to the prior diminution, but an ordinary salary adjustment does not erase an existing violation merely because it exceeds the amount previously withheld. The 1984 increase addressed the erosion of judicial purchasing power and was not identified as a make-whole adjustment for the Social Security burden. Treating every later increase as an automatic offset would permit an unconstitutional diminution to remain without a source-authorized remedy and would obscure the legal basis and period of the violation. The lower courts must determine the proper relief, limitations, and accounting consequences under the sources governing the claim.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part: He agrees that the Social Security tax violated the Compensation Clause and that the later salary increase did not automatically cure the violation. He would also hold the Medicare tax unconstitutional because elimination of an employment-conditioned tax exemption reduces judicial compensation even when the resulting tax burden is generally shared.

Thomas, concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part: He would preserve *Evans v. Gore* and hold that any tax reducing the net compensation of a sitting judge violates Article III. He therefore would affirm the Federal Circuit on both tax components and on the absence of an automatic cure.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Evans v. Gore*: overruled in part; neutral and generally applicable taxation of sitting judges does not violate the Compensation Clause merely because it reduces take-home pay.  
*O’Malley v. Woodrough*: followed; judges remain subject to generally shared tax burdens imposed through neutral sovereign taxation.  
*United States v. Will*: distinguished and clarified; the absolute prohibition on direct salary reductions does not govern neutral taxation, and an unrelated later increase does not automatically cure an earlier constitutional diminution.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is affirmed as to the Social Security tax and the absence of an automatic cure, reversed as to the Medicare tax, and the case is remanded for a source-authorized remedy.  
Effect: The Government may collect the neutral Medicare tax from the affected judges but may not impose the challenged Social Security burden on them. The lower courts must determine the lawful retrospective and prospective relief without treating the 1984 salary increase as an automatic offset.

### Wharf (Holdings) Ltd. v. United International Holdings, Inc.

Argued March 21, 2001 | Decided May 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: securities-fraud judgment affirmed, 210 F.3d 1207 (10th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/588/))  
Area of Law: Securities fraud; Section 10(b); Misrepresentation of present intent

Questions Presented:
1. Does selling an option while concealing a contemporaneous intent never to permit its exercise constitute deception in connection with the sale of a security?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Secret intent not to honor a securities option

Holding and operative rule: Selling a security while concealing a contemporaneous intent never to honor the bargained-for option is a misrepresentation of present fact in connection with the sale, not merely later nonperformance. Liability continues to require proof of the original fraudulent intent and every other element of the securities-fraud claim.

Reasoning: The option itself is a security, and United purchased it by providing the services Wharf requested. Section 10(b) is not categorically limited to written transactions, nor does *Blue Chip Stamps* exclude an actual purchaser whose bargain was completed through an oral agreement. Wharf’s alleged deception concerned the value and operative character of the security at the moment of sale: an option the seller already intended never to permit the buyer to exercise was materially different from the option represented. That theory does not federalize every contract dispute. A later breach, changed circumstances, ambiguity, or failure of performance does not prove securities fraud without evidence that the seller possessed the concealed fraudulent intent when the security was sold. The verdict and judgment before the Court rested on evidence sufficient to support that contemporaneous-intent theory. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/588/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Blue Chip Stamps v. Manor Drug Stores*: distinguished; United actually purchased the option and is not a disappointed potential purchaser.  
Section 10(b) and Rule 10b-5: clarified; concealment of a contemporaneous intent never to honor the security sold is actionable present-fact deception when the remaining elements are proved.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The securities-fraud judgment against Wharf remains in force. Ordinary breach, ambiguity, changed circumstances, and later nonperformance without proof of original fraudulent intent remain outside the holding.

### Freedom From Religion Foundation, Inc. v. City of Marshfield

Argued March 27, 2001 | Decided May 21, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the City and private owner vacated and remand ordered for an attribution-focused remedy, 203 F.3d 487 (7th Cir. 2000). ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/203/f3d/487/freedom-from-religion-foundation-inc-v-city-of-marshfield))  
Area of Law: Establishment Clause; Private religious expression; Governmental attribution

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a city ends Establishment Clause responsibility for a religious monument by transferring the monument parcel when the physical and administrative arrangement still attributes the display to the city.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Treats the completed sale and private ownership as ending governmental attribution |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Attribution after transfer of a monument parcel

Holding and operative rule: A formal sale does not end Establishment Clause responsibility when the Government’s continuing physical and administrative arrangement leaves a religious display reasonably attributable to the city. Relief must eliminate governmental attribution while preserving genuinely private religious expression.

Reasoning: The sale complied with state law, transferred title for fair value, and placed ownership and ordinary maintenance responsibilities in private hands. Those facts weigh strongly toward private attribution, but they do not conclusively resolve how the continuing arrangement operates. The small parcel remains embedded within the municipal park, lacks an effective visual boundary, is dedicated to public use, and contains the permanent religious display around which the park historically was organized. The City retains control of the surrounding property, and the present layout leaves an ordinary observer unable to distinguish the private parcel from municipal land while giving this message a permanent position unavailable to other park users. The Constitution does not require suppression of the private owner’s religious expression. It requires an administrable boundary, such as effective physical separation, unmistakable private identification, relocation, or another measure that ends reasonable governmental attribution. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/203/f3d/487/freedom-from-religion-foundation-inc-v-city-of-marshfield))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: A lawful sale for fair value transferred the parcel and monument to a private owner, ended municipal maintenance, and removed the religious message from governmental control. They would not treat visual proximity to a public park or the absence of a more substantial barrier as sufficient state action to burden private religious expression.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette*: clarified; private ownership and neutral treatment are highly relevant, but attribution remains contextual when the Government’s physical arrangement and operation obscure the line between public and private expression.  
*Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller*: applied; neutral identification and other administrable boundaries may protect private religious speech while preventing reasonable attribution to the State.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed, and the remand shall proceed for relief sufficient to end governmental attribution through effective separation, unmistakable private identification, relocation, or another workable boundary.  
Effect: Removal of the monument is not mandatory if a narrower measure genuinely establishes that the display is private. A disclaimer alone is insufficient if the physical operation of the site continues to communicate municipal ownership, sponsorship, or preferential access.

### Booth v. Churner

Argued March 20, 2001 | Decided May 29, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: prisoner action dismissed without prejudice for failure to exhaust administrative remedies, 206 F.3d 289 (3d Cir. 2000). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-1964.ZS.html))  
Area of Law: Prison litigation; PLRA exhaustion

Questions Presented:
1. Must a prisoner seeking only money damages exhaust an administrative process that cannot award money but can investigate and provide some other response?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Exhaustion when the requested remedy is unavailable

Holding and operative rule: A prisoner must exhaust an available administrative process that can take some action responsive to the complaint, even when that process cannot award the particular monetary remedy sought in court.

Reasoning: Section 1997e(a) requires exhaustion of available administrative remedies before a prisoner brings an action concerning prison conditions. In context, exhaustion concerns completion of the administrative process, not whether that process can award every form of relief a court might later provide. A grievance system capable of investigating the incident, correcting conduct, changing conditions, disciplining personnel, or supplying another responsive measure remains available for the complaint even if it cannot award damages. Booth initiated the Pennsylvania grievance procedure but did not complete the available administrative appeal. Dismissal without prejudice was therefore proper. The statutory term available retains practical force: a prisoner need not pursue a process that is inaccessible, lacks authority to respond in any way, or is obstructed by officials. Exhaustion resolves no question concerning the merits or the damages recoverable after completion. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-1964.ZS.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*McCarthy v. Madigan*: declined to extend; its judge-made exhaustion exceptions do not override the Prison Litigation Reform Act’s enacted exhaustion command.  
42 U.S.C. § 1997e(a): clarified; availability requires a process capable of some responsive action, not authority to award the specific judicial remedy requested.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit dismissing the action without prejudice is affirmed.  
Effect: Booth must complete the available grievance process before returning to federal court. No exhaustion is required if the process is inaccessible, incapable of any response, officially obstructed, or otherwise unavailable in operation, and the decision resolves no merits or damages question.
