## Decisions and Dispositions

### Buckhannon Board & Care Home, Inc. v. West Virginia Department of Health and Human Resources

Argued February 27, 2001 | Decided May 29, 2001 `OT_2000CHUNK6.md`  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: denial of prevailing-party attorney fees affirmed, 203 F.3d 819 (4th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/598/))  
Area of Law: Civil-rights remedies; Attorney fees

Questions Presented:
1. Does litigation-induced voluntary conduct, without a judgment, consent decree, or comparable judicially enforceable relief, make the plaintiff a prevailing party?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes the administrability and statutory-source grounds for requiring judicially enforceable relief |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Would reverse | Would preserve catalyst-based fees when the litigation materially caused the defendant to provide the relief sought |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: prevailing-party status requires a judicially enforceable disposition materially altering the parties’ legal relationship. Litigation-induced voluntary compliance, without such relief, is insufficient.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Prevailing-party status after voluntary compliance

Holding and operative rule: A plaintiff is a prevailing party only when a judgment, consent decree, enforceable court-backed settlement, or comparable judicial disposition materially alters the parties’ legal relationship. A defendant’s voluntary change in conduct, even when caused by the litigation, does not by itself confer prevailing-party status.

Reasoning: The fee-shifting provisions use prevailing party as a legal term tied to relief that changes legal rights and obligations through an enforceable judicial act. A merits judgment and a consent decree qualify because each gives the prevailing party relief backed by judicial authority. Nominal damages, an injunction, or qualifying declaratory relief may likewise suffice when legally operative. Voluntary legislative or administrative change differs because it leaves no judicially enforceable obligation between the parties. Making causation alone sufficient would require collateral litigation over why a defendant acted, whether the lawsuit was necessary, and how much of the requested relief it produced, without an enacted standard governing those inquiries. Buckhannon’s facilities obtained the practical benefit sought after West Virginia changed its law, but the merits action became moot before any judgment or court-backed disposition altered the parties’ legal relationship. The fee denial therefore must be affirmed.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The judicial-relief requirement follows both from the ordinary legal meaning of prevailing party and from the need for an administrable rule. A motive-centered catalyst inquiry would invite extensive collateral litigation without clear statutory authorization.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer: A plaintiff has prevailed when the suit was a substantial cause of the defendant’s provision of the requested relief, even if the defendant acts before judgment. They would permit a carefully administered catalyst inquiry and reverse the fee denial.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Friends of the Earth, Inc. v. Laidlaw Environmental Services (TOC), Inc.*: clarified; its reservation of the catalyst-fee question is resolved against prevailing-party status based solely on voluntary compliance.  
*Farrar v. Hobby*: followed; judicial relief materially altering the legal relationship is required, and nominal damages may qualify.  
*Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America*: applied; a private arrangement lacking incorporation, retained enforcement jurisdiction, or comparable judicial backing ordinarily lacks the enforceability required here.  
*Maher v. Gagne*: clarified; a consent decree may support fees because it is judicially enforceable, not merely because the litigation caused a settlement.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit denying prevailing-party attorney fees is affirmed.  
Effect: Buckhannon may not obtain statutory prevailing-party fees on the catalyst theory presented. Sanctions, bad-faith fees, and fees based on a qualifying judgment, consent decree, nominal-damages award, or other judicially enforceable relief remain governed by their independent sources.

### NLRB v. Kentucky River Community Care, Inc.

Argued February 21, 2001 | Decided May 29, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: enforcement of the Board’s bargaining order denied and the registered nurses treated as supervisors, 193 F.3d 444 (6th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/706/))  
Area of Law: Labor law; Supervisory status

Questions Presented:
1. Who bears the burden of proving supervisory status under NLRA § 2(11)?
2. Is professional or technical judgment categorically outside the statutory term independent judgment?

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded. The component votes are stated below.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Supervisory-status burden | Reversed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The employer bears the burden of proving the statutory exclusion. |
| Rejection of the Board’s categorical interpretation and nonenforcement of the existing order | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Professional origin does not categorically remove judgment from § 2(11). |
| Conclusive classification of the nurses as supervisors | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The Board must make the first fact-sensitive application of the corrected rule. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer as to Question 1; O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to Question 2; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to Question 3 | Supports the judgment by component | The issue propositions stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins Questions 1 and 2; would affirm the conclusive supervisory-status determination |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins Questions 1 and 3; would sustain the Board’s professional-judgment interpretation |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm rejection of the Board’s categorical interpretation and nonenforcement of the existing order, to reverse the burden and conclusive-status rulings, and to remand for Board application.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court, with different majorities joining its three controlling components.  
Announced ground: the employer bears the statutory burden; professional origin neither excludes nor establishes independent managerial judgment; and the Board must determine in the first instance whether these nurses actually exercised listed supervisory authority.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Burden of proving supervisory status

Holding and operative rule: The party asserting that an employee is excluded from NLRA protection as a supervisor bears the burden of proving every element of supervisory status under § 2(11). (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Supervisory status removes an otherwise covered worker from the Act’s employee protections. The employer ordinarily possesses the records, job descriptions, delegation practices, disciplinary evidence, and operational information bearing on the claimed authority. Requiring the employer to prove the exclusion therefore accords with the statutory structure and ordinary allocation of proof concerning an asserted exception. The Sixth Circuit erred insofar as it placed the burden on the Board or the employees. The allocation does not establish that the nurses are employees, however. Kentucky River may prevail by proving that they exercised one or more of the listed powers in the employer’s interest through genuinely independent judgment.

##### Question 2: Professional judgment and independent judgment

Holding and operative rule: Judgment does not fall outside § 2(11) merely because it originates in professional or technical expertise. Professional judgment may count when it is exercised through genuine statutory management authority, but professional status, skilled patient care, or routine direction alone does not establish supervisory status. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: The statutory term independent judgment describes the character of the employee’s exercise of listed authority, not the disciplinary source of the employee’s knowledge. A categorical rule excluding professional or technical judgment would remove language Congress enacted and could exclude genuine front-line managers merely because they rely on expertise. The opposite categorical rule is equally unsound. Nurses do not become supervisors simply because they direct aides as an incident of treating patients, allocate routine tasks under established protocols, or possess greater skill. The inquiry remains whether the employee independently exercises authority to assign, responsibly direct, discipline, recommend discipline, adjust grievances, or perform another listed function in a way that aligns the employee with management.

##### Question 3: First application of the corrected rule

Holding and operative rule: After invalidating the Board’s categorical interpretation, a reviewing court may not conclusively classify the employees as supervisors when material application and factfinding remain. The Board must apply the corrected statutory distinction in the first instance. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Whether the nurses exercised genuine supervisory authority depends on the actual scope of their assignments, accountability, discretion, disciplinary influence, and relationship to management. The existing order rests on a legally defective categorical rule, but that defect does not establish the employer’s factual case. The Sixth Circuit therefore properly declined to enforce the order yet went too far by treating the nurses as supervisors conclusively. Administrative law assigns the initial application of the governing statute to the Board on a record evaluated under the correct burden. Remand preserves the distinction recognized in *NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America* between skilled professional direction and genuine labor-management authority.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that the employer bears the burden and that professional judgment is not categorically excluded from § 2(11). They would hold that the record establishes supervisory status and would not return the matter to the Board.

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree with the burden allocation and with remanding rather than declaring the nurses supervisors. They would sustain the Board’s authority to distinguish judgment exercised as part of professional patient care from independent managerial judgment under the Act.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America*: clarified; professional or technical origin neither categorically excludes judgment from § 2(11) nor independently establishes supervisory status, and the operative distinction remains genuine management authority.  
*SEC v. Chenery Corp.*: followed; the Board, rather than a reviewing court, must make the first fact-sensitive application of the corrected statutory rule.  
NLRA § 2(11): clarified; the party asserting supervisory exclusion bears the burden of proof.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it denies enforcement of the existing Board order, reversed as to the burden allocation and conclusive supervisory-status determination, and remanded with instructions to return the matter to the Board for application of the corrected rule.  
Effect: Kentucky River must prove that the nurses exercised one or more listed supervisory powers through independent managerial judgment. Neither professional status nor routine patient-care direction alone resolves that inquiry.

### PGA TOUR, Inc. v. Martin

Argued January 17, 2001 | Decided May 29, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: permanent injunction requiring an individualized cart accommodation affirmed, 204 F.3d 994 (9th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/661/))  
Area of Law: Disability access; Reasonable modification

Questions Presented:
1. Does Title III cover access to PGA tournaments and require an individualized cart modification when the modification is reasonable and does not fundamentally alter the competition?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Would treat the walking rule as part of the competition defined by the Tour and outside judicial modification |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Individualized modification of tournament access

Holding and operative rule: A covered public accommodation must make an individualized reasonable modification for a qualified person with a disability unless the operator proves that the modification would fundamentally alter an essential feature of the offered activity. On this record, allowing Martin to use a cart does not fundamentally alter the professional golf competition.

Reasoning: The Tour stages qualifying and tournament play at golf courses and offers competitors access to the privileges and services of those facilities. Title III therefore reaches the terms on which an otherwise qualified competitor participates. The statute does not require abandonment of essential competitive rules, alteration of the skill being tested, or acceptance of a material safety risk. It does require individualized consideration rather than categorical exclusion. The lower courts found that Martin’s disability substantially limits walking, that walking the course imposes fatigue beyond that ordinarily experienced by other competitors, and that use of a cart would permit access without changing the shotmaking, course management, concentration, or other golf skills determining the competition. PGA TOUR did not prove that the walking requirement, as applied to Martin, supplies an essential competitive test that the cart would remove. The permanent individualized injunction therefore remains proper.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The competition includes the rules by which the Tour has chosen to define professional tournament golf, including walking. They would not authorize courts to separate essential from nonessential athletic rules through individualized review and would reverse the injunction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Southeastern Community College v. Davis*: applied; a modification is not required when it fundamentally alters the activity, but reasonable access measures that preserve its essential features remain mandatory.  
*Bragdon v. Abbott*: applied; disability and modification questions require individualized assessment rather than categorical assumptions.  
ADA Title III: clarified; its reasonable-modification duty applies to the terms on which qualified competitors obtain access to tournament services offered at covered facilities.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit affirming the individualized permanent injunction is affirmed.  
Effect: PGA TOUR must permit Martin to use a golf cart under the circumstances adjudicated. The judgment establishes no general entitlement to carts or other rule waivers for materially different disabilities, courses, competitive structures, safety conditions, or asserted essential features.

### United States v. Hilario

Argued March 22, 2001 | Decided May 29, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the indictment reversed, 218 F.3d 19 (1st Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/218/19/633317/))  
Area of Law: Separation of powers; Appointments Clause; Interim prosecutors

Questions Presented:
1. Is a United States Attorney an inferior officer whose appointment Congress may vest in a federal district court?
2. Does a judicial appointment continuing until the Executive fills the vacancy create unconstitutional encroachment, incompatibility, or interference with prosecutorial power?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Status of a United States Attorney

Holding and operative rule: A United States Attorney is an inferior officer because the office’s authority is territorially and statutorily bounded, the Attorney General directs and supervises prosecutorial work, and superior Executive officers retain authority to control and displace the appointee.

Reasoning: Inferior-officer status turns on the officer’s relationship to constitutionally appointed superiors, not on the importance of individual prosecutions. A United States Attorney exercises substantial federal authority, but only within an assigned district and within statutes and Department of Justice supervision. The Attorney General may direct litigation policy, require reports, reassign matters, and supervise the office. The President may remove the United States Attorney, and installation of a presidentially nominated and Senate-confirmed successor ends the interim appointment. Those structural constraints distinguish the district office from a principal officer possessing independent nationwide authority. Congress therefore may use the Appointments Clause’s inferior-officer options.

##### Question 2: Appointment by a district court and duration of service

Holding and operative rule: Congress may vest appointment of an interim United States Attorney in a federal district court. Service until an Executive replacement is installed does not transfer prosecutorial power to the judiciary when judges possess no authority to direct charges, supervise prosecutions, or prevent Executive removal or replacement.

Reasoning: The Appointments Clause expressly permits Congress to vest appointment of inferior officers in the Courts of Law. That authority does not require the appointee’s duties to be judicial, although an appointment incompatible with the judiciary’s constitutional role could present a separate problem. Section 546(d) assigns only the appointment needed to prevent a prolonged vacancy. Once appointed, the United States Attorney remains within the Executive Branch, answers to Department supervision, exercises prosecutorial judgment independently of the appointing judges, and serves only until displaced through the Executive appointment process. The statute gives the district court no charging, litigation, removal, or supervisory authority. The possibility of recusal in a particular case does not make the appointment structure unconstitutional. The First Circuit therefore properly reinstated the prosecution.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Edmond v. United States*: applied; supervision, direction, bounded authority, and removability establish inferior-officer status.  
*Ex parte Siebold*: followed; Congress may vest appointment of an inferior officer in a Court of Law when the assignment is not incompatible with judicial office.  
Appointments Clause separation principles: clarified; judicial appointment does not amount to judicial exercise of the appointed officer’s executive power.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed, and the criminal prosecution shall proceed in the district court.  
Effect: The interim United States Attorney’s appointment and continued service under § 546(d) do not require dismissal of the indictment. Actual judicial direction of prosecution, a statute insulating the prosecutor from Executive supervision or displacement, and case-specific recusal remain outside the holding.

### Penry v. Johnson

Argued March 27, 2001 | Decided June 4, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: federal habeas relief denied, 215 F.3d 504 (5th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/782/))  
Area of Law: Capital habeas corpus; Mitigation; Psychiatric evidence

Questions Presented:
1. Did the supplemental capital instruction permit the jury to give Penry’s mitigating evidence full effect?
2. Does the separate psychiatric-report claim satisfy AEDPA’s requirements for federal habeas relief?

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mitigation-instruction claim | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | A conditional sentence-only writ shall issue unless Texas conducts a constitutionally adequate new penalty proceeding. |
| Psychiatric-report claim | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Habeas relief remains denied on this claim. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to the psychiatric-report holding | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion and partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | Scalia | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Supports affirmance of the psychiatric-report ruling but would also affirm denial of the mitigation claim |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm denial of the psychiatric-report claim, reverse denial of the mitigation claim, and remand for a conditional sentence-only writ.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Full effect for mitigating evidence

Holding and operative rule: A capital instruction violates clearly established mitigation law when its commands leave the jury without a lawful and comprehensible vehicle for giving constitutionally relevant mitigating evidence full effect. A state court unreasonably applies that law when it sustains a supplemental instruction whose operative directions contradict the special-issue structure and the defect prejudices the sentencing decision.

Reasoning: *Penry v. Lynaugh* required Texas to provide a vehicle through which jurors could express the reasoned moral effect of mitigating evidence that did not fit the statutory special issues. The supplemental instruction used at Penry’s retrial purported to supply that vehicle, but it directed jurors to answer the special issues truthfully and also suggested that they could give mitigating effect by returning an answer inconsistent with those truthful findings. The conflict did not clearly authorize the jury to select life because of Penry’s evidence. Under *Williams v. Taylor*, the state court’s approval was not merely incorrect but objectively unreasonable in light of the governing mitigation principle. As in *Weeks v. Angelone*, a presumption that jurors follow instructions cannot cure directions that themselves leave the life-or-death decision without a clear lawful path. The defect had a substantial and injurious effect on the sentence, requiring sentence-limited relief.

##### Question 2: Psychiatric-report claim

Holding and operative rule: Penry’s psychiatric-report claim does not establish that the state court’s decision was contrary to or an unreasonable application of clearly established federal law on the supplied record.

Reasoning: AEDPA review is claim specific. The report issue arose in a materially different evidentiary setting from the compelled, uncounseled examination and direct prosecution use addressed by the closest clearly established authority. Penry has not shown that the Supreme Court’s existing cases dictated exclusion under the circumstances in which the report was introduced and used here. The state court’s rejection of this separate claim therefore falls within the range permitted by § 2254(d)(1), even though the mitigation instruction independently requires sentencing relief. The conviction and the psychiatric-report ruling remain undisturbed.

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that the psychiatric-report claim fails under AEDPA. They would hold that the supplemental instruction gave the jury a constitutionally sufficient means of considering mitigation and would affirm the death sentence.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Penry v. Lynaugh*: followed; a capital sentencer must possess a lawful vehicle for giving constitutionally relevant mitigating evidence full effect.  
*Weeks v. Angelone*: applied; demonstrated instructional contradiction or unresolved confusion cannot be cured by an abstract presumption that jurors follow instructions.  
*Williams v. Taylor*: applied; § 2254(d)(1) permits relief for an objectively unreasonable application of clearly established law, not merely for legal error.  
*Estelle v. Smith*: distinguished; the psychiatric report was used in a materially different setting that did not make the state judgment objectively unreasonable.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed as to the psychiatric-report claim, reversed as to the mitigation-instruction claim, and remanded with instructions to direct a conditional writ unless Texas conducts a constitutionally adequate new penalty proceeding within a reasonable period or imposes a lawful noncapital sentence.  
Effect: Penry’s conviction remains in force, but the existing death sentence may not. Texas retains authority to conduct a new penalty proceeding under constitutionally adequate instructions, and the Court prescribes no required verbal formula.

### Pollard v. E. I. du Pont de Nemours & Co.

Argued April 23, 2001 | Decided June 4, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: front pay treated as compensatory damages subject to the statutory cap, 213 F.3d 933 (6th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/843/))  
Area of Law: Title VII remedies; Front pay  
Participation: O’Connor took no part in the consideration or decision of the case; the basis was not publicly stated.

Questions Presented:
1. Is front pay awarded instead of reinstatement equitable relief authorized under Title VII § 706(g) and therefore outside § 1981a(b)(3)’s damages cap?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice. Took no part: O’Connor.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Classification of front pay

Holding and operative rule: Front pay awarded as a substitute for reinstatement is equitable relief authorized by Title VII § 706(g) and is not compensatory damages subject to § 1981a(b)(3)’s cap. The amount and duration remain matters for reasoned equitable determination.

Reasoning: Before enactment of the Civil Rights Act of 1991, courts used front pay as a make-whole substitute when reinstatement was unavailable, inappropriate, or infeasible. Section 1981a added compensatory and punitive damages while expressly preserving relief authorized by § 706(g). Although § 1981a refers generally to future pecuniary losses, reading that phrase to absorb front pay would bring a preexisting equitable remedy within the new cap despite Congress’s preservation of § 706(g) relief. Front pay addresses the prospective earnings consequences of the unlawful employment decision for the period reasonably necessary to restore the plaintiff to the position discrimination denied. It is not automatic, and it may not become speculative, duplicative, or perpetual. The Sixth Circuit therefore erred by treating Pollard’s front-pay award as capped compensatory damages.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Albemarle Paper Co. v. Moody*: followed; Title VII authorizes make-whole equitable relief fitted to the consequences of proven discrimination.  
Title VII § 706(g): clarified; front pay may substitute for reinstatement when equitable circumstances warrant.  
42 U.S.C. § 1981a(b)(3): clarified; its compensatory-damages cap does not encompass front pay authorized under § 706(g).

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for treatment of front pay as uncapped equitable relief under § 706(g).  
Effect: Pollard’s front-pay remedy may not be reduced merely by application of § 1981a(b)(3)’s cap. The lower courts retain responsibility for determining a reasonable amount and duration and for preventing speculation, duplication, or recovery extending beyond the period equitable relief can support.

### Gibbs v. Babbitt

Argued April 17, 2001 | Decided June 4, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: application of the Endangered Species Act’s taking prohibition to reintroduced red wolves affirmed, 214 F.3d 483 (4th Cir. 2000). ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/gibbs-v-babbitt-no-894519521))  
Area of Law: Commerce Clause; Environmental law; Wildlife regulation

Questions Presented:
1. May Congress prohibit the taking of a purely intrastate, noncommercial endangered species under the Commerce Clause?
2. What limiting principle and evidentiary nexus are required when federal authority rests on aggregate effects or the regulation’s place in a comprehensive national scheme?

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Accepts the interstate-externality and anti-impairment framework while favoring broader deference to supported congressional judgments |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Would hold the prohibition beyond Congress’s commerce authority as applied to noneconomic local taking |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: noneconomic intrastate taking is not regulable merely because wildlife has economic associations or because the prohibition appears within a comprehensive statute. Federal authority requires proof of material impairment of a valid interstate regime or a substantial interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Commerce authority over noneconomic intrastate taking

Holding and operative rule: Noneconomic intrastate taking is not regulable merely because the actor has an economic motive, wildlife supports tourism or research, or the prohibition forms part of a comprehensive statute. Congress may reach the conduct when excluding it would materially impair or permit evasion of a valid interstate regulatory regime, or when the taking produces a substantial interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg)

Reasoning: *National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt* establishes that noneconomic character is relevant but not categorically dispositive. The Commerce Clause inquiry must identify the operative interstate mechanism rather than collect economic consequences at a high level of generality. Protecting livestock does not transform killing a wolf into commercial activity. Tourism, scientific interest, biodiversity, possible pelt value, and the national scope of the ESA likewise do not independently establish federal power. Congress may, however, protect an interstate regime against evasion or material impairment, and it may address a materially supported cross-border externality that no single State can reasonably internalize. Those routes preserve national authority over genuine interstate problems without converting every local interaction with wildlife into commerce.

##### Question 2: Evidentiary nexus and the red-wolf record

Holding and operative rule: The Government must identify and materially support the interstate mechanism on which the challenged application depends. Because the lower courts did not determine material impairment, anti-evasion necessity, actual cross-border population effects, or the States’ capacity to internalize the asserted externality, the judgment must be vacated and the case remanded.

Reasoning: The record includes evidence concerning reintroduction, interstate wolf movement, tourism, research, livestock protection, and the integrated federal recovery program. Those facts may bear on the governing routes, but they are not substitute findings. An animal’s crossing of a state line does not permanently convert every later local taking into regulation of a thing in interstate commerce. Nor does the ESA’s comprehensive character establish that excluding every local taking would materially impair an otherwise valid interstate regime. The lower courts must identify the shared population or interstate system at issue, determine how the regulated class affects it, assess whether state-by-state regulation can internalize the consequences, and evaluate the materiality of the claimed impairment. Appellate inference cannot supply those missing determinations.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Breyer, concurring in the judgment: They agree that the governing interstate mechanism must be identified and that the existing findings are insufficiently focused. They would afford broader deference to materially supported congressional and agency judgments concerning shared wildlife populations and integrated recovery programs.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Regulation of a noneconomic local taking of wildlife lies beyond Congress’s commerce authority, regardless of aggregate biodiversity, tourism, or programmatic effects. They would reverse and direct judgment against the challenged federal application rather than remand for additional findings.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt*: followed and reaffirmed; noneconomic intrastate conduct requires a materially supported anti-evasion, material-impairment, or substantial-interstate-externality basis.  
*United States v. Lopez*: applied; generalized chains through economic motive, tourism, research, or possible future value do not replace an identified interstate mechanism.  
*Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon*: distinguished; its statutory construction of harm does not establish Commerce Clause authority for every application of the taking prohibition.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for findings concerning material impairment, anti-evasion necessity, actual cross-border population effects, the asserted interstate externality, and the States’ capacity to internalize it.  
Effect: The Court enters no final judgment that the red-wolf taking prohibition is constitutional or unconstitutional as applied. The application may be sustained only if the Government proves one of the controlling interstate mechanisms on a materially adequate record.

### Cedric Kushner Promotions, Ltd. v. King

Argued April 18, 2001 | Decided June 11, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: civil RICO claim dismissed for lack of person-enterprise distinctness, 219 F.3d 115 (2d Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/158/))  
Area of Law: Civil RICO; Person-enterprise distinctness

Questions Presented:
1. Is a natural-person owner or employee legally distinct from the separately incorporated entity whose affairs he allegedly conducts through racketeering?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Distinctness between a natural person and a corporation

Holding and operative rule: A natural-person owner or employee is legally distinct from a separately incorporated enterprise and may be liable under 18 U.S.C. § 1962(c) for conducting that corporation’s affairs through a pattern of racketeering. Corporate separateness establishes only the distinctness element and does not prove any other element of liability.

Reasoning: Section 1962(c) distinguishes the person liable from the enterprise whose affairs are conducted. A natural person and a corporation are separate legal actors even when the person owns all or most of the corporation, serves as its principal employee, or exercises extensive control over it. Treating that relationship as legally identical would disregard the corporate form for the sole purpose of defeating a statutory element that the form otherwise supplies. The complaint alleges that King, a natural person, conducted the affairs of his separately incorporated promotion company through racketeering activity. That allegation satisfies person-enterprise distinctness at the pleading stage. It does not establish operation or management, qualifying predicate acts, a pattern, causation, injury to business or property, damages, or any disputed defense. The Second Circuit therefore erred by dismissing the claim solely for want of distinctness.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler*: applied; civil RICO analysis follows the enacted elements without adding an extra categorical limitation.  
*Reves v. Ernst & Young*: applied; the plaintiff must still prove that the defendant participated in operating or managing the enterprise’s affairs.  
18 U.S.C. § 1962(c): clarified; a natural person is distinct from a separately incorporated enterprise even when the person owns or controls it.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the remaining civil RICO elements and defenses.  
Effect: The action may proceed past the person-enterprise distinctness objection. Ownership or corporate control alone establishes no racketeering liability, and Kushner Promotions retains the burden of proving operation or management, predicates, pattern, causation, statutory injury, and damages.

### Good News Club v. Milford Central School

Argued February 28, 2001 | Decided June 11, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: exclusion of the religious club from the after-school forum sustained, 202 F.3d 502 (2d Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/98/))  
Area of Law: Free Speech Clause; Establishment Clause; Religious viewpoint access

Questions Presented:
1. May a school exclude an otherwise eligible after-school club because its instruction in morals and character employs prayer, Bible lessons, and an evangelical religious viewpoint?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes the viewpoint character of the exclusion and the distinction between private speech and government worship |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Would permit the exclusion because of the students’ age, the devotional character of the meetings, and perceived school attribution |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Religious viewpoint access and attribution

Holding and operative rule: A public school that opens a limited after-hours forum for private instruction in morals and character may not exclude an otherwise eligible club because its private expression presents that subject from a religious viewpoint. Neutral access does not establish religion when the school neither finances, selects, controls, nor attributes the private message and workable attribution safeguards remain available.

Reasoning: Milford defined the forum to include private instruction concerning character and moral development. The Good News Club addresses those same subjects through prayer, Bible stories, and an evangelical understanding of moral conduct. Excluding the Club because its treatment is religious therefore discriminates by viewpoint rather than enforcing a neutral subject-matter boundary. The elementary age of the participants and the meetings’ devotional content are relevant to attribution, but they do not convert private speech into governmental worship. The meetings occur after instructional hours, participation depends on parental consent, private adults direct the activities, and the school provides neither public financing nor curricular control. Neutral access rules, clear identification of private sponsorship, and reasonable disclaimers may address genuine confusion. The Establishment Clause does not require the school to suppress an eligible private viewpoint in order to avoid misattribution that narrower measures can prevent.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Once Milford permits private instruction on morals and character, religious treatment of those subjects is a viewpoint the school may not suppress. Neutral access for a private group is not government-led religious exercise.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg: The meetings combine moral instruction with worship directed to young elementary students in their own school. They would permit Milford to treat that activity as outside the forum or to exclude it to avoid a substantial risk of perceived school sponsorship.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue*: followed; a school may not exclude religious treatment of character and moral development from an otherwise open private-access program.  
*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: applied; religious perspective is a protected viewpoint when the underlying subject is otherwise permitted.  
*Freedom From Religion Foundation, Inc. v. City of Marshfield*, decided May 21, 2001: applied; attribution depends on the arrangement’s practical operation, and effective private identification or another workable boundary may preserve private religious expression without governmental sponsorship.  
*Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette*: followed; neutral access for private religious speech does not itself establish religion.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for entry of relief providing the Good News Club equal after-hours access under neutral terms, with reasonable disclaimers or other effective attribution safeguards if needed.  
Effect: Milford may not exclude the Club because its instruction presents permitted subjects from a religious viewpoint. The judgment does not authorize school-led prayer, public financing of devotional instruction, curricular worship, compelled attendance, teacher control, or attribution of the Club’s message to the school.

### Kyllo v. United States

Argued February 20, 2001 | Decided June 11, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: warrantless thermal imaging of the home held not to be a search, 190 F.3d 1041 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/27/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Technology; The home

Questions Presented:
1. Is use of sense-enhancing technology not in general public use to obtain otherwise inaccessible information about a home’s interior a search?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | O’Connor, Kennedy | Would affirm | Would distinguish detection of heat leaving the exterior from acquisition of interior details |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Technological acquisition of interior-home information

Holding and operative rule: Government conducts a Fourth Amendment search when it uses sense-enhancing technology not in general public use to obtain otherwise inaccessible information about the interior of a home. The protection turns on the method and location of revelation, not on a judicial assessment that the particular interior detail is insufficiently intimate.

Reasoning: The home stands at the core of the Fourth Amendment. Physical entry is not the only means by which government can obtain information the constitutional boundary protects. A contrary rule would allow advances in surveillance technology to erode home privacy without any corresponding constitutional limit. The thermal imager was aimed at Kyllo’s residence from a public location and revealed heat patterns that ordinary observation could not obtain, allowing officers to draw information about activity within the home. Because the device was not in general public use, its employment to obtain that otherwise inaccessible interior information constituted a search. Courts need not classify each revealed detail as intimate before the warrant requirement applies, because that inquiry would provide no stable protection as technology develops. The Ninth Circuit’s no-search rule must therefore be reversed.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by O’Connor and Kennedy: The device detected heat emitted from the exterior rather than exposing visual or auditory details inside the home. They would treat the observations as analogous to sensing smoke, odor, or other physical effects reaching a lawful public vantage and would affirm.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Katz v. United States*: extended; technologically obtaining otherwise inaccessible interior-home information may constitute a search without physical trespass.  
*Silverman v. United States*: followed; the Fourth Amendment protects information within the home against technologically assisted intrusion as well as physical penetration.  
*California v. Ciraolo*: distinguished; ordinary visual observation from a lawful vantage differs from technological acquisition of otherwise inaccessible interior information.  
*Florida v. Riley*: distinguished; observation using commonly available means does not control technology outside general public use directed at the home’s interior.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for reassessment of warrant sufficiency, independent source, and suppression without reliance on the thermal-imaging information.  
Effect: The thermal scan may not be treated as warrantless nonsearch activity. Ordinary observation, technology in general public use, surveillance that does not obtain otherwise inaccessible interior-home information, and the consequences of excising the thermal evidence remain governed by their own requirements.

---

**Sources:**

- [Buckhannon Board & Care Home, Inc. v. West Virginia Department of Health and Human Resources | 532 U.S. 598 (2001) | Justia U.S. Supreme Court Center](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/598/)
- [United States of America, Appellant, v. Fermin Hilario, Defendant, Appellee, 218 F.3d 19 (1st Cir. 2000) :: Justia](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/218/19/633317/)
- [Gibbs v. Babbitt (Gibbs v. Babbitt, 214 F.3d 483 (4th Cir. 1999)) - vLex United States](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/gibbs-v-babbitt-no-894519521)
