Source correction: The official record in *Nevada v. Hicks* identifies the court below as the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 196 F.3d 1020 (9th Cir. 1999), not the Supreme Court of Nevada. This correction does not affect the granted question, Stone’s approved position, or the available disposition. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-353.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Tuan Anh Nguyen v. INS

Argued January 9, 2001 | Decided June 11, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: citizenship claim rejected and removal sustained, 208 F.3d 528 (5th Cir. 2000). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-53.pdf))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; Citizenship; Sex classifications

Questions Presented:
1. Do the different citizenship-transmission requirements imposed on unmarried citizen fathers and citizen mothers violate equal protection?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Sex-based citizenship-transmission requirements

Holding and operative rule: Different citizenship-transmission burdens for unmarried citizen fathers and citizen mothers violate equal protection when important interests in reliable parentage and a meaningful parental connection can be served through timely, administrable, sex-neutral requirements. Biological differences may be considered only when they materially bear on the specific governmental function.

Reasoning: *Miller v. Albright*, decided April 22, 1998, already establishes that Congress may not use sex as a substitute for proof of parentage or a meaningful parent-child relationship when neutral methods can serve those interests. Assuring biological parentage and a genuine connection to the citizen parent are important objectives. The statute nevertheless places materially different acknowledgment and relationship burdens on fathers and mothers as universal legal classes. The fact that maternity ordinarily is established through birth may justify a different evidentiary method directed to that fact, but it does not justify every additional paternal burden or a general assumption that only fathers must demonstrate a relationship. Congress may require reliable documentation, timely acknowledgment, residence, support, or evidence of an actual connection on sex-neutral terms. Because the Fifth Circuit sustained the unequal scheme as such, its judgment must be reversed. The Court does not itself find every statutory prerequisite satisfied or confer citizenship without the necessary sex-neutral adjudication. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-53.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens: The statutory distinction rests on biological circumstances bearing directly on proof of parentage and the opportunity for a relationship at birth. He would hold the available acknowledgment options sufficiently fitted to Congress’s interests and affirm.

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Even assuming a constitutional defect, citizenship may be conferred only on the terms enacted by Congress. They would affirm rather than direct a judicially constructed alternative path to citizenship.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Miller v. Albright*, decided April 22, 1998: followed; materially different acknowledgment and relationship requirements may not rest on sex stereotypes when reliable sex-neutral requirements can serve the same parentage and connection interests.  
*United States v. Virginia*, 518 U.S. 515 (1996): applied; important governmental interests must be served through substantially related, nonstereotyped means.  
*Lehr v. Robertson*, 463 U.S. 248 (1983): distinguished; recognition of a biological difference relevant to proving parentage does not authorize avoidable, universally different legal burdens on citizen fathers and mothers.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for sex-neutral adjudication of Nguyen’s citizenship claim under reliable and timely proof and relationship requirements.  
Effect: The present sex-differentiated statutory burdens may not control the citizenship determination. Citizenship is not established automatically, and the responsible decisionmaker must determine whether the lawful neutral requirements are satisfied.

### Wallis v. Spencer

Argued April 23, 2001 | Decided June 11, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment reversed and constitutional claims remanded, 202 F.3d 1126 (9th Cir. 2000). ([Law Resource Guide](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/202/202.F3d.1126.html))  
Area of Law: Family integrity; Fourth Amendment; Emergency child protection

Questions Presented:
1. When may officials remove a child from parental custody without prior judicial authorization?
2. Does protective custody itself authorize an invasive forensic examination?

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the reversal of summary judgment and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Emergency removal without prior authorization

Holding and operative rule: Officials may remove a child without prior judicial authorization only when specific and articulable facts provide reasonable cause to believe that the child faces imminent serious bodily injury during the time reasonably required to obtain authorization. The scope and duration of the intrusion must remain bounded by that danger.

Reasoning: Parents and children possess a protected interest in remaining together, and removal of a child also constitutes a seizure of the child’s person. A genuine emergency may make advance process impracticable, but generalized suspicion, an allegation of past abuse, or the seriousness of the subject alone does not establish the required temporal exigency. Officials must possess concrete information supporting imminent serious harm before judicial approval reasonably can be obtained, and they must use measures proportionate to that immediate risk. The record contains material disputes concerning what the officers knew, the reliability and urgency of the allegations, the availability of less intrusive protective arrangements, and whether the removal continued beyond any emergency. Those disputes cannot be resolved against the family on summary judgment. The district court must determine the relevant facts and address causation, municipal responsibility, and any defendant-specific immunity separately. ([Law Resource Guide](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/202/202.F3d.1126.html))

##### Question 2: Invasive forensic examination after removal

Holding and operative rule: Protective custody alone does not authorize an invasive forensic examination undertaken principally to gather evidence. Absent a medical emergency or material risk that evidence will be lost before authorization can be obtained, valid parental consent or prior judicial authorization ordinarily is required.

Reasoning: Authority to secure a child against an immediate danger does not create unrestricted authority to conduct a separate bodily search for investigative purposes. An invasive examination implicates the child’s privacy and bodily integrity, as well as the parents’ continuing role in medical decisions and support. A medical emergency may justify immediate treatment, and a genuine risk of evidence loss may justify prompt judicially supervised action. Where the suspected parent cannot supply valid consent, officials may seek authorization from a neutral judicial officer rather than treat the child’s custodial status as consent. The record permits a finding that the examinations were investigatory, highly intrusive, unsupported by an immediate medical need, and performed without notice, consent, or prior judicial approval. Summary judgment therefore was improper, but the Supreme Court enters no finding of liability or damages. ([Law Resource Guide](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/202/202.F3d.1126.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Officials responding to credible child-abuse allegations must retain substantial latitude to remove children and obtain medically reasonable examinations without first conducting a judicial proceeding. They would hold the challenged protective actions reasonable on the record presented and reverse the Ninth Circuit.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Albright v. Oliver*, 510 U.S. 266 (1994): clarified; the children’s seizure and bodily-examination claims are governed by the directly relevant Fourth Amendment source, while the parents’ family-integrity claim remains independently protected.  
*Parham v. J. R.*, 442 U.S. 584 (1979): clarified; protective custody does not itself eliminate the parental role in nonemergency invasive medical decisions.  
U.S. Const. amends. IV and XIV: clarified; emergency child removal requires a temporally specific danger, and protective custody does not independently authorize an evidentiary bodily examination.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for defendant-specific factfinding, causation, municipal-liability, immunity, and remedial determinations under the stated constitutional boundaries.  
Effect: The claims may proceed beyond summary judgment. No final damages judgment is entered, no emergency response is prohibited when imminent serious danger exists, and no absolute parental veto applies when a parent is suspected or unable to consent.

### Saucier v. Katz

Argued March 20, 2001 | Decided June 18, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: denial of qualified immunity affirmed, 194 F.3d 962 (9th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-194.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Excessive force; Qualified immunity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the force alleged was objectively unreasonable under the Fourth Amendment.
2. What sequence governs the constitutional-merits and qualified-immunity inquiries, and whether materially identical precedent is required.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Souter.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas in full; Souter as to the qualified-immunity sequence and clearly-established-law propositions | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion and partial join as stated |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | Stevens, Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Agrees that the alleged force was reasonable and that exact factual precedent is not categorically required, but rejects a separate second inquiry in ordinary excessive-force cases |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the sequence and fair-notice propositions but would remand the excessive-force merits rather than direct judgment |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand with instructions to enter judgment for Saucier on the individual-capacity claim.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Force used in the protective-security setting

Holding and operative rule: On the plaintiff-supported facts, the limited force promptly used to remove Katz from an uncertain protective-security area was objectively reasonable under the Fourth Amendment. The record establishes no gratuitous force after control or other conduct exceeding the immediate security need.

Reasoning: Objective reasonableness accounts for the circumstances confronting the officer rather than later opportunities for a more measured response. Katz displayed a protest banner near a restricted event attended by the Vice President, and military police had to remove him promptly from the protected area while the number, purpose, and intentions of nearby persons remained uncertain. On Katz’s account, Saucier half-walked and half-dragged him away and shoved him into a vehicle, causing limited injury. That account describes force connected to completing the removal and does not show gratuitous force after Katz was secured. The Fourth Amendment does not require officers protecting a high-level official to select the least forceful conceivable technique. Because the plaintiff-supported facts establish no constitutional violation, the individual-capacity claim fails without a separate immunity determination. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-194.pdf))

##### Question 2: Merits-first sequence and clearly established law

Holding and operative rule: A court deciding qualified immunity should first determine whether the plaintiff-supported facts establish a constitutional violation and should reach clearly established law only if a violation exists. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas) Clearly established law does not categorically require a materially identical prior case; governing principles, obviousness, training, and the official’s own conduct may provide constitutionally sufficient notice. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Merits and immunity perform different legal functions. The constitutional inquiry identifies the conduct prohibited by the governing source, while qualified immunity asks whether an official had fair notice of that prohibition when acting. Deciding the merits first prevents the law from remaining indefinitely undeveloped and avoids calling conduct immune without determining whether it was lawful. If no violation occurred, the litigation ends without an immunity ruling. If a violation is established, fair notice must be assessed from the complete body of materially relevant law under *Elder v. Holloway*, not through a categorical demand for a case with identical facts. Specific precedent may be necessary when the governing boundary is uncertain, but obvious misconduct can be clearly prohibited by established principles without a factual duplicate. The sequence does not merge the two questions or authorize appellate reweighing of disputed evidence.

#### Concurrences

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens and Breyer: They agree that the force alleged was objectively reasonable and that judgment should be entered for Saucier. In ordinary excessive-force cases, however, the same objective-reasonableness determination often resolves the controversy, so they would not require courts to conduct a duplicative second inquiry.

Souter, concurring in part and dissenting in part: He agrees that constitutional merits ordinarily should precede immunity and that fair notice does not demand an identical precedent. He concludes that Katz’s version of the force presents a question requiring further lower-court application and would not direct judgment for Saucier.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Graham v. Connor*, 490 U.S. 386 (1989): applied; force used to complete a prompt protective-security removal is assessed under context-specific objective reasonableness.  
*Siegert v. Gilley*, 500 U.S. 226 (1991): clarified; the constitutional-merits question ordinarily precedes the clearly-established-law inquiry.  
*Elder v. Holloway*, 510 U.S. 510 (1994): clarified; the complete body of materially relevant law supplies notice, and no categorical materially-identical-case requirement applies.  
*Anderson v. Creighton*, 483 U.S. 635 (1987): clarified; specificity concerns fair notice in context rather than a demand for factual duplication.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to enter judgment for Saucier on the individual-capacity excessive-force claim.  
Effect: Saucier faces no individual liability on the claim before the Court because the alleged conduct was constitutionally reasonable. Entity liability and independent claims not presented by the interlocutory judgment remain untouched.

### United States v. Mead Corp.

Argued November 8, 2000 | Decided June 18, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below: Customs’ classification independently rejected, 185 F.3d 1304 (Fed. Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-218.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; Statutory interpretation; Agency deference

Questions Presented:
1. What deference is due Customs’ informal tariff-classification letter?
2. Should the classification of Mead’s day planners as bound diaries be sustained?

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Legal weight of the classification letter

Holding and operative rule: An informal, transaction-specific agency classification letter that was not issued through delegated authority carrying the force of law does not control under *Chevron*. It receives only the persuasive weight earned by its reasoning, consistency, expertise, care, and fit with the enacted statute.

Reasoning: *Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. U.S. Army Corps of Engineers*, decided January 9, 2001, and *Whitman v. American Trucking Associations, Inc.*, decided February 27, 2001, require a court first to identify the statutory boundary through ordinary interpretation and then to determine the legal weight of the agency’s action. Customs classification letters may issue from numerous offices, ordinarily bind only the particular transaction, need not be published, and do not use notice-and-comment or another procedure indicating a congressional delegation to make controlling law. The letter therefore does not receive *Chevron* control. It is not irrelevant, because Customs possesses experience in administering the tariff schedule and may offer persuasive reasoning. The reviewing court must independently interpret the statutory terms and then evaluate the letter under the traditional persuasion-based considerations. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-218.pdf))

##### Question 2: Classification of the day planners

Not reached; the Federal Circuit must independently interpret the tariff provisions and assess the classification letter’s persuasive value under the governing standard.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia: An authoritative interpretation adopted by the agency charged with administering an ambiguous statute should receive *Chevron* deference even when issued without notice-and-comment rulemaking. He would uphold Customs’ construction and reverse the Federal Circuit’s contrary judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*, 467 U.S. 837 (1984): clarified; controlling deference requires an interpretation issued through delegated authority to carry the force of law.  
*Skidmore v. Swift & Co.*, 323 U.S. 134 (1944): reaffirmed; noncontrolling agency interpretations may earn persuasive weight through reasoning, consistency, expertise, care, and statutory fit.  
*Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. U.S. Army Corps of Engineers*, decided January 9, 2001: followed; informal agency reasoning cannot enlarge a statutory boundary and receives only the weight its persuasive force warrants.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for independent statutory interpretation informed by the classification letter’s persuasive weight.  
Effect: The classification letter does not bind the courts under *Chevron*. Customs interpretations issued through a valid force-of-law procedure may receive different treatment, and the correct classification of Mead’s planners remains unresolved.

### United States v. Weston

Argued April 25, 2001 | Decided June 18, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: involuntary-medication order reversed and case remanded for separate consideration of the Government’s asserted rationales, 206 F.3d 9 (D.C. Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/206/9/598001/))  
Area of Law: Due process; Involuntary medication; Competence to stand trial

Questions Presented:
1. What findings govern involuntary medication justified by present dangerousness or ordinary medical need?
2. What additional sequence and findings govern medication sought to restore trial competence, and how must trial fairness be considered?

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the reversal of the medication order and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Dangerousness-based treatment and medical care

Holding and operative rule: Involuntary antipsychotic medication justified by present dangerousness or ordinary medical need requires current, individualized findings of serious mental illness, current danger or medical necessity, medical appropriateness, and adequate neutral procedure. A court may not sustain the order on a rationale that the supporting record did not actually establish.

Reasoning: A detainee retains a substantial liberty interest in avoiding unwanted antipsychotic medication, while the Government possesses legitimate responsibilities for safety and medical care. *Washington v. Harper* permits treatment tied to current dangerousness and medical appropriateness, but those predicates must be established by present evidence rather than inferred from the seriousness of the pending charge or the possibility that treatment also might restore competence. The record before the district court focused substantially on competence restoration, while the order rested on dangerousness and medical need without adequate findings supporting that distinct rationale. The district court must obtain current evidence, consider reasonable less intrusive measures, and determine whether the dangerousness or medical predicate independently exists. The Court does not authorize medication under the present order. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/206/9/598001/))

##### Question 2: Competence restoration and trial fairness

Holding and operative rule: A competence-restoration order may be considered only after the court separately identifies that rationale and determines that medication is independently necessary, likely to restore competence, medically appropriate, unsupported by adequate less intrusive alternatives, and consistent with a fair trial. The Court does not announce a complete generally applicable competence-restoration test on this record.

Reasoning: Medication sought to make a defendant triable serves a different governmental objective from immediate safety or ordinary treatment and therefore requires its own findings. A court must identify the concrete governmental interest in proceeding, the likely efficacy of the proposed medication for this defendant, medical risks and side effects, available alternatives, and the consequences for the defendant’s ability to communicate with counsel, understand proceedings, present a defense, and appear before the factfinder. The Government may not blend evidence supporting one rationale into an undifferentiated authorization under another. Because the existing proceedings did not separately adjudicate these considerations and current circumstances may have changed, the proper course is a focused remand rather than a final competence-only authorization or a comprehensive rule extending beyond the record. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/206/9/598001/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Existing dangerousness and medical-treatment doctrine gives responsible prison physicians and trial judges sufficient authority to order medically appropriate treatment subject to ordinary review. They would not impose the Court’s required sequence and would reverse the D.C. Circuit’s remand.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Washington v. Harper*, 494 U.S. 210 (1990): clarified; dangerousness-based medication requires a current, individualized safety and medical predicate that cannot be supplied by a different competence-restoration rationale.  
*Riggins v. Nevada*, 504 U.S. 127 (1992): applied and clarified; necessity, medical appropriateness, less intrusive alternatives, and trial consequences must be addressed before medication affecting the criminal trial may be authorized.  
U.S. Const. amend. V: clarified; dangerousness treatment, ordinary medical care, competence restoration, and trial fairness are distinct grounds requiring separate adjudication.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for current, rationale-specific findings under the dangerousness, medical-care, competence, and trial-fairness principles stated.  
Effect: The existing medication order may not be executed. A competence-only order may be considered only if independently necessary after the other grounds are separately adjudicated, and no medication is authorized by this decision.

### Calcano-Martinez v. INS

Argued April 24, 2001 | Decided June 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: direct petitions for review dismissed under 8 U.S.C. § 1252(a)(2)(C), with separate habeas review preserved, 232 F.3d 328 (2d Cir. 2000). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-348.pdf))  
Area of Law: Immigration; Federal courts; Review channels

Questions Presented:
1. Whether § 1252(a)(2)(C) bars the direct petitions for review filed by covered criminal aliens.
2. Whether enforcement of the direct-review bar also eliminates any independently preserved habeas channel.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Direct-petition bar | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The direct petitions remain dismissed. |
| Preservation of a separate habeas route | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | The dismissals remain without prejudice to timely and otherwise authorized habeas actions. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees that the direct petitions are barred but rejects preservation of a separate § 2241 route |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm dismissal of the direct petitions without prejudice to timely and otherwise available habeas actions.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Direct petitions for review

Holding and operative rule: Section 1252(a)(2)(C) forecloses the covered petitioners’ direct petitions for review in the courts of appeals. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The direct-review provision expressly removes court-of-appeals jurisdiction over final removal orders entered against aliens covered by the specified criminal grounds. The petitioners fall within that class and cannot preserve the direct route by invoking concerns that may support a legally distinct form of collateral review. Statutory review channels must be treated separately. Preserving a habeas remedy under another source does not permit a court to disregard the express limitation Congress placed on direct petitions. The Second Circuit therefore correctly dismissed the petitions filed under the direct-review provision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-348.pdf))

##### Question 2: Independently preserved habeas review

Holding and operative rule: Enforcement of the direct-petition bar does not eliminate historic habeas jurisdiction independently preserved under *Reno v. Goncalves*. Any habeas action remains confined to its lawful scope and does not reopen factual or discretionary matters Congress validly made unreviewable.

Reasoning: *Reno v. Goncalves*, decided June 3, 1999, distinguishes statutory channeling from withdrawal of the historic writ. A route-specific bar directed to petitions for review does not unmistakably repeal § 2241 or identify an equally clear and meaningful substitute for pure legal or constitutional custody challenges. The petitioners therefore may pursue timely habeas actions if their claims fall within the jurisdiction and remedy that *Goncalves* preserves. This conclusion does not transfer every issue excluded from direct review into habeas. Questions of discretionary judgment, disputed removal facts, exhaustion, timeliness, custody, and other statutory limits remain independently controlling. Because the direct petitions were properly dismissed but the separate route remains legally possible, the dismissals stay without prejudice.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that § 1252(a)(2)(C) bars the direct petitions. They would treat the immigration-review scheme as eliminating collateral § 2241 review of the same final removal orders and would not preserve a separate habeas route.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Reno v. Goncalves*, decided June 3, 1999: followed; historic habeas jurisdiction survives absent unmistakable withdrawal and an equally clear meaningful substitute.  
8 U.S.C. § 1252(a)(2)(C): clarified; its express direct-review bar remains fully operative even when a separate habeas channel survives.  
Removal-review channeling doctrine: clarified; statutory routes are analyzed source by source, and preservation of one does not rewrite the limits of another.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed, and the direct petitions remain dismissed without prejudice to any timely and otherwise authorized habeas action.  
Effect: The petitioners may not proceed through direct court-of-appeals review. A qualifying habeas action may address only issues within the independently preserved jurisdiction and may not reopen factual or discretionary matters validly excluded from review.

### FEC v. Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee

Argued February 28, 2001 | Decided June 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: the coordinated-expenditure limitation facially invalidated, 213 F.3d 1221 (10th Cir. 2000). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-431.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Campaign finance; Coordinated expenditures

Questions Presented:
1. May Congress limit political-party expenditures proved coordinated with a candidate as contribution-like support?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Contribution-like treatment of proven coordination

Holding and operative rule: Political-party spending proved coordinated with a candidate may be limited as contribution-like support under closely drawn review. Coordination must be established transaction by transaction through a candidate’s request, direction, control, consultation, material participation, or another operative connection; party identity, shared ideology, parallel advocacy, and use of public information do not establish coordination by themselves.

Reasoning: *Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. FEC*, decided June 26, 1996, protects genuinely independent party expenditures and rejects any presumption that party identity or shared electoral objectives alone make spending coordinated. *Mariani v. United States*, decided March 12, 2001, now supplies the same-term rule for proven coordination: spending placed under a candidate’s request, direction, control, consultation, or material participation furnishes campaign value in a contribution-like form and may be regulated to prevent corruption and circumvention. Political parties remain entitled to associate with and advocate for their candidates, but unlimited coordinated spending could function as a substitute for direct contributions and as a conduit for donors seeking candidate-controlled value. The Tenth Circuit therefore erred by facially invalidating the limitation instead of requiring precise proof and application to the transactions claimed to be coordinated. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-431.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Scalia and Kennedy: Coordination between a political party and its candidates is central to the party’s speech and associational function rather than a disguised contribution. They would apply strict scrutiny, hold the expenditure limitation insufficiently tailored, and affirm the facial judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Mariani v. United States*, decided March 12, 2001: followed; candidate-controlled or materially candidate-participated spending is contribution-like and receives closely drawn review.  
*Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. FEC*, decided June 26, 1996: clarified; genuinely independent party spending remains protected, while transaction-specific proof may establish constitutionally regulable coordination.  
*Buckley v. Valeo*, 424 U.S. 1 (1976): applied; coordinated expenditures functioning as contributions may be limited to address corruption and circumvention.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for transaction-specific application of the coordinated-expenditure limitation under the stated proof boundary.  
Effect: The limitation may be enforced when constitutionally sufficient coordination is proved. Genuinely independent party expenditures, ordinary association, shared political objectives, and parallel public advocacy remain protected.

### INS v. St. Cyr

Argued April 24, 2001 | Decided June 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: § 2241 jurisdiction and continued eligibility for pre-IIRIRA discretionary relief sustained, 229 F.3d 406 (2d Cir. 2000). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-289.pdf))  
Area of Law: Immigration habeas; Retroactivity; Discretionary relief

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Congress clearly withdrew historic § 2241 review of St. Cyr’s legal eligibility claim.
2. Whether repeal of former § 212(c) relief applies to a plea entered before the 1996 enactments.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm on habeas jurisdiction and retroactivity.  
Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Historic habeas jurisdiction

Holding and operative rule: Section 2241 remains available for a pure legal challenge to removal-related custody absent an unmistakable congressional withdrawal of the writ or an equally clear and meaningful substitute review channel.

Reasoning: *Reno v. Goncalves*, decided June 3, 1999, controls the jurisdictional inquiry. The 1996 provisions restrict particular forms of immigration review, but they do not state with the clarity necessary to eliminate historic habeas jurisdiction over a pure question of legal eligibility. Nor does the statutory structure provide an equally clear substitute capable of adjudicating that claim. The strong presumption favoring judicial review and the constitutional concerns raised by eliminating the writ reinforce the enacted-text analysis, but the holding rests on the absence of an unmistakable withdrawal. St. Cyr therefore may use § 2241 to obtain a legal determination whether the repeal applies to him. Habeas jurisdiction does not authorize the court to decide disputed removal facts, disregard exhaustion or filing requirements, or grant the discretionary waiver itself. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-289.pdf))

##### Question 2: Application of the repeal to a pre-enactment plea

Holding and operative rule: Repeal of former § 212(c) relief does not apply to an alien who entered a guilty plea before the repeal while statutory eligibility for that discretionary relief remained available.

Reasoning: Under *Landgraf v. USI Film Products*, a statute operates retroactively when it attaches a new legal disability to completed conduct without sufficiently clear congressional direction. St. Cyr entered a plea that resolved criminal charges while the immigration law preserved eligibility to seek § 212(c) relief. Applying the later repeal would change the legal consequences of that completed bargain by making a previously available form of relief categorically unavailable. The judgment does not rest on a guarantee that the waiver would have been granted, nor does it transform individual reliance into a separate element. It preserves only the legal eligibility attached to the plea when made. The agency retains authority to conduct removal proceedings, determine every remaining statutory condition, and grant or deny relief through the lawful exercise of discretion. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-289.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: The comprehensive immigration-review provisions clearly displace collateral § 2241 jurisdiction over final removal orders. They also would apply the repeal to pending removal proceedings because it governs prospective relief rather than imposing punishment for the earlier plea.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Reno v. Goncalves*, decided June 3, 1999: followed and extended; preserved habeas jurisdiction reaches a pure legal challenge to eligibility when Congress supplies neither unmistakable withdrawal nor a meaningful substitute.  
*Landgraf v. USI Film Products*, 511 U.S. 244 (1994): applied; eliminating relief eligibility after a completed plea attaches a new legal disability to the prior transaction.  
*Felker v. Turpin*, 518 U.S. 651 (1996): followed; restrictions on particular review routes do not eliminate historic habeas authority without sufficiently clear enacted direction.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: St. Cyr may obtain § 2241 adjudication of his legal eligibility for former § 212(c) relief, and the repeal does not govern his pre-enactment plea. No discretionary waiver is ordered, and the agency may continue removal proceedings subject to the governing eligibility holding and all remaining lawful determinations.

### Nevada v. Hicks

Argued March 21, 2001 | Decided June 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: tribal-court jurisdiction and exhaustion sustained, 196 F.3d 1020 (9th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-353.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal Indian law; Tribal adjudicatory jurisdiction; State officers

Questions Presented:
1. Does a tribal court possess civil jurisdiction over state game wardens sued for executing state process on reservation land concerning an off-reservation offense?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Would categorically deny tribal jurisdiction over the claims against the state officers |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Tribal jurisdiction over execution of state process

Holding and operative rule: A tribal court ordinarily lacks jurisdiction over claims challenging nonmember state officers’ lawful execution of state process concerning an off-reservation offense. Materially distinct allegations that officers exceeded the warrants or acted outside the state enforcement function must be analyzed claim by claim under an independently recognized source of tribal authority. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Under *Montana v. United States*, tribal civil authority over nonmembers is limited and cannot be inferred solely from reservation landownership or a general tribal interest in conduct occurring within reservation boundaries. State officers investigating an off-reservation violation do not enter a consensual relationship with the tribe by obtaining and executing state and tribal warrants, and lawful execution of that process is not subject to general tribal supervision. Section 1983 creates a federal cause of action where its requirements are met, but it does not confer jurisdiction on a tribal forum. The record also includes allegations that the wardens exceeded the warrants or engaged in conduct outside their legitimate state function. Those allegations cannot be placed within or outside tribal authority categorically without identifying the particular claim, the conduct proved, and an applicable source of tribal power. Immunity, cause of action, forum, and ultimate merits remain separate questions. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-353.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas, concurring in the judgment: Tribal courts lack adjudicatory authority over nonmember state officers executing process connected to an off-reservation state offense. They would apply that categorical rule to every claim before the Court and require dismissal rather than preserve claim-specific tribal-jurisdiction inquiries.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Montana v. United States*, 450 U.S. 544 (1981): clarified; tribal landownership and generalized sovereign interests do not alone establish civil jurisdiction over nonmember state officers.  
*National Farmers Union Insurance Cos. v. Crow Tribe*, 471 U.S. 845 (1985): limited; exhaustion is not required merely to permit a tribal court to decide claims plainly outside its authority, while materially distinct claims requiring application of a recognized jurisdictional source may remain subject to ordinary analysis.  
42 U.S.C. § 1983: clarified; the statute creates no tribal forum and does not itself establish tribal adjudicatory jurisdiction.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for dismissal of claims attacking lawful execution of valid state process and for claim-specific jurisdictional treatment of any materially distinct allegations that the officers exceeded the warrants or acted outside the state enforcement function.  
Effect: The tribal court may not exercise general supervisory jurisdiction over the wardens’ execution of state process for the off-reservation offense. The decision does not resolve immunity, ultimate liability in a competent forum, jurisdiction over tribal members, or a claim supported by a qualifying consensual relationship or another independently recognized source of tribal authority.

### New York Times Co. v. Tasini

Argued March 28, 2001 | Decided June 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: electronic database reproduction held outside the § 201(c) privilege, 206 F.3d 161 (2d Cir. 2000). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-483.pdf))  
Area of Law: Copyright; Collective works; Electronic databases

Questions Presented:
1. Does § 201(c) permit publishers to reproduce freelance articles in databases that present each article separately from its original collective-work context?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Section 201(c) revision privilege

Holding and operative rule: A publisher’s § 201(c) privilege does not authorize electronic database distribution that presents a freelance contribution separately from the original collective work or a cognizable revision of that collective work.

Reasoning: Section 201(c) permits a collective-work owner to reproduce a contribution as part of the original collective work, a revision of that work, or a later collective work in the same series. The inquiry turns on the context in which the contribution is reproduced, not merely on whether the article’s words remain unchanged or whether the database identifies its original publication. Users of the challenged databases retrieve each article as a stand-alone item, separated from the surrounding articles, page arrangement, photographs, advertisements, and editorial selection that constituted the original newspaper or magazine issue. That presentation is not the original collective work and does not revise the issue as a collective whole. Technological change does not eliminate the statutory distinction between rights in individual contributions and rights in the collective work. Liability therefore is affirmed, while the form and scope of relief remain subject to the Copyright Act and equitable principles. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-533/pdf/USREPORTS-533-483.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Breyer: The databases preserve the articles, their authorship, and identifying information concerning the original publication and should be treated as technologically different revisions of the collective works. They would reverse, particularly because the Court’s construction threatens socially valuable electronic archival access.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Copyright Act § 201(c): clarified; the revision privilege depends on reproduction of the contribution within the original collective-work context or a cognizable revision of that collective whole.  
*Feist Publications, Inc. v. Rural Telephone Service Co.*, 499 U.S. 340 (1991): applied; copyright in a collective work is distinct from copyright in the individual contributions and extends only to the protectable selection and arrangement.  
Copyright Act § 106: applied; stand-alone database distribution remains subject to the individual author’s exclusive rights absent a sufficient transfer or statutory privilege.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed, and any further remedial proceedings shall provide source-authorized relief tailored to preserve lawful archival access where possible.  
Effect: The challenged stand-alone database reproductions fall outside § 201(c). Page-faithful reproductions, microfilm, archives preserving the collective context, broader contractual transfers, lawful licensing arrangements, and the proper measure or form of relief remain governed separately.
