Chronology reconciliation: *United States v. United Foods, Inc.* shares the June 25, 2001 effective date with the five decisions completed in Chunk 7. Under the Engine’s same-day rule, those six decisions use the same entering-law baseline and none changes another’s adjudication. *Lorillard Tobacco Co. v. Reilly* and *Palazzolo v. Rhode Island* likewise are uncoordinated same-day decisions using the same June 28 baseline.

Source correction: The attached Section I describes the Sixth Circuit as sustaining the mushroom assessment, while its own material-facts section states that the court held the compelled subsidy unconstitutional. The official record confirms that the Sixth Circuit reversed the District Court and held the assessment unconstitutional. The corrected posture aligns with Stone’s approved affirmance and changes no controlled judgment, legal position, remedy, or boundary. `OT_2000CHUNK8.md` ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/405/))

## Matter-Specific Validation Conflicts

### United States v. Valentine

Adjudication is paused. The controlled brief describes the judgment below as sustaining both the stop and a conviction, and Stone’s approved remedy states that the conviction remains in force. The authoritative record instead establishes that the Government took an interlocutory appeal from a District Court order suppressing the firearm, that the Third Circuit reversed the suppression order, and that the criminal case was remanded for further proceedings before any conviction under review. Correcting that posture would materially change the procedural stage and Stone’s controlled remedy. The Engine may not substitute a pretrial remand instruction for the approved conviction-preserving remedy without a revised and approved Section II. The matter remains at the post-argument merits stage. `OT_2000CHUNK8.md` ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/232/350/514839/))

### Chen v. City of Houston

Adjudication is paused. Standing State limits the simulated grant to two apportionment-denominator questions: whether local districts must equalize total population, eligible-voter population, or citizen voting-age population, and what constitutional limit applies when equal-total-population districts contain substantial eligible-voter disparities. The controlled case brief adds a separate racial-predominance question and directs Stone to adjudicate that theory. Racial predominance is a distinct equal-protection claim with its own proof, record application, holding, and remedial consequences. The Engine may not silently enlarge the grant or discard a controlled Stone position. A revised approved Section II confined to the operative grant, or an authoritative correction of the grant, is required. The matter remains at the post-argument merits stage. `OT_2000CHUNK8.md` `STANDING_STATE.md`

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. United Foods, Inc.

Argued April 17, 2001 | Decided June 25, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: the District Court’s enforcement judgment reversed and the mandatory promotional assessment held unconstitutional, 197 F.3d 221 (6th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/405/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Compelled subsidy; Commercial speech

Questions Presented:
1. May independent mushroom handlers be compelled to finance generic advertising when the assessment is not ancillary to an integrated collective regulatory program?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. Opposing: O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Ancillary-program boundary |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Strict-scrutiny ground |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor as to Parts I and III; Ginsburg in full | Would reverse | Complete dissent and partial join as stated |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Stand-alone compelled promotional subsidy

Holding and operative rule: A mandatory assessment violates the First Amendment when its principal function is to compel independent market participants to finance generic promotional speech rather than to support an integrated collective regulatory program.

Reasoning: The First Amendment may permit assessments germane to a lawful association or comprehensive regulatory arrangement that independently binds the assessed parties together. Here, generic advertising is the program’s central operation rather than an incident of broader regulation restricting handlers’ marketing autonomy, prices, output, or competitive decisions. The handlers remain independent businesses and are compelled to finance a message that may conflict with their own branded advertising. Treating the speech as germane merely because promotion is the program’s stated object would make the germaneness limit circular. The assessment therefore cannot be sustained under the received compelled-subsidy principles. Accurate factual disclosures, ordinary program-administration charges, and assessments genuinely ancillary to a broader regulatory enterprise remain outside the holding. The Government’s separate government-speech theory was not preserved in the court below and is not reached.

#### Concurrences

Stevens: Compelled payment differs from compelled personal expression, but a subsidy remains constitutionally suspect when it is not ancillary to a valid cooperative endeavor and instead makes one entrepreneur finance competitors’ advertising.

Thomas: Any regulation compelling a private party to finance advertising should receive the most stringent First Amendment scrutiny. He agrees that this assessment cannot survive.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Ginsburg and by O’Connor as to Parts I and III: The assessment is economic regulation addressing collective-action and free-rider problems in a national commodity market. He would sustain the program because handlers remain free to advertise independently and the compelled payment supports lawful commercial promotion rather than political or ideological expression.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Abood v. Detroit Board of Education*: extended; compelled funding is permissible only when germane to an independently valid associational or regulatory function.  
*Keller v. State Bar of California*: followed; speech cannot satisfy germaneness merely by being the sole purpose for which the compelled association or assessment exists.  
*Zauderer v. Office of Disciplinary Counsel*: distinguished; an accurate factual disclosure preventing consumer deception differs from compelled financing of another speaker’s generic promotional message.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The challenged mushroom-promotion assessment may not be enforced against United Foods on the program presented. Government may still impose accurate disclosure duties, ordinary administrative assessments, and promotional charges genuinely ancillary to an integrated regulatory scheme.

### Lorillard Tobacco Co. v. Reilly / Altadis U.S.A. Inc. v. Reilly

Argued April 25, 2001 | Decided June 28, 2001  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: the cigarette-advertising regulations held not preempted and most challenged advertising and sales provisions sustained, 218 F.3d 30 (1st Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/525/))  
Area of Law: Federal preemption; Commercial speech; Tobacco regulation

Questions Presented:
1. Which Massachusetts tobacco-advertising rules are preempted by federal law?
2. Whether the surviving advertising and product-access restrictions satisfy the First Amendment.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded. The component votes are stated below.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cigarette-advertising preemption | Reversed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The cigarette-advertising provisions within the federally preempted field are set aside. |
| One-thousand-foot outdoor advertising restrictions | Reversed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The outdoor restrictions may not be enforced. |
| Five-foot point-of-sale advertising restriction | Reversed, 6-3 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | The height-based advertising restriction is set aside. |
| Neutral product-access and sales regulations | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The conduct regulations remain in force. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone in full; Stevens as to Parts I, II-C, II-D, and III-B-1; Scalia and Kennedy except Part III-B-1; Souter as to Parts I, II-C, II-D, III-A, III-B-1, III-C, and III-D; Thomas except Part III-B-1; Ginsburg and Breyer as to Parts I, II-C, II-D, and III-B-1 | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion and partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter as to Part I; Ginsburg and Breyer in full | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Preemption, outdoor-remand, point-of-sale, and sales-practice issues |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Kennedy | Scalia | Concurs in part and in the judgment | All except the Court’s direct-advancement analysis |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the stated portions and would remand the outdoor-advertising issue |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in part and in the judgment | All except the Court’s direct-advancement analysis; strict-scrutiny ground |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the cigarette-advertising preemption judgment, reverse the outdoor and five-foot advertising judgments, affirm the neutral product-access and sales judgment, and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Federal preemption of cigarette-advertising regulation

Holding and operative rule: The Federal Cigarette Labeling and Advertising Act preempts state requirements and prohibitions concerning cigarette advertising or promotion when they are based on smoking and health, including location-based rules directed to that concern. The Act does not preempt every tobacco sales rule or a rule governing a product outside the federal cigarette-advertising field.

Reasoning: Congress enacted a uniform federal program governing cigarette labeling and advertising and expressly displaced state requirements concerning cigarette advertising based on smoking and health. Massachusetts’ cigarette-location rules regulate where promotional messages may appear because of their anticipated effect on smoking, particularly youth smoking. Recasting those provisions as location rules does not remove them from the field Congress expressly occupied. Separate rules regulating sales, product placement, access by minors, or non-cigarette products must be classified independently rather than invalidated merely because they appear in the same regulatory package. Severability remains for the lower courts because it was not resolved below.

##### Question 2(a): One-thousand-foot outdoor advertising restrictions

Holding and operative rule: Youth protection is a substantial governmental interest, and the record supplies a material connection between tobacco advertising and youth demand. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer) The one-thousand-foot outdoor restrictions nevertheless fail because their practical sweep suppresses excessive lawful adult-directed communication. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: Massachusetts may act against underage tobacco use and may rely on evidence connecting advertising exposure to youth demand. The outdoor rules, however, prohibit outdoor, window-visible, and much point-of-sale advertising throughout large portions of major cities and communities. The result is not a targeted restriction on child-directed promotion but a broad foreclosure of truthful communication concerning products lawful for adults. The State retains direct alternatives, including youth-sales enforcement, access restrictions, product-placement requirements, and narrower evidence-supported advertising rules. The breadth of the prohibited zones therefore lacks the reasonable fit required for suppression of truthful commercial information.

##### Question 2(b): Five-foot point-of-sale advertising restriction

Holding and operative rule: The categorical prohibition on tobacco advertising below five feet inside retail establishments does not have a sufficiently demonstrated and reasonably fitted connection to youth protection and violates the First Amendment.

Reasoning: The rule applies to advertising placed inside stores even when the communication is not visible from outside and adults remain the intended audience. The record does not establish that advertising height operates as a reliable proxy for child-directed promotion or that a complete ban below five feet is needed to address access by minors. Massachusetts may regulate product placement and direct physical access, but it may not suppress truthful in-store advertising through a broad height rule without an adequate evidentiary and tailoring basis.

##### Question 2(c): Neutral product-access and sales regulations

Holding and operative rule: Neutral rules governing placement, handling, and sale of tobacco products may be sustained as conduct regulations when they directly restrict youth access and leave lawful product communication available. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas)

Reasoning: Requiring tobacco products to remain behind a counter or within a salesperson’s control regulates the manner of sale and physical access rather than the content or viewpoint of advertising. The provisions directly reduce opportunities for minors to obtain the products without adult intervention while leaving manufacturers and retailers free to identify, describe, and promote lawful products to adults. All members of the Court agree that these conduct provisions may remain in force, although three Justices rest that judgment on a narrower distinction between sales regulation and meaningful speech restriction.

##### Question 2(d): Cigar sampling and promotional giveaways

Not reached; the cigar petitioners did not sufficiently brief and argue the claim before the Court.

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer, and by Souter as to Part I: The cigarette-location provisions are not preempted, and the outdoor-advertising record should be returned for trial rather than resolved against Massachusetts. He also would sustain the point-of-sale and sales-practice provisions.

Kennedy, joined by Scalia: The obvious overbreadth of the outdoor restrictions resolves the case under the reasonable-fit component of commercial-speech review. They decline to join the Court’s direct-advancement analysis and reserve broader objections to the governing commercial-speech test.

Souter: He agrees with the Court’s treatment of direct advancement, point-of-sale advertising, and sales practices but would remand the one-thousand-foot outdoor restriction for fuller factual development.

Thomas: Truthful commercial speech should receive strict scrutiny when government suppresses information to influence lawful consumer choice. He agrees that every advertising restriction before the Court fails.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Federal Cigarette Labeling and Advertising Act*: clarified; state cigarette-advertising requirements based on smoking and health are preempted even when framed as location restrictions.  
*Cipollone v. Liggett Group, Inc.*: clarified; express preemption turns on the legal duty imposed and its smoking-and-health basis rather than the State’s regulatory label.  
*Greater New Orleans Broadcasting Association v. United States*: applied; truthful commercial-speech restrictions require evidence of material advancement and reasonable fit.  
*44 Liquormart, Inc. v. Rhode Island*: followed; authority to regulate or prohibit an underlying product does not automatically authorize broad suppression of truthful adult-directed information about lawful sales.  
*Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc.*: applied; a targeted speech restriction requires an evidence-supported fit between its scope and the specific harm asserted.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed as to the neutral product-access and sales regulations, reversed as to the preempted cigarette-advertising provisions and the unconstitutional outdoor and five-foot advertising restrictions, and the consolidated cases are remanded for severability and provision-specific proceedings.  
Effect: Massachusetts may enforce neutral youth-sales, product-placement, and access rules but may not enforce the provisions set aside by this judgment. Accurate disclosures, neutral zoning and sign rules, and narrower evidence-supported advertising restrictions remain available.

### Palazzolo v. Rhode Island

Argued February 26, 2001 | Decided June 28, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Rhode Island; judgment below: regulatory-takings claims rejected as unripe and barred in part by post-enactment acquisition, 746 A.2d 707 (R.I. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/606/))  
Area of Law: Takings Clause; Land-use finality; Regulatory takings

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the land-use decision was sufficiently final for regulatory-takings review.
2. Whether acquisition after enactment categorically bars the takings claim.
3. Whether the record establishes a total taking or requires adjudication under *Penn Central*.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded. The component votes are stated below.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Finality | Reversed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The claim is sufficiently final for review. |
| Post-enactment acquisition | Reversed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The categorical ownership-date bar is rejected. |
| Total-taking theory | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The rejection of the total-taking claim remains in force. |
| Partial-taking claim | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The claim returns for *Penn Central* adjudication. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas in full; Stevens as to Part II-A | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion and partial join as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Finality only; would affirm the remaining judgment |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Effect of acquisition timing within *Penn Central* |
| Concurrence | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Limited relevance of post-enactment acquisition |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Souter, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Breyer | No other Justice | Would affirm | Additional ripeness and ownership observations |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the finality and post-acquisition rulings, affirm rejection of the total-taking theory, reverse the partial-taking ruling, and remand.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Finality of the land-use restriction

Holding and operative rule: A regulatory-takings claim is final when the responsible agency has made the practical extent of the restriction known and further applications would be futile. The land-use decision here satisfies that requirement.

Reasoning: Finality requires a sufficiently definite governmental position so a court can identify the property burden being challenged. It does not require repetitive applications after the agency has made clear that the proposed development cannot proceed and further submissions would not materially alter the permissible use. Rhode Island rejected development proposals that established the operative restriction on the wetlands parcel. The record therefore permits review without requiring another futile application. The rule does not make every initial permit denial final or excuse a landowner from seeking a meaningful variance when one remains realistically available.

##### Question 2: Acquisition after enactment

Holding and operative rule: Acquisition of property after enactment of a regulation does not categorically bar a regulatory-takings claim. The existing regulatory environment may bear on the owner’s investment-backed expectations, but notice alone does not erase the property interest or waive the Takings Clause.

Reasoning: A contrary rule would permit government to place an uncompensated restriction on property permanently by asserting that every later owner purchased with notice. Constitutional protection attaches to the property interest and cannot be extinguished merely through transfer. The timing of acquisition may remain relevant to the fact-sensitive expectations inquiry under *Penn Central*, together with the nature of the regulation, the property’s history, and the owner’s actual investment position. It is not a threshold rule eliminating the claim before that inquiry occurs.

##### Question 3(a): Total taking

Holding and operative rule: A regulation does not effect a categorical total taking when the parcel as a whole retains significant economically beneficial use. On this record, the property’s remaining residential value defeats the total-taking theory. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: The categorical rule for a deprivation of all economically beneficial use is measured against the legally relevant parcel as a whole. Palazzolo did not preserve a claim treating only the wetlands portion as the denominator, and the record establishes that the full parcel retains significant value for residential construction. Because some economically beneficial use remains, the total-taking rule does not apply. That conclusion does not decide whether the retained use and the regulation’s economic impact are sufficient to defeat a partial-taking claim.

##### Question 3(b): Partial taking

Holding and operative rule: When the total-taking rule does not apply, the owner’s preserved claim must be adjudicated under *Penn Central*, including the regulation’s economic impact, interference with reasonable investment-backed expectations, and character.

Reasoning: The Rhode Island courts rejected the claim through finality and ownership-date rules and therefore did not perform the required partial-taking analysis. The Supreme Court cannot supply the missing findings concerning economic impact, parcel history, expectations, transferable value, and the character of the wetlands regulation. The proper course is a remand for that source-specific inquiry. No compensation is ordered, and no view is expressed on the ultimate merits.

#### Concurrences

Stevens: He agrees that the agency position was sufficiently final. He would hold that a claimant cannot recover for a taking that occurred before the claimant acquired the property and would affirm the remainder of the judgment.

O’Connor: Post-enactment acquisition is not a categorical bar, but the regulatory regime in place at acquisition may materially inform reasonable investment-backed expectations on remand.

Scalia: Once a regulation effects a compensable taking, later acquisition should not reduce the constitutional protection attached to the property. He would give ownership timing less independent weight than O’Connor.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by Souter and Breyer: The record does not establish a sufficiently final agency position because materially different development possibilities were not presented. They would affirm without reaching the remaining takings questions.

Breyer: He agrees that unresolved record questions concerning finality and the parcel make adjudication premature. If the claim were ripe, acquisition timing could remain relevant to the expectations analysis.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Williamson County Regional Planning Commission v. Hamilton Bank*: clarified; finality requires a known practical restriction, not futile repetition of applications.  
*Nollan v. California Coastal Commission*: followed; advance notice of a regulation does not convert the restriction into a background property limitation or a categorical waiver.  
*Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council*: applied; the total-taking rule does not govern when the parcel as a whole retains significant beneficial use.  
*Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City*: followed; noncategorical regulatory burdens require the established fact-sensitive inquiry.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Rhode Island is reversed as to finality, the categorical post-acquisition bar, and dismissal of the partial-taking claim, affirmed as to the total-taking claim, and the case is remanded for *Penn Central* adjudication.  
Effect: Palazzolo may pursue the partial-taking claim without a categorical ownership-date bar. The Court awards no compensation, makes no finding of liability, and does not isolate the wetlands from the parcel as a whole.

### Children’s Healthcare Is a Legal Duty, Inc. v. Min De Parle

Argued June 5, 2001 | Decided July 2, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: the federal reimbursement provision sustained against the Establishment Clause challenge, 212 F.3d 1084 (8th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/212/1084/632477/))  
Area of Law: Establishment Clause; Public benefits; Religious providers

Questions Presented:
1. Whether federal reimbursement of care furnished by religious nonmedical institutions is constitutionally limited to genuinely severable secular services verified on religion-neutral terms.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Direct reimbursement of services at religious institutions

Holding and operative rule: Government may reimburse a religious provider for genuinely severable secular care only under religion-neutral eligibility, public verification, accounting, and use restrictions that prevent payment for worship, faith healing, devotional nursing, or institutional religious expense.

Reasoning: Religious identity does not disqualify an institution from receiving payment for secular services available on equivalent terms to secular providers. Neutral eligibility and patient choice, however, do not by themselves establish that a direct provider payment finances only secular care. The challenged category is tied to a religious objection to medical treatment, relieves participating institutions from ordinary medical oversight, and permits activities described as nursing to include prayer, Bible reading, and support for faith healing. Statutory labels such as bed, board, and physical care cannot substitute for practical verification of what public funds purchase. The lower courts must identify independently secular services, determine whether public officials verify eligibility and necessity without deciding religious truth, examine accounting and use safeguards, and decide whether any invalid applications can be severed.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The program neutrally reimburses patient-selected care and expressly excludes payment for religious services. They would affirm absent proof that federal money was actually diverted from the secular benefits Congress identified.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Mitchell v. Helms*, decided June 28, 2000: followed; neutral allocation does not validate direct provision of fungible public resources when a religious institution controls their use and the aid may substitute for institutional expense.  
*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: clarified; neutral eligibility does not convert direct provider reimbursement for religious activity into private expenditure or private speech.  
*Bowen v. Kendrick*: applied; social-service funding remains subject to as-applied review of whether public money finances secular services or religious activity.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for service-specific findings concerning secular severability, public verification, accounting, use restrictions, and severability of invalid applications.  
Effect: No categorical prohibition on reimbursement to religious providers is entered. Payment remains permissible for secular care that is independently identifiable, neutrally available, publicly verified, and protected against religious use.

### Sterling v. Borough of Minersville

Argued June 6, 2001 | Decided July 9, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: denial of summary judgment on qualified-immunity grounds affirmed, 232 F.3d 190 (3d Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/232/190/514967/))  
Area of Law: Informational privacy; Qualified immunity; Section 1983

Questions Presented:
1. Whether coercive official use of a person’s sexual orientation violates informational privacy.
2. Whether the individual officer is personally liable in damages.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded. The component votes are stated below.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Informational-privacy merits | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The constitutional claim is legally cognizable on the assumed facts. |
| Qualified immunity | Reversed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The individual officer is immune from damages. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees with qualified immunity and rejects the informational-privacy holding |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm recognition of the constitutional claim, reverse the denial of qualified immunity, and remand.  
Public role: joins O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Coercive use of sexual-orientation information

Holding and operative rule: Sexual orientation is highly intimate personal information. A government official violates the limited Fourteenth Amendment informational-privacy protection by intentionally using that information as coercive leverage for a purpose unrelated to a genuine and proportionate governmental function, even when the threatened disclosure is not completed.

Reasoning: The constitutional protection is limited and does not create a general right to control every fact known to government. It applies when official acquisition, use, or disclosure of exceptionally intimate information lacks a genuine and proportionate relationship to the governmental function. Taking the plaintiff-supported facts as true, the officer learned Wayman’s sexual orientation during a minor alcohol encounter and threatened disclosure to compel Wayman to reveal the information to his grandfather. That use served no identified investigative, safety, adjudicative, or administrative need. The harm lies in the official exploitation of the intimate information, not only in completed publication. The Court does not review whether the threat was actually made and does not decide suicide causation, municipal responsibility, or state-law liability.

##### Question 2: Qualified immunity

Holding and operative rule: The officer is entitled to qualified immunity because, in April 1997, existing law did not clearly establish that sexual orientation received this informational-privacy protection or that threatened coercive use without completed dissemination violated the Constitution.

Reasoning: *Saucier v. Katz*, decided June 18, 2001, requires the constitutional boundary to be stated before the fair-notice inquiry. The right announced today follows from existing protection for highly intimate medical information, but that analogy was not sufficiently definite when the officer acted. No controlling authority then applied the protection to sexual orientation or treated the threatened use of undisclosed information as the completed constitutional injury. The absence of an identical prior case is not automatically dispositive, but the governing principles had not made this application apparent to a reasonable officer. Individual damages therefore are unavailable.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They reject a substantive-due-process right governing official use of intimate information. They agree that the officer must receive qualified immunity and that the denial of individual summary judgment must be reversed.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Denius v. Dunlap*, decided April 2, 2001: extended; limited informational-privacy protection now reaches intentional, unrelated coercive use of sexual-orientation information.  
*Saucier v. Katz*, decided June 18, 2001: applied; the constitutional-merits question precedes qualified immunity.  
*Elder v. Holloway*: followed; fair notice is determined from the complete body of materially relevant law rather than a categorical identical-case requirement.  
*Johnson v. Jones*: followed; an interlocutory immunity appeal does not permit reconsideration of whether the officer made the disputed threat.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed as to the existence of the informational-privacy claim, reversed as to qualified immunity, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the officer’s immunity from individual damages.  
Effect: The officer incurs no personal damages liability. Entity liability, causation concerning Wayman’s death, prospective relief, and state-law claims remain subject to their independent requirements.

### Randell v. Johnson

Argued June 6, 2001 | Decided July 16, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the § 1983 complaint for failure to state a claim affirmed, 227 F.3d 300 (5th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/227/300/615129/))  
Area of Law: Section 1983; Habeas corpus; Overdetention

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a claim accepting the sentence as valid but alleging custody beyond its expiration necessarily implies invalidity.
2. What rule applies if success would imply invalidity but habeas was not practically available or became unavailable.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Classification of an overdetention claim

Holding and operative rule: A claim accepting the conviction and sentence as valid and alleging only confinement beyond their lawful expiration does not necessarily imply invalidity and falls outside *Heck*. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: *Heck* turns on the legal consequence of success rather than the label assigned to the claim. A plaintiff who accepts the sentence’s validity and alleges only that officials held him after the sentence had ended does not seek to invalidate the judgment authorizing the earlier custody. A different claim attacking a sentence-credit calculation, revocation judgment, or other still-operative custody determination would imply invalidity and remain subject to the favorable-termination rule. Randell’s pleadings and the lower-court treatment do not permit confident classification between those alternatives. Remand is required so the district court can identify the actual judgment, calculation, or administrative act whose validity the claim places in issue.

##### Question 2: No-fault unavailability of habeas

Holding and operative rule: When success would imply the invalidity of a custody judgment, favorable termination is excused only if no practicable habeas route existed or habeas became unavailable despite the claimant’s reasonable diligence and through no fault of the claimant. Strategic delay or failure to use an available route receives no exception.

Reasoning: The favorable-termination rule coordinates damages litigation with the more specific federal and state channels for challenging custody judgments. It should not eliminate every federal damages remedy where a claimant never possessed a practicable opportunity to use habeas or lost that opportunity despite timely and reasonable effort. The exception is narrow and places the claimant under an obligation to identify the available review period, the steps actually taken, and the external reason relief could not be obtained before custody ended. Mere expiration of custody is insufficient. Because the present record does not establish habeas availability or diligence, those questions must be resolved on remand before causation and damages.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: They agree that a genuinely separable overdetention claim falls outside *Heck*, but they read Randell’s allegations as attacking an unresolved custody calculation. They would preserve the favorable-termination rule without an unavailable-habeas exception and affirm dismissal.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Heck v. Humphrey*: clarified; a claim challenging only detention after an otherwise valid sentence expired does not necessarily imply invalidity.  
*Edwards v. Balisok*: followed; a claim attacking a custody-affecting judgment or calculation remains barred when success would establish its invalidity.  
*Spencer v. Kemna*: clarified; expiration of custody does not itself preserve habeas, but a reasonably diligent claimant who lacked any practicable habeas opportunity may invoke the narrow no-fault exception stated here.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for classification of the claim, findings concerning practical habeas availability and diligence, and adjudication of causation and damages under the applicable channel.  
Effect: Randell’s action is not categorically barred merely because custody has ended. He receives no presumption that habeas was unavailable and no relief if he strategically delayed or failed to pursue a practicable review route.

### Appalachian Power Co. v. EPA

Argued August 7, 2001 | Decided September 17, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: EPA guidance held final and set aside to the extent it imposed binding obligations, 208 F.3d 1015 (D.C. Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/208/1015/592975/))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; Final agency action; Rulemaking

Questions Presented:
1. When is an agency guidance document final and reviewable, and may an agency impose new binding duties through guidance without the force-of-law procedure Congress required?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: finality turns on practical legal consequences rather than labels, and an agency may not use nominally nonbinding guidance to impose new binding duties while avoiding the rulemaking procedure and judicial review Congress prescribed.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Binding guidance and final agency action

Holding and operative rule: A guidance document is final and reviewable when it represents the agency’s settled position and in practical operation determines regulated parties’ or permitting authorities’ obligations, notwithstanding a nonbinding disclaimer. Guidance may not create duties beyond the governing statute and regulation without the procedure Congress required.

Reasoning: Finality depends on completion of the agency’s decisionmaking and the practical legal consequences of the action. EPA had adopted a settled position, instructed regional officials and permitting authorities to administer it as controlling, and placed regulated entities under a compliance expectation that affected their permits and continuing operations. A boilerplate disclaimer cannot neutralize those consequences. An agency remains free to issue genuinely advisory interpretations, explain existing duties, and offer technical assistance. Such documents receive only the persuasive weight their reasoning earns. It may not enlarge substantive obligations, demand compliance as if a document carried force of law, and then avoid notice and comment and judicial review by calling the command guidance. The D.C. Circuit correctly set aside the portions operating as new binding law.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Mead Corp.*, decided June 18, 2001: extended; an interpretation lacking force-of-law deference may nevertheless be final and reviewable when the agency administers it as binding law.  
*Christensen v. Harris County*, decided May 1, 2000: followed; informal guidance earns persuasive weight but cannot create controlling statutory duties.  
Administrative Procedure Act §§ 553 and 704: clarified; settled practical legal consequences establish finality, and new binding duties require the procedure authorized by law.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and the portions of EPA’s guidance imposing new binding obligations remain set aside.  
Effect: EPA may issue genuine interpretive advice and may promulgate a binding rule through authorized procedure. It may not enforce the challenged guidance as an independent source of duties beyond the governing statute and regulations.

### Harris v. Garner

Argued August 20, 2001 | Decided September 24, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: 42 U.S.C. § 1997e(e) applied to the prisoners’ constitutional damages claims and the District Court’s judgment affirmed in part and vacated in part, 216 F.3d 970 (11th Cir. 2000) (en banc). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/216/970/570374/))  
Area of Law: Prison litigation; Civil remedies; Physical-injury requirement

Questions Presented:
1. Whether § 1997e(e) bars compensatory damages for mental or emotional injury without a prior showing of physical injury.
2. Whether the statute extinguishes the constitutional claim or categorically bars other forms of relief.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded. The component votes are stated below.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Compensatory damages for mental or emotional injury | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | That category of compensatory recovery remains barred absent the statutory physical injury. |
| Nominal, prospective, economic, property, and punitive relief | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The remaining remedy categories must be adjudicated separately. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees with the compensatory-damages bar and rejects the Court’s preservation of other monetary remedies |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the bar on compensatory damages for mental or emotional injury, reverse the categorical treatment of the other remedies, and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: § 1997e(e) limits recovery for a specified injury category without extinguishing the underlying claim or remedies that serve a legally distinct function and satisfy their own requirements.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Compensatory mental or emotional injury

Holding and operative rule: Section 1997e(e) bars compensatory damages for mental or emotional injury suffered in custody when the prisoner cannot make the statute’s required prior showing of physical injury.

Reasoning: The enacted text identifies a claimant class, an injury category, and a physical-injury condition. It therefore forecloses compensatory recovery for mental or emotional suffering as such when that condition is not met, including when the underlying claim alleges a constitutional violation. The rule does not depend on whether the mental or emotional injury is serious, whether the constitutional right is important, or whether a court believes compensation would be desirable. Congress selected the statutory condition, and courts may not create an exception for constitutional claims that the text does not contain.

##### Question 2: Other remedies and the underlying claim

Holding and operative rule: Section 1997e(e) does not extinguish the underlying cause of action or categorically bar nominal damages, prospective declaratory or injunctive relief, independently proved economic or property loss, or punitive damages directed to culpable misconduct. Each remedy remains subject to its own legal source, elements, defendant, immunity, causation, and constitutional limits.

Reasoning: The statute speaks to an action for mental or emotional injury, not to the existence of every constitutional right or every remedy available for its violation. Nominal damages recognize the completed violation without compensating mental distress. Prospective relief prevents or corrects ongoing unlawful conduct. Economic and property damages compensate a distinct material loss. Punitive damages, when independently authorized, punish and deter sufficiently culpable misconduct rather than compensate emotional suffering. Preserving those categories does not rewrite the physical-injury condition because none may be used as disguised compensation for the injury Congress addressed. The lower courts must classify the relief actually pleaded and determine whether every independent requirement is satisfied.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part: They agree that compensatory damages for mental or emotional injury are barred. They would read the prohibition more broadly against monetary relief arising from the same nonphysical injury and would not preserve punitive or other damages through the Court’s remedy-by-remedy distinction.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

42 U.S.C. § 1997e(e): clarified; the physical-injury requirement limits compensation for mental or emotional injury but does not extinguish the underlying claim or every distinct remedy.  
*Carey v. Piphus*: applied; nominal damages remain legally distinct from compensation for proved mental or emotional injury.  
*Smith v. Wade*: applied; punitive damages serve punishment and deterrence and remain available only when independently authorized and supported by the required culpability.  
*Booth v. Churner*, decided May 29, 2001: distinguished; mandatory exhaustion governs completion of the administrative process, while § 1997e(e) separately governs remedy categories.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed insofar as it bars compensatory damages for mental or emotional injury without the statutory physical injury, reversed insofar as it categorically forecloses other remedies, and the case is remanded for remedy-specific adjudication.  
Effect: The prisoners may not recover compensatory damages for mental or emotional suffering on the supplied record. Properly pleaded nominal, prospective, economic, property, and punitive relief may proceed only if each remedy’s independent requirements are satisfied.

---

**Sources:**

- [United States v. United Foods, Inc. | 533 U.S. 405 (2001) | Justia U.S. Supreme Court Center](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/533/405/)

### United States v. Valentine

The defect is almost entirely procedural posture and remedy, not Stone’s Fourth Amendment analysis.

The current brief incorrectly says the Third Circuit “sustained” the stop and a conviction, then says the conviction remains in force. But this case reached the Third Circuit on the Government’s interlocutory appeal from a suppression order. There was no conviction being reviewed. Stone’s substantive position is still that the officers had reasonable suspicion and that the Third Circuit reached the correct Fourth Amendment result.

So you do not need to rewrite the merits rule.

The surgical fix is:

- Correct Section I to say the District Court suppressed the firearm, the Government appealed under the authorized interlocutory route, and the Third Circuit reversed the suppression order and remanded for further criminal proceedings.
- Keep Stone’s controlled disposition as **Affirm**.
- Replace the remedy. Do not say “the conviction remains in force.”

The corrected remedy should essentially be:

> **Stone’s Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** Affirm the judgment of the Third Circuit.
>
> **Stone’s Remedy and Remand Instruction:** The Third Circuit’s reversal of the suppression order remains in force. The criminal case returns to the District Court for further proceedings consistent with that judgment. The Court does not determine guilt, direct conviction, or resolve any issue beyond the Fourth Amendment suppression question presented.

That produces the correct judgment bridge:

> District Court suppressed the firearm → Third Circuit reversed suppression → Stone agrees the stop was constitutional → Supreme Court affirms the Third Circuit → prosecution continues below.

Stone’s existing substantive holding can remain almost word for word: a face-to-face unidentified informant can have reliability beyond a bare anonymous tip because officers can observe demeanor, apparent firsthand basis, immediacy, proximity, corroborated detail, and conduct occurring before seizure. The brief also correctly reserves any categorical firearm exception.

One other cleanup is worthwhile. Standing State actually states two granted questions, including what corroboration or suspect conduct beyond innocent-description matching is necessary. The current brief compresses these into one question. That is not necessarily fatal because the existing reasoning answers both, but the Question Granted field should reproduce the two Standing State questions exactly.

So for Valentine: **fix Section I posture, exact grant wording, and Section II remedy. Leave Stone’s merits reasoning and affirmative disposition alone.**

### Chen v. City of Houston

This is a genuine question-scope error.

Standing State expressly grants only:

1. which population base one-person-one-vote permits or requires; and
2. what constitutional limit applies when equal-total-population districts contain substantial disparities in eligible voters.

The current brief instead adds racial predominance to the granted question, invokes *Hunt*, decides that racial-predominance claim against the challengers, and incorporates it into the proposed holding.

That has to come out.

The source reconstruction itself actually tells us why. Stone’s original cert-stage position was to grant **only the population-base issue** and reserve the record-heavy racial-predominance claim. So Standing State is consistent with Stone’s intended grant.

The fix is therefore not to amend Standing State. Fix the brief.

Stone’s controlled disposition can remain **Affirm**, but it should be carefully confined:

> **Stone’s Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** Affirm the Fifth Circuit’s judgment insofar as challenged by the granted one-person-one-vote and population-base questions.

Then the remedy:

> **Stone’s Remedy and Remand Instruction:** Houston’s use of total population is not unconstitutional on the granted one-person-one-vote theories. No further relief is required on those questions.

The merits should then answer the two granted questions directly:

> **Stone’s Legal Position:** Total population is a constitutionally permissible basis for equalizing local legislative districts because representatives represent residents, not merely persons currently eligible to vote. Equal Protection does not require a jurisdiction that has substantially equalized total population also to equalize eligible-voter population or citizen voting-age population. Substantial disparities in eligible voters per representative therefore do not, standing alone, invalidate districts substantially equal in total population.

Then preserve the necessary limit:

> The Constitution still requires substantial equality under the lawful population base actually used. The holding does not authorize manipulation of the population measure as a device for independently unconstitutional discrimination.

The proposed holding can be exactly the clean rule already developed in your source material:

> **Equal representation may constitutionally be measured by persons represented; disparities in the number of eligible voters do not themselves invalidate districts substantially equal in total population.**

And then the boundary:

> **Boundary or Reserved Question:** The Court does not decide whether citizen voting-age population, eligible-voter population, registered-voter population, or another materially different denominator may constitutionally be used in another case. It does not decide any racial-predominance or racial-gerrymandering claim, which lies outside the simulated grant.

That last sentence is important. Do not say racial predominance “fails.” Do not apply *Hunt*. Do not discuss whether race subordinated traditional criteria. Simply reserve it.

This also preserves your doctrinal sequencing. Your supplied analysis specifically wanted **Chen to leave Easley alone**. Easley does the race-versus-party attribution work. Chen does the denominator work.

## Judgment

I would make these as very narrow corrections.

For **Valentine**, Stone’s actual vote and Fourth Amendment reasoning are already sound. The mistake is saying there was a conviction. Change the posture and remedy only, plus reproduce the exact two-question grant.

For **Chen**, remove racial predominance from every operative Section II field: controlling-law analysis, threshold, legal position, proposed holding, reasoning, and remedy. Keep only a reservation saying that racial-districting questions are outside the grant. Stone still votes to affirm on the denominator questions.

Neither case requires changing Stone’s intended substantive result.

# OCTOBER TERM 2000, CHUNK 8 OF 8

## Docket and Chronology

| Date | Case or matter | Event |
|---|---|---|
| August 6, 2001 | *United States v. Valentine* | Merits decision following resolution of the prior posture-and-remedy blocker |
| September 10, 2001 | *Chen v. City of Houston* | Merits decision following resolution of the prior grant-scope blocker |

Chronology reconciliation: Both matters were previously paused at their scheduled decision dates. The approved revisions resolve the blockers. Neither decision materially changes the entering law, participation, posture, judgment, remedy, or procedural stage of *Appalachian Power Co. v. EPA*, decided September 17, 2001, or *Harris v. Garner*, decided September 24, 2001. Those completed adjudications require no rerun.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Valentine

Argued July 9, 2001 | Decided August 6, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: District Court suppression order reversed and criminal case remanded for further proceedings, 232 F.3d 350 (3d Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/232/350/514839/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; Investigatory stops; Informant reliability

Questions Presented:
1. When does an unidentified person’s face-to-face report that a nearby individual possesses a firearm carry sufficient indicia of reliability to justify a *Terry* stop after *Florida v. J.L.*?
2. What corroboration or suspect conduct, beyond matching an innocent description, is required before officers may stop and frisk? `STANDING_STATE.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone; Stevens as to Question 1; O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion except that Stevens joins Question 1 only |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Reliability of an unidentified face-to-face informant

Holding and operative rule: An unidentified face-to-face report may carry sufficient indicia of reliability when officers can observe the informant, the report claims immediate firsthand knowledge, the described person remains nearby, and prompt corroboration confirms details indicating that the source identified the correct person. A matching innocent description alone remains insufficient, and firearm possession creates no categorical exception to the reliability requirement.

Reasoning: *Florida v. J.L.* requires information supporting the reliability of the accusation, not merely the tipster’s ability to describe a person at a location. This informant personally approached the officers and reported that he had seen the firearm moments earlier. The officers could observe his demeanor, knew his appearance, and understood the asserted basis of knowledge. They then found Valentine approximately 50 to 100 feet away, accompanied as reported and matching the detailed description. Those circumstances supplied source-based reliability absent from the unaccountable telephone accusation in *J.L.*. The report was immediate rather than stale or secondhand, and prompt corroboration reduced the possibility that the source had identified the wrong person. The Court relies on those individualized circumstances, not a generalized characterization of the neighborhood and not an exception for reports involving firearms. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/232/350/514839/)) `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`

##### Question 2: Corroboration and conduct before seizure

Holding and operative rule: No fixed form of predictive corroboration is indispensable; the complete circumstances must contain information beyond innocent identification that bears on source reliability, the asserted illegality, or the suspect’s own conduct. When a person does not submit to a show of police authority, conduct occurring before physical force or submission may be considered in determining whether reasonable suspicion existed at the moment of seizure. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The immediate eyewitness basis, face-to-face encounter, officer observation of the source, proximity, and prompt corroboration supplied reasonable suspicion before the officers commanded Valentine to stop. The District Court also erred by freezing the inquiry at that command. Under *California v. Hodari D.*, a show of authority unaccompanied by submission is not yet a seizure. Valentine did not submit. He questioned the command, charged toward the officer, attempted to push through the officer’s arms, and was seized only when physical restraint followed. That intervening conduct therefore formed part of the circumstances known at the moment of seizure and further strengthened the individualized suspicion. Once the officers reasonably suspected that Valentine was armed, a limited protective frisk was permissible while they investigated. The rule does not make walking away, refusal to answer, neighborhood character, or firearm possession independently sufficient. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/232/350/514839/))

#### Concurrences

Stevens, concurring in the judgment: The immediate face-to-face eyewitness report, the source’s proximity, and prompt corroboration supplied reasonable suspicion before the officers commanded Valentine to stop. He would not rely on conduct following the show of authority because he continues to reject *Hodari D.*’s definition of seizure.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Florida v. J.L.*, 529 U.S. 266 (2000): clarified; an immediate face-to-face eyewitness report may possess source reliability absent from a bare anonymous telephone accusation, while the prohibition on a categorical firearm exception remains unchanged.  
*California v. Hodari D.*, 499 U.S. 621 (1991): followed; conduct occurring after an unheeded show of authority but before physical force or submission may enter the reasonable-suspicion assessment.  
*Illinois v. Wardlow*, 528 U.S. 119 (2000): applied; suspect conduct remains relevant only within the complete individualized circumstances and does not independently replace reliable information connecting the person to suspected crime.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed; its reversal of the suppression order remains in force, and the criminal case shall proceed in the District Court.  
Effect: The firearm may not be suppressed on the Fourth Amendment ground presented. This Court determines neither guilt nor conviction and resolves no issue beyond the suppression question.

### Chen v. City of Houston

Argued August 6, 2001 | Decided September 10, 2001  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for Houston affirmed, 206 F.3d 502 (5th Cir. 2000). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/206/502/597940/))  
Area of Law: Equal Protection; Local apportionment; Population denominator

Questions Presented:
1. Does one person, one vote require local legislative districts to equalize total population, eligible-voter population, or citizen voting-age population, or may the jurisdiction choose among those measures?
2. What constitutional limit applies when equal-total-population districts produce substantial disparities in the number of eligible voters per representative? `STANDING_STATE.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia as to Question 2, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas as to Question 2, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion except that Scalia and Thomas join Question 2 only |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | Scalia | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Permissibility of total-population districting

Holding and operative rule: Total population is a constitutionally permissible basis for equalizing districts of a local general-government body because representatives represent residents, not merely persons presently eligible to vote. Equal Protection does not require a jurisdiction that substantially equalizes total population also to equalize eligible-voter population or citizen voting-age population. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: *Reynolds v. Sims* requires a good-faith effort to construct substantially equal-population districts, and *Avery v. Midland County* applies that requirement to local bodies exercising general governmental power. Total population measures the number of constituents for whom each representative exercises governmental responsibility. Minors, noncitizens, and other residents presently unable to vote remain subject to municipal law, use public services, and may seek assistance from their representatives. Houston therefore acted within the Equal Protection Clause by apportioning its nine single-member council districts through total population. The record showed a maximum total-population deviation of 8.63 percent, and the challengers did not contend that the variation under that measure exceeded the ordinary ten-percent threshold. *Burns v. Richardson* establishes that total population is not the exclusive conceivable measure, but it supplies no rule requiring Houston to replace or supplement a lawful total-population base with citizen voting-age population. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/206/502/597940/))

##### Question 2: Eligible-voter disparities within equal-total-population districts

Holding and operative rule: Substantial disparities in eligible voters per representative do not, standing alone, invalidate districts substantially equal in total population. The jurisdiction must substantially equalize the lawful population base it actually uses, and that choice does not insulate independently unconstitutional discrimination or manipulation from review.

Reasoning: Different population measures can diverge when persons ineligible to vote are distributed unevenly. Requiring simultaneous equality in total population, eligible voters, and citizen voting-age population would impose several potentially incompatible constitutional denominators. One-person-one-vote instead requires substantial equality under a constitutionally permissible population base. Because total population is such a base, the variation in Houston’s citizen voting-age population does not independently establish vote dilution or require the City to redraw its districts. The selected measure must be applied consistently and in good faith; a jurisdiction may not manipulate its denominator or boundaries as a device for an independently unconstitutional classification. That limitation does not place the separate racial-predominance claim within this grant. The Court therefore affirms only on the population-base and eligible-voter-disparity questions and expresses no view on the racial-districting allegations litigated below. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/206/502/597940/))

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurring in the judgment: Total population is permissible because the existing one-person-one-vote cases leave substantial denominator choice to the political branches, not because the Constitution adopts representational equality as the preferred theory of political representation. They would reserve both the constitutionality of other denominators and any claim that total population enjoys constitutional preference.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Reynolds v. Sims*, 377 U.S. 533 (1964): clarified; a local jurisdiction may satisfy population equality by substantially equalizing total resident population.  
*Avery v. Midland County*, 390 U.S. 474 (1968): applied; a local body exercising general governmental authority remains subject to one-person-one-vote.  
*Burns v. Richardson*, 384 U.S. 73 (1966): followed and clarified; denominator choice remains context dependent, but use of total population does not become unconstitutional merely because eligible voters are unevenly distributed, and the validity of alternative bases remains open.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it sustains Houston’s plan against the granted population-base and eligible-voter-disparity challenges.  
Effect: Houston may retain the challenged plan on the one-person-one-vote theories adjudicated here. The Court does not decide whether citizen voting-age population, eligible-voter population, registered-voter population, or another materially different denominator may constitutionally be used in another case, and it does not decide any racial-predominance or racial-gerrymandering claim.
